Three thresholds give \((\text{FPR}, \text{TPR})\) points \((0, 0)\), \((0.1, 0.6)\), \((0.3, 0.8)\), \((1, 1)\). By the trapezoid rule (Section 13.5): \(0.1\cdot\tfrac{0+0.6}{2} + 0.2\cdot\tfrac{0.6+0.8}{2} + 0.7\cdot\tfrac{0.8+1}{2} = 0.03 + 0.14 + 0.63 = 0.80\). Pair-counting check: positives scored \([0.9, 0.6]\), negatives \([0.7, 0.2]\); of the 4 pairs, the positive wins in 3 (\(0.9>0.7\), \(0.9>0.2\), \(0.6>0.2\)), so AUC \(= 3/4 = 0.75\).

6.14 Resampling: cross-validation and the bootstrap

Getting a trustworthy score from limited data

A single train/test split can be lucky or unlucky. k-fold cross-validation splits the data into \(k\) equal parts, trains on \(k-1\) of them and tests on the remaining one, rotating until every part has been the test set; the average of the \(k\) scores is a steadier estimate, and their spread tells you how uncertain it is. (For time series, never shuffle: always train on the past and test on the future, Section 12.) The bootstrap instead builds new datasets of the same size by drawing examples with replacement, and recomputes the quantity of interest on each, to see how much it wobbles. The jackknife does the same by leaving out one example at a time.

\[ \text{CV score} = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\text{score}_j \qquad SE = \frac{s}{\sqrt{k}} \qquad P(\text{example never drawn}) = \left(1 - \frac{1}{m}\right)^{m} \xrightarrow{m\to\infty} e^{-1} \approx 0.368 \]
  • \(s\): the standard deviation of the \(k\) fold scores
  • \(m\): number of examples in the dataset
Worked example

Five fold errors \([0.20, 0.25, 0.15, 0.22, 0.18]\) average to \(0.20\), with standard deviation \(0.038\) and standard error \(0.038/\sqrt{5} = 0.017\): report "20% \(\pm\) 1.7%". Bootstrap fact: with \(m = 10\) examples, each is left out of a resample with probability \(0.9^{10} = 0.349\); with \(m = 100\) it is \(0.366\); in the limit \(e^{-1} = 0.368\). So each bootstrap sample contains about 63.2% distinct examples (\(1 - 0.368\)), and the remaining ~36.8% are free "out-of-bag" test data (next section).

6.15 Bagging, random forests and out-of-bag error

A jury of slightly different models

Bagging (bootstrap aggregating) trains many models, each on a different bootstrap resample, and averages their predictions (or takes a majority vote). Averaging cancels random errors, so it cuts variance (Section 6.9) without raising bias. A random forest is bagging of decision trees with one extra trick: at each split only a random subset of the features (often \(\sqrt{d}\)) is considered, which makes the trees less alike and the average better. Because each tree never saw about 36.8% of the data, every example has "out-of-bag" trees that never trained on it; letting only those trees vote on it gives a free test-set estimate with no separate validation set needed.

\[ \text{Var}\big(\bar{X}\big) = \rho\,\sigma^2 + \frac{1-\rho}{M}\,\sigma^2 \qquad \text{Err}_{\text{OOB}} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{I}\big(\hat{y}_i^{\text{OOB}} \ne y_i\big) \]
  • \(M\): number of models
  • \(\rho\): the correlation between the models’ errors (0 = independent, 1 = identical)
  • \(\sigma^2\): the variance of a single model
  • \(\hat y_i^{\text{OOB}}\): the majority vote of the trees that did not train on example \(i\)
Worked example

With single-model variance \(\sigma^2 = 1\) and correlation \(\rho = 0.3\): one model has variance 1; \(M = 10\) models have \(0.3 + 0.7/10 = 0.37\); \(M = 100\) have \(0.307\); infinitely many have \(0.3\), a floor set by the correlation. If the models were fully independent (\(\rho = 0\)), 10 of them would reach \(0.1\). That is why random forests decorrelate the trees. OOB vote: an example was out-of-bag for trees 1, 3 and 5, whose votes are A, A, B, so the OOB prediction is A. With \(d = 100\) features, each split looks at only \(\sqrt{100} = 10\).

6.16 SVM kernels and the soft margin

Curved boundaries without ever leaving the original space

A linear SVM (Section 6.6) can only draw straight boundaries. The kernel trick: imagine mapping each point into a much higher-dimensional space where a straight boundary does work, but never compute that mapping, because the SVM only ever needs dot products between points and a kernel function \(k(x,z)\) returns the dot product of the mapped points directly. Real data is rarely perfectly separable, so the soft margin allows some points to cross the margin by an amount \(\xi\), at a cost controlled by \(C\): large \(C\) means "do not tolerate mistakes", small \(C\) means "prefer a wider, simpler margin".

\[ k_{\text{poly}}(x,z) = (x\cdot z + 1)^2 \qquad k_{\text{RBF}}(x,z) = \exp\!\big(-\gamma\|x - z\|^2\big) \qquad \min_{w,b,\xi}\ \tfrac12\|w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i \]
  • \(\gamma\): RBF width: larger means a more local, wigglier boundary
  • \(\xi_i\): how far point \(i\) violates the margin (0 if it does not)
Worked example

For \(x = [1, 2]\) and \(z = [3, 1]\): \(x\cdot z = 5\), so the polynomial kernel gives \((5 + 1)^2 = 36\). Computing the same thing the long way, with the explicit 6-number feature map \(\phi(x) = [1,\ \sqrt2x_1,\ \sqrt2x_2,\ x_1^2,\ \sqrt2x_1x_2,\ x_2^2]\), also gives \(\phi(x)\cdot\phi(z) = 1 + 6 + 4 + 9 + 12 + 4 = 36\), but the kernel needed one dot product instead of building 6-dimensional vectors. The RBF kernel: \(\|x - z\|^2 = 4 + 1 = 5\), so with \(\gamma = 0.5\), \(k = e^{-2.5} = 0.082\) (far-apart points are almost unrelated).

6.17 Elastic Net and why Lasso gives exact zeros

Soft-thresholding

Section 5.12 introduced L2 (shrinks smoothly) and L1 (can reach exactly zero). Elastic Net uses both at once, which helps when several features are correlated (Lasso alone tends to pick one arbitrarily). The reason L1 produces exact zeros is visible in its update for a single coefficient: the soft-thresholding operator pulls every coefficient toward zero by \(\lambda\) and sets it to zero if it would cross.

\[ \mathcal{L} = \text{loss} + \lambda_1\sum|w_j| + \lambda_2\sum w_j^2 \qquad S(z,\lambda) = \text{sign}(z)\,\max\big(|z| - \lambda,\ 0\big) \]
  • \(z\): the unpenalized best value for the coefficient
  • \(\lambda\): the L1 strength
Worked example

With \(\lambda = 1\): a coefficient with \(z = 3\) becomes \(S = 3 - 1 = 2\) (shrunk but kept); one with \(z = 0.5\) becomes \(\max(0.5 - 1, 0) = 0\) (removed entirely). Features whose evidence is smaller than \(\lambda\) are switched off, which is automatic feature selection.

6.18 Gradient boosting in more detail (the XGBoost idea)

Second-order boosting

Gradient boosting (Section 6.11) fits each new tree to the gradient of the loss. XGBoost-style boosting also uses the second derivative (curvature) \(h_i\), the same extra information that makes Newton’s method (Section 13.1) faster than plain gradient descent. With gradients \(g_i\) and curvatures \(h_i\) of the examples that fall in a leaf, the best leaf value and the gain from splitting a node into left (L) and right (R) children are:

\[ w^{*} = -\frac{G}{H + \lambda} \qquad \text{Gain} = \tfrac12\left[\frac{G_L^2}{H_L + \lambda} + \frac{G_R^2}{H_R + \lambda} - \frac{(G_L + G_R)^2}{H_L + H_R + \lambda}\right] - \gamma \]
  • \(G = \sum g_i,\ H = \sum h_i\): sums over the examples in the leaf
  • \(\lambda\): an L2 penalty on leaf values
  • \(\gamma\): a penalty for adding a leaf (prunes useless splits)
Worked example

For squared error, \(g_i = \hat y_i - y_i\) and \(h_i = 1\). A leaf with residuals \(g = [-2, -1, -3]\) has \(G = -6\), \(H = 3\); with \(\lambda = 1\) the best leaf value is \(w^{*} = 6/(3+1) = 1.5\). A split with \(G_L = -4, H_L = 2\) and \(G_R = 2, H_R = 2\) has gain \(\tfrac12[16/3 + 4/3 - 4/5] = \tfrac12(5.333 + 1.333 - 0.8) = 2.933\). Only splits with positive gain after subtracting \(\gamma\) are kept.

In your vault

A01 to A03, B01 to B09, C06, D01 to D04 and I01 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives cover Sections 2.3, 4.8 to 4.10, 5.16 to 5.17 and 6.12 to 6.18 in full depth.

Part 7. Deep learning architectures

Deep learning takes the neuron and forward/backward pass from Part 5 and arranges them in specialized wiring patterns, each suited to a different kind of data.

7.1 CNNs: networks that understand images

\[ S(i,j) = \sum_{m}\sum_{n} I(i+m,j+n)\cdot K(m,n) \qquad\qquad \text{Output size} = \left\lfloor\frac{W-F+2P}{S}\right\rfloor+1 \]

Instead of connecting every pixel to every neuron (which would need an enormous number of weights), a CNN slides a small grid of learned weights (a kernel or filter) across the image, computing a dot-product (Section 2.1.2) at each position. The same small filter is reused everywhere, which is what lets a CNN recognize an edge or a shape no matter where it appears in the image.

Worked example: applying a 2×2 kernel

Image patch \(I=\begin{bmatrix}1&2&3\\4&5&6\\7&8&9\end{bmatrix}\), kernel \(K=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{bmatrix}\) (a simple edge-style detector: top-left minus bottom-right). Sliding it over each 2×2 window: top-left window \([1,2;4,5]\to 1\cdot1+5\cdot(-1)=-4\); top-right \([2,3;5,6]\to 2-6=-4\); bottom-left \([4,5;7,8]\to4-8=-4\); bottom-right \([5,6;8,9]\to5-9=-4\). Every position gives \(-4\) here because the pixel values increase steadily — the kernel is measuring a rate of change, and that rate is constant across this smooth patch. On a real photo, this same kernel would light up wherever brightness jumps sharply, which is exactly what an edge looks like.

Output size formula: with input width \(W=5\), filter size \(F=3\), padding \(P=0\), stride \(S=1\): output \(=\lfloor(5-3+0)/1\rfloor+1=3\). Padding adds a border of zeros so the output doesn't shrink every layer; stride controls how far the kernel jumps each step.

7.2 RNNs: networks with memory, for sequences

\[ h_t = \tanh(W_{hh}h_{t-1}+W_{xh}x_t+b_h) \qquad\qquad \hat y_t = \text{softmax}(W_{yh}h_t+b_y) \]

A Recurrent Neural Network processes a sequence (like a sentence) one element at a time, keeping a hidden state \(h_t\) that acts as a compressed memory of everything seen so far. At every time step, the new hidden state mixes the previous memory with the new input, through a tanh (Section 5.2.3). The weakness: gradients have to flow backward through every single time step (Section 3.5's chain rule applied dozens or hundreds of times in a row), which tends to either vanish to zero or explode to infinity over long sequences.

7.3 LSTM/GRU: RNNs with gates that control what to remember

\[ f_t=\sigma(W_f[h_{t-1},x_t]+b_f)\quad i_t=\sigma(W_i[h_{t-1},x_t]+b_i)\quad \tilde C_t=\tanh(W_C[h_{t-1},x_t]+b_C) \] \[ C_t = f_t \odot C_{t-1} + i_t \odot \tilde C_t \qquad\qquad h_t = o_t \odot \tanh(C_t) \]

An LSTM fixes the RNN's memory problem by adding a separate "cell state" \(C_t\) plus three sigmoid gates (each outputs a number between 0 and 1, acting like a dial): the forget gate \(f_t\) decides how much old memory to keep, the input gate \(i_t\) decides how much new information to add, and (not shown above) the output gate \(o_t\) decides how much of the memory to reveal as the hidden state. The \(\odot\) symbol is element-wise multiplication (Part 0.3) — each gate value multiplies its corresponding piece of the memory vector, dial by dial.

7.4 Autoencoders and VAEs: compressing then reconstructing data

An autoencoder squeezes an input down to a small "bottleneck" vector (the encoder), then tries to rebuild the original from just that bottleneck (the decoder) — forcing the network to learn the most essential, compressed representation of the data.

A Variational Autoencoder (VAE) makes the bottleneck a probability distribution instead of a fixed point, using the reparameterization trick:

\[ z = \mu + \sigma \odot \epsilon,\quad \epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1) \qquad\qquad D_{KL} = -\frac12\sum_j\left(1+\log\sigma_j^2-\mu_j^2-\sigma_j^2\right) \]

The trick samples randomness (\(\epsilon\)) separately from the learned parameters (\(\mu,\sigma\)) so gradients can still flow through \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) via the chain rule, even though sampling itself is random and has no derivative. The KL term (from Section 4.6) is a penalty that pushes the learned distribution to stay close to a simple standard bell curve \(\mathcal N(0,1)\), which keeps the compressed space smooth and well-organized.

Worked example

One latent dimension with \(\mu=1\), \(\sigma=1\) (so \(\sigma^2=1\), \(\log\sigma^2=0\)): \(D_{KL} = -\frac12(1+0-1-1) = 0.5\). If instead \(\mu=0,\sigma=1\) (matching the standard bell curve exactly), \(D_{KL}=-\frac12(1+0-0-1)=0\) — no penalty, because there's nothing to correct.

7.5 GANs: two networks competing against each other

\[ \min_G\max_D\ \mathbb{E}_{x\sim p_{\text{data}}}[\log D(x)] + \mathbb{E}_{z\sim p_z}[\log(1-D(G(z)))] \]

A Generator (\(G\)) tries to create fake data realistic enough to fool a Discriminator (\(D\)), while \(D\) tries to correctly tell real data from \(G\)'s fakes. \(D\) wants to maximize this expression (get better at spotting fakes); \(G\) wants to minimize it (get better at fooling \(D\)) — a tug-of-war that, at its best, drives \(G\) to produce data indistinguishable from the real thing.

7.6 Diffusion models: learning to reverse noise, step by step

\[ x_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon,\quad \epsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,1) \qquad\qquad \mathcal L = \mathbb E\left[\|\epsilon-\epsilon_\theta(x_t,t)\|^2\right] \]

Forward process: gradually blend a real image \(x_0\) with pure random noise \(\epsilon\), over many steps, until nothing recognizable remains. \(\bar\alpha_t\) is a schedule that starts near 1 (mostly image) and decays toward 0 (mostly noise) as \(t\) increases. Training teaches a network \(\epsilon_\theta\) to predict exactly which noise was added, at any step \(t\) — a mean-squared-error task (Section 5.4.1) — so that at generation time, starting from pure noise, the network can be run in reverse, repeatedly subtracting its predicted noise, to conjure a brand-new image out of static.

Worked example

Suppose \(\bar\alpha_t = 0.64\), original pixel value \(x_0=1\), and sampled noise \(\epsilon=0.5\): \(x_t = \sqrt{0.64}(1) + \sqrt{1-0.64}(0.5) = 0.8(1)+0.6(0.5) = 0.8+0.3=1.1\) — a blend that's 80% "signal strength" from the original and 60% "noise strength" from the random draw (they don't need to sum to 1 because they're weighting variance, not a simple average).

7.7 GNNs: networks that operate on graphs

\[ h_v^{(k)} = \text{UPDATE}\Big(h_v^{(k-1)},\ \text{AGGREGATE}\big(\{h_u^{(k-1)} : u\in\mathcal N(v)\}\big)\Big) \]

Not all data is a grid (image) or a sequence (text) — some is a graph of connected nodes (a social network, a molecule). A Graph Neural Network updates every node's representation by aggregating (e.g. averaging or summing) information from its immediate neighbors \(\mathcal N(v)\), then combines that with the node's own previous state. Stack \(k\) layers of this and a node's final representation reflects information gathered from up to \(k\) hops away in the graph.

In your vault

Transformer/Transformer/Code/Deep_Dives/01–08 implement MLP, CNN, RNN, LSTM/GRU, VAE, GAN, Diffusion, and GNN from scratch; Reports/Deep_Dives/01–08.md explain each in prose; Reports/07_All_Neural_Network_Types.md gives a side-by-side comparison table of every architecture family.

Part 8. Transformers and LLMs

The architecture behind every modern large language model. It reuses almost everything from Parts 2, 3, and 5 — matrix multiplication, softmax, the chain rule, cross-entropy — arranged around one new idea: attention.

8.1 Turning words into numbers: embeddings and positional encoding

A sentence like "The cat sat" first becomes a matrix \(X\) of shape \([\text{sequence length}, \text{embedding dimension}]\) — each word is looked up in a big table and replaced by a list of numbers (a vector, Section 2.1) called an embedding. For example, the word "apple" might become \([0.12,\,-0.45,\,0.80,\dots]\), maybe 4096 numbers long. Nobody writes these numbers by hand: they start random and are learned during training (Section 5.5), so that words with similar meanings end up with similar vectors (you can check this with the cosine similarity from Section 2.1.3). Because attention (Section 8.3) treats the sequence as an unordered set of vectors with no inherent notion of order, a positional encoding is added so the model can tell "cat sat" apart from "sat cat":

\[ PE_{(pos, 2i)} = \sin\left(\frac{pos}{10000^{2i/d}}\right) \qquad\qquad PE_{(pos, 2i+1)} = \cos\left(\frac{pos}{10000^{2i/d}}\right) \]

Every position gets a unique pattern of sine and cosine waves at different frequencies, added directly onto the word embedding. At position 1 with a fast-varying frequency: \(\sin(1)=0.841\), \(\cos(1)=0.540\); at the same position with a slow-varying frequency (frequency scaled by 1/100): \(\sin(0.01)=0.0100\), \(\cos(0.01)=0.99995\) — mixing many frequencies like this gives every position a distinct, learnable "fingerprint".

8.2 Q, K, V: turning embeddings into Queries, Keys, and Values

\[ Q = XW_Q \qquad K = XW_K \qquad V = XW_V \]

Three separate learned weight matrices project every word's embedding into three new vectors: \(Q\) ("what am I looking for?"), \(K\) ("what do I contain, that others might look for?"), and \(V\) ("what is my actual content, to be passed along if I'm relevant?"). This is just three more matrix multiplications, exactly like Section 5.6.

8.3 Scaled Dot-Product Attention: the equation that makes modern AI work

\[ \text{Attention}(Q,K,V) = \text{softmax}\left(\frac{QK^T}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right)V \]
  1. \(QK^T\): dot-product (Section 2.1.2) every word's Query against every word's Key, producing a square \([\text{seq\_len}, \text{seq\_len}]\) matrix of raw relevance scores — high when a Query and a Key point in a similar direction.
  2. \(/\sqrt{d_k}\): divide by the square root of the Key dimension. With large embedding dimensions, raw dot products get huge, which pushes softmax (Section 5.2.4) into a region where its gradient is nearly flat (vanishing gradients again, as in Section 5.2.1) — this scaling keeps the variance stable.
  3. \(\text{softmax}(\cdot)\): turns each row of scores into a probability distribution that sums to 1 — "how much attention should this word pay to every other word".
  4. \(\cdots V\): use those probabilities to take a weighted blend of every word's Value vector — each word's new representation becomes a mixture of the words it "attended to" most.
Worked example: attention on 2 tiny words

\(Q=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{bmatrix}\), \(K=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{bmatrix}\), \(V=\begin{bmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{bmatrix}\), \(d_k=2\) so \(\sqrt{d_k}=1.414\).

\[ QK^T = \begin{bmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{bmatrix} \quad\Rightarrow\quad \frac{QK^T}{\sqrt{2}} = \begin{bmatrix}0.707&0.707\\0&0.707\end{bmatrix} \]

Softmax each row: row 1 (two equal scores) \(\to[0.5,\ 0.5]\); row 2 \(\to[0.330,\ 0.670]\).

\[ \text{Attention} = \begin{bmatrix}0.5&0.5\\0.330&0.670\end{bmatrix}\times V = \begin{bmatrix}2.0&3.0\\2.339&3.339\end{bmatrix} \]

Word 1's new representation is an exact 50/50 blend of both Values (\(0.5\times[1,2]+0.5\times[3,4]=[2,3]\)); word 2's is a 33/67 blend, weighted more toward word 2's own value.

8.4 Multi-head attention: doing this several times in parallel

Instead of one attention calculation, the model runs several smaller ones ("heads") side by side, each with its own \(W_Q,W_K,W_V\), then concatenates the results. Each head can specialize — one might learn to track grammatical subject-verb relationships, another might track which pronoun refers to which noun — and their outputs are combined with one more learned weight matrix.

8.5 LayerNorm and residual connections in the Transformer

Every attention block and every feedforward block is wrapped as \(x + \text{Sublayer}(\text{LayerNorm}(x))\): the block's output is added back to its own input (a "residual" or "skip" connection) rather than replacing it outright. This gives the gradient a direct, undiminished path backward through the whole network during backpropagation (Section 3.5), which is essential for training networks dozens of layers deep without gradients vanishing. LayerNorm itself is exactly the formula from Section 5.8.

8.6 The output: softmax and cross-entropy over the vocabulary

\[ \mathcal L = -\sum_{c=1}^{\text{Vocab}} y_c\log(\hat y_c) = -\log(\hat y_{\text{correct}}) \]

The final layer outputs one raw score ("logit") per possible next word — over a vocabulary that can be 50,000+ words large — then softmax (Section 5.2.4) turns those into probabilities. Since the true label \(y_c\) is 1 for exactly the correct next word and 0 for every other word, the general cross-entropy formula (Section 4.6) collapses to just \(-\log\) of whatever probability the model assigned to the actual correct word.

Worked example: reusing the softmax scores from Section 5.2.4

Predicted distribution \([0.659, 0.242, 0.099]\), and suppose the correct next word was class 0 (the model's top pick, probability 0.659): loss \(=-\ln(0.659)=0.417\). If instead the correct word had been the unlikely class 2 (probability 0.099): loss \(=-\ln(0.099)=2.313\) — much larger, correctly punishing the model for having assigned that word a low probability.

Perplexity

\(\text{Perplexity} = e^{\mathcal L}\) — the standard way LLM quality is reported. A loss of 2.0 gives perplexity \(e^{2.0}=7.39\), loosely interpretable as "the model was, on average, as uncertain as if it were guessing uniformly among about 7.4 words." Lower perplexity is better.

8.7 Temperature: controlling how "confident" generation is

\[ \text{softmax}(z/T)_i = \frac{e^{z_i/T}}{\sum_j e^{z_j/T}} \]

Dividing the logits by a temperature \(T\) before softmax controls how sharp or flat the output distribution is. \(T=1\) is unmodified. \(T \lt 1\) sharpens it (more confident, less random); \(T>1\) flattens it (more random, more creative).

Worked example

Same logits \([2.0,1.0,0.1]\) as before, but with \(T=0.5\) (so effectively doubling each logit): softmax becomes \([0.864,\ 0.117,\ 0.019]\) — compare to \([0.659,0.242,0.099]\) at \(T=1\). Lower temperature made the model's top choice noticeably more dominant.

8.8 Mixture of Experts (MoE): only using part of the network per token

\[ G(x) = \text{softmax}(W_g\cdot x) \qquad\qquad y = \sum_{i=1}^N G_{\text{sparse}}(x)_i \cdot E_i(x) \]

Instead of one giant feedforward block per layer, an MoE layer has many smaller "expert" sub-networks \(E_i\), plus a small gating network \(G\) that, for each token, decides which few experts to route it to (via softmax, keeping only the top few scores — "KeepTopK"). This lets the total model have a huge number of parameters while only activating a small fraction of them for any single token, keeping inference fast.

8.9 How big is an LLM, actually?

A rough parameter count for one Transformer layer with embedding dimension \(d\) is about \(12d^2\) (from the Q/K/V/output projections plus the feedforward block). For \(d=4096\) across 32 layers: \(12 \times 32 \times 4096^2 \approx 6.44\) billion parameters — in the same ballpark as real mid-sized open LLMs, using nothing but the matrix-shape reasoning from Part 2.

LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) fine-tunes a model cheaply by learning a small correction \(\Delta W = BA\) instead of updating the full weight matrix \(W\): \(Y = XW + XBA\). A full \(4096\times4096\) weight matrix has \(16{,}777{,}216\) parameters; a rank-8 LoRA update (\(B\) is \(4096\times8\), \(A\) is \(8\times4096\)) has only \(8\times(4096+4096)=65{,}536\) — about 256× fewer trainable numbers, while the frozen original \(W\) stays untouched.

8.10 RoPE: a newer way to encode position (a preview)

\[ q_m = q\,e^{im\theta} \]

Rotary Position Embedding encodes a token's position by literally rotating its Query/Key vectors by an angle proportional to their position \(m\), using complex-number rotation. The elegant payoff: the dot product between a rotated Query at position \(m\) and a rotated Key at position \(n\) ends up depending only on their relative distance \(m-n\), not their absolute positions — letting the model generalize to sequence lengths it never saw during training.

In your vault

Transformer/Transformer/Code/03_Self_Attention.py and 04_Transformer_Architecture.py implement all of Sections 8.1–8.6 from scratch; 05_MoE_Architecture.py and Deep_Dives/14_Transformer_MoE_Sparse.py implement Section 8.8; Reports/Deep_Dives/30_Positional_Encodings_and_RoPE.md and 38_Quantization_and_LoRA.md go deeper into Sections 8.10 and 8.9; Transformer_Cheat_Sheet.md and math_mastery_transformer.html are dedicated, denser references on exactly this part.

8.11 Tokenization: how text becomes numbers

Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE)

A neural network only understands numbers, so text is first chopped into tokens (pieces of words) and each token gets an integer ID. Whole words would need a gigantic vocabulary and single letters would make sequences far too long, so BPE finds a middle path: start with single characters, then repeatedly merge the most frequent neighbouring pair into a new token. Each merge adds one token to the vocabulary.

\[ \text{Embedding table size} = V \times d \]
  • \(V\): vocabulary size (number of distinct tokens)
  • \(d\): length of each token's embedding vector (Section 8.1)
Worked example

Take the string aaabdaaabac (11 symbols). The pair aa occurs 4 times, the most common, so merge it into a new symbol Z: ZabdZabac. Now Za and ab are tied at two occurrences each (ties can be broken any way; we take the leftmost, Za) and merge Za into Y: YbdYbac. Finally Yb occurs twice, so merge it into X: XdXac. Three merges shrank 11 symbols to 5. For a real model with \(V = 50{,}000\) and \(d = 4096\), the embedding table alone holds \(50{,}000 \times 4096 = 204{,}800{,}000\) numbers.

Why it matters

Tokenization decides what the model can "see". A rule of thumb for English is that one token is about three-quarters of a word, so a "4096-token context" is roughly 3000 words.

8.12 Scaling laws: how loss falls as models grow

Power laws and compute-optimal training

Researchers found that a language model's loss falls as a smooth power law when you add parameters, data or compute. Two rules of thumb follow. First, the total training compute is about six FLOPs (basic arithmetic operations) per parameter per token: about 2 for the forward pass and about 4 for the backward pass. Second, for a fixed compute budget, the best results come from growing model size and data together (the "Chinchilla" finding).

\[ L(N) \approx \left(\frac{N_c}{N}\right)^{\alpha} \qquad C \approx 6\,N\,D \qquad D \approx 20\,N \qquad N \propto C^{0.5},\ D \propto C^{0.5} \]
  • \(L\): the loss (lower is better)
  • \(N\): number of parameters
  • \(D\): number of training tokens
  • \(C\): training compute in FLOPs
  • \(\alpha\): a small positive exponent (values around 0.076 were reported in early scaling-law work; the exact value depends on the setup)
  • \(N_c\): a constant that depends on the data and architecture
Worked example

With \(\alpha = 0.076\), doubling the parameters multiplies the loss term by \(2^{-0.076} = 0.949\), i.e. about 5% lower. That is a small gain per doubling, which is why progress needs huge increases. For a 7-billion-parameter model trained on 140 billion tokens (the 20-to-1 ratio), \(C = 6 \times 7\times10^{9} \times 1.4\times10^{11} = 5.88\times10^{21}\) FLOPs.

8.13 Quantization: storing weights with fewer bits

Symmetric INT8 quantization

Each weight is normally stored in 16 bits. Quantization squeezes it into 8 or even 4 bits by mapping the range of weights onto a small set of integers. You lose a little precision but save a lot of memory and speed.

\[ s = \frac{\max|w|}{127} \qquad q = \text{round}\!\left(\frac{w}{s}\right) \qquad \hat{w} = q \cdot s \]
  • \(s\): the scale: how much real value one integer step is worth
  • \(q\): the stored integer, between -127 and 127
  • \(\hat{w}\): the reconstructed (dequantized) weight used in the calculation
Worked example

Suppose the largest weight has size 2.54, so \(s = 2.54/127 = 0.02\). A weight \(w = 0.505\) becomes \(q = \text{round}(25.25) = 25\) and is recovered as \(\hat{w} = 25 \times 0.02 = 0.50\), an error of only 0.005. Memory for a 7-billion-parameter model: 16-bit = \(7\times10^9 \times 2\) bytes = 14 GB; 8-bit = 7 GB; 4-bit = 3.5 GB.

Why it matters

QLoRA combines this with LoRA (Section 8.9): keep the big model frozen in 4 bits and train only the small LoRA matrices, so a large model can be fine-tuned on a single GPU.

8.14 Fine-tuning: SFT and DPO

Supervised fine-tuning (SFT)

SFT continues training a pretrained model on example conversations. It is the same next-word cross-entropy loss as Section 8.6, but the loss is counted only on the answer tokens. The prompt tokens are "masked" so the model is not trained to write the question.

\[ \mathcal{L}_{\text{SFT}} = -\sum_{t \in \text{answer}} \log p_\theta\left(y_t \mid y_{\lt t},\ \text{prompt}\right) \]
  • \(y_t\): the correct \(t\)-th answer token
  • \(y_{\lt t}\): all the tokens before it
  • \(p_\theta\): the model's predicted probability

Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)

RLHF (Section 9.5) needs a separate reward model and a reinforcement-learning loop. DPO reaches a similar goal with one simple loss on pairs of a chosen (preferred) and a rejected answer. It pushes up the chosen answer's probability and pushes down the rejected one's, relative to a frozen reference copy of the model.

\[ \mathcal{L}_{\text{DPO}} = -\log\sigma\Big(\beta\big[\underbrace{\log\tfrac{\pi_\theta(y_w)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_w)}}_{\text{chosen}} - \underbrace{\log\tfrac{\pi_\theta(y_l)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_l)}}_{\text{rejected}}\big]\Big) \]
  • \(y_w, y_l\): the winning (chosen) and losing (rejected) answers
  • \(\pi_\theta\): the model being trained
  • \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\): the frozen starting model
  • \(\beta\): a strength knob, e.g. 0.1
  • \(\sigma\): the sigmoid from Section 5.2.1
Worked example

With \(\beta = 0.1\), suppose the chosen answer's log-ratio is \(+2\) and the rejected one's is \(-1\). The bracket is \(2 - (-1) = 3\), so the sigmoid input is \(0.1 \times 3 = 0.3\), \(\sigma(0.3) = 0.574\), and the loss is \(-\ln(0.574) = 0.554\). As the model separates chosen from rejected more strongly, the loss shrinks toward 0. This is the same \(-\log\sigma(\text{gap})\) shape as the Bradley-Terry loss in Section 9.5.

8.15 State-space models and Mamba: memory instead of all-pairs attention

A running memory state

Attention compares every word with every other word, so its cost grows with the square of the text length (\(\mathcal{O}(n^2)\)). A state-space model (SSM) instead carries a small memory \(h\), updated once per word, so cost grows only linearly (\(\mathcal{O}(n)\)). Mamba makes the update depend on the current input (it is "selective"), so the model can choose what to remember and what to forget.

\[ h_k = \bar{A}\,h_{k-1} + \bar{B}\,x_k \qquad y_k = C\,h_k \qquad \bar{A} = \exp(\Delta A) \]
  • \(h_k\): the memory after reading word \(k\)
  • \(x_k\): the current input
  • \(\bar{A}\): how much of the old memory survives (between 0 and 1 here)
  • \(\bar{B}\): how much of the new input is written into memory
  • \(C\): how memory is read out into an output \(y_k\)
  • \(\Delta\): a step size that Mamba computes from the input: small means remember, large means forget
Worked example

Use single numbers \(\bar{A} = 0.5\), \(\bar{B} = 1\), \(C = 2\) and inputs \([1, 0, 0]\). Then \(h = 1,\ 0.5,\ 0.25\) and \(y = 2,\ 1,\ 0.5\): the memory of the first input halves every step. About \(\Delta\): with \(A = -1\) and \(\Delta = 0.1\), \(\bar{A} = e^{-0.1} = 0.905\), so 90.5% of memory is kept each step.

8.16 Linear attention, RWKV, xLSTM and Titans

Linear attention: rearranging the multiplication

Standard attention computes \((QK^{T})V\), forming the big \(n \times n\) matrix first. Because matrix multiplication can be regrouped, \(Q(K^{T}V)\) gives the same kind of result while building only a small summary \(K^{T}V\) that can be updated one word at a time. Softmax breaks this regrouping, so linear attention replaces it with a simpler feature map \(\phi\).

\[ S_t = S_{t-1} + \phi(k_t)\,v_t^{T} \qquad z_t = z_{t-1} + \phi(k_t) \qquad \text{out}_t = \frac{\phi(q_t)^{T}S_t}{\phi(q_t)^{T}z_t} \]
  • \(S_t\): the running summary of all key-value pairs so far
  • \(z_t\): the running total used to normalize the weights
  • \(\phi\): a simple positive function applied to queries and keys
Worked example

With single numbers and \(\phi(x) = x\): keys \(1, 2\) with values \(3, 4\) give \(S = 1\cdot3 + 2\cdot4 = 11\) and \(z = 1 + 2 = 3\). For query \(q = 1\): out \(= (1\cdot11)/(1\cdot3) = 3.667\), which is exactly a weighted average of the values with weights \(1/3\) and \(2/3\).

RWKV, xLSTM and Titans in one paragraph each

RWKV uses the same running-summary idea but with an exponential time decay \(w\), so recent words matter more than old ones: \(a_t = w\,a_{t-1} + k_t v_t\). With \(w = 0.5\) and contributions \([6, 2]\), \(a_1 = 6\) and \(a_2 = 0.5 \times 6 + 2 = 5\). xLSTM modernizes the LSTM (Section 7.3) with exponential gates and a full matrix memory instead of a single vector. Titans adds a long-term memory network that keeps learning while it reads: when the input is surprising (the memory predicted it badly), it takes a gradient-descent step (Section 3.2) at test time, \(M_t = M_{t-1} - \eta\,\nabla\ell\). Simplified illustration: memory value \(m = 0\), target 1, loss \(\tfrac{1}{2}(m-1)^2\), gradient \(m - 1 = -1\), \(\eta = 0.5\): the update gives \(m = 0 + 0.5 = 0.5\).

8.17 FlashAttention: the same answer with far less memory traffic

Online softmax

Normal attention builds the full \(n \times n\) score matrix and stores it in slow GPU memory. For \(n = 4096\) that is \(16{,}777{,}216\) numbers, about 33.6 MB in 16-bit format, per head, per layer. FlashAttention processes the scores in small tiles kept in fast on-chip memory and never stores the big matrix. The trick is the online softmax: keep a running maximum \(m\) and running sum \(l\) of exponentials, and correct them whenever a new block arrives.

\[ m_{\text{new}} = \max(m_{\text{old}},\ m_{\text{block}}) \qquad l_{\text{new}} = l_{\text{old}}\,e^{m_{\text{old}} - m_{\text{new}}} + \sum_{s \in \text{block}} e^{s - m_{\text{new}}} \]
  • \(m\): the largest score seen so far. Subtracting it keeps \(e^{s}\) from overflowing to a huge number
  • \(l\): the running softmax denominator (the sum of the exponentials)
Worked example

Scores \([1, 2, 3]\) arrive in two blocks, \([1, 2]\) then \([3]\). Block 1: \(m = 2\), \(l = e^{-1} + e^{0} = 1.3679\). Block 2: the new maximum is 3, so \(l = 1.3679 \cdot e^{2-3} + e^{0} = 1.5032\). Computing it all at once with maximum 3 gives \(e^{-2} + e^{-1} + 1 = 1.5032\), identical. FlashAttention is exact, not an approximation.

8.18 Fast generation: KV cache, speculative decoding and MLA

The KV cache and why it is huge

When generating text, the model produces one token at a time. To avoid recomputing the Keys and Values (Section 8.2) of all earlier tokens, it stores them in a KV cache. That cache grows with every token and every user.

\[ \text{KV bytes} = 2 \times \text{layers} \times \text{heads} \times d_{\text{head}} \times \text{seq\_len} \times \text{bytes per number} \]
  • \(2\): one copy for Keys and one for Values
Worked example

32 layers, 32 heads, \(d_{\text{head}} = 128\), sequence length 4096, 2 bytes per number: \(2 \times 32 \times 32 \times 128 \times 4096 \times 2 = 2{,}147{,}483{,}648\) bytes, i.e. 2 GiB for a single conversation. Multi-head Latent Attention (MLA) stores one small compressed vector per token per layer instead. If that vector has 512 numbers, the cache is \(32 \times 512 \times 4096 \times 2 = 134{,}217{,}728\) bytes = 128 MiB, which is 16 times smaller (illustrative numbers; your vault reports around a 93% reduction for a real model).

Speculative decoding

Generating with a huge model is slow because each token requires reading all the weights from memory. Speculative decoding lets a small, fast draft model guess several tokens, then the big model checks them all in a single pass. A guessed token is kept with probability \(\min(1, p_{\text{target}}/p_{\text{draft}})\), so the final text has exactly the same distribution as the big model alone.

\[ P(\text{accept}) = \min\!\left(1,\ \frac{p_{\text{target}}}{p_{\text{draft}}}\right) \qquad \mathbb{E}[\text{tokens per big pass}] = \frac{1 - \alpha^{k+1}}{1 - \alpha} \]
  • \(\alpha\): the average chance a drafted token is accepted
  • \(k\): how many tokens the draft model proposes each round
Worked example

If the big model gives a token probability 0.6 and the draft gave it 0.8, the acceptance chance is \(\min(1, 0.6/0.8) = 0.75\). With \(\alpha = 0.8\) and \(k = 4\), each big-model pass yields \((1 - 0.8^5)/(1 - 0.8) = 3.36\) tokens on average instead of 1.

8.19 Distributed training and ZeRO

Why one GPU is not enough

With data parallelism, each GPU processes a different slice of the batch and the gradients are averaged: \(g = \tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N} g_i\). But every GPU still holds a full copy of everything. With mixed-precision Adam, each parameter needs about 16 bytes: 2 for the 16-bit weight, 2 for its gradient, and 12 for the optimizer (a 4-byte master copy, 4-byte momentum \(m\) and 4-byte velocity \(v\), Section 5.7). ZeRO splits (shards) these pieces across the \(N\) GPUs instead of copying them.

\[ \text{no ZeRO: } 16 \quad \text{ZeRO-1: } 2 + 2 + \tfrac{12}{N} \quad \text{ZeRO-2: } 2 + \tfrac{2 + 12}{N} \quad \text{ZeRO-3: } \tfrac{16}{N} \quad (\text{bytes per parameter}) \]
  • \(N\): the number of GPUs
Worked example

A 7-billion-parameter model on \(N = 8\) GPUs: without ZeRO, \(16 \times 7\times10^9 = 112\) GB per GPU (too big). ZeRO-1: 38.5 GB. ZeRO-2: 26.25 GB. ZeRO-3: 14 GB.

Why it matters

Two other splits exist. Tensor parallelism cuts one big matrix multiplication into column blocks, \(XW = [XW_1 \mid XW_2]\), one block per GPU. Pipeline parallelism puts different layers on different GPUs.

8.20 Mechanistic interpretability: looking inside the network

Sparse autoencoders (SAE)

Networks squeeze more concepts than they have neurons by overlapping them in the same space (superposition), so a single neuron mixes several ideas. A sparse autoencoder re-expresses an internal activation \(x\) using many more features \(f\), but with a penalty so that only a few are active at once. Each feature then tends to line up with one human-readable idea.

\[ f = \text{ReLU}(W_e x + b_e) \qquad \hat{x} = W_d f \qquad \mathcal{L} = \|x - \hat{x}\|_2^2 + \lambda\,\|f\|_1 \]
  • \(x\): an activation vector taken from inside the model
  • \(f\): the (mostly zero) feature activations
  • \(W_e, W_d\): encoder and decoder weights
  • \(\lambda\): how strongly to punish using many features (the L1 penalty from Section 5.12)
Worked example

Suppose the features come out as \(f = [0, 2, 0, 1]\). Then \(\|f\|_1 = 3\), so with \(\lambda = 0.1\) the sparsity penalty is \(0.3\). Two features being non-zero is "sparse"; forcing all four to be active would cost more. Overlap between feature directions is measured with the cosine similarity of Section 2.1.3.

8.21 Test-time scaling, reward models for reasoning, and pass@k

Best-of-N and majority voting

Instead of making the model bigger, let it "think longer": sample many answers and pick the best. If each attempt is correct with probability \(p\) and you have a reliable checker, the chance at least one of \(N\) attempts is right is \(1 - (1-p)^N\). Without a checker, you can take a majority vote (if 45 of 100 samples say 42, choose 42).

\[ P(\text{at least one correct}) = 1 - (1 - p)^{N} \]
  • \(p\): chance a single attempt is right
  • \(N\): number of attempts
Worked example

With \(p = 0.3\) and \(N = 8\): \(1 - 0.7^{8} = 1 - 0.0576 = 0.942\). A model that is right only 30% of the time on one try is right about 94% of the time with 8 tries and a checker.

Process reward models (PRM) and pass@k

An outcome reward model judges only the final answer. A process reward model scores every reasoning step, so a lucky wrong path can be caught. One simple way to combine step scores is to multiply them. For code, pass@k reports the chance that at least one of \(k\) sampled solutions passes the tests.

\[ \text{PRM score} = \prod_{t} s_t \qquad \text{pass@}k = 1 - \frac{\binom{n-c}{k}}{\binom{n}{k}} \]
  • \(s_t\): the score (between 0 and 1) for reasoning step \(t\)
  • \(n\): total samples generated
  • \(c\): how many of them are correct
  • \(\binom{n}{k}\): "n choose k": the number of ways to pick \(k\) items out of \(n\)
Worked example

Step scores \(0.9, 0.8, 0.95\) give \(0.9 \times 0.8 \times 0.95 = 0.684\). For pass@k with \(n = 10\) samples of which \(c = 3\) are correct: pass@1 \(= 1 - \binom{7}{1}/\binom{10}{1} = 0.3\); pass@5 \(= 1 - \binom{7}{5}/\binom{10}{5} = 1 - 21/252 = 0.917\).

8.22 Vector databases, LSH, HNSW and RAG

Searching by meaning

An embedding turns a sentence into a vector so that similar meanings land close together. A vector database stores millions of these vectors and finds the ones nearest to a query, usually by cosine similarity (Section 2.1.3). Comparing the query against every stored vector costs \(N \times d\) multiplications, which is too slow at scale, so databases use approximate nearest-neighbour tricks.

\[ \text{cosine}(q, d) = \frac{q \cdot d}{\|q\|\,\|d\|} \qquad P(\text{same hash bit}) = 1 - \frac{\theta}{\pi} \]
  • \(q, d\): the query vector and a document vector
  • \(\theta\): the angle between two vectors (in radians)
  • \(N, d\): number of stored vectors and their length
Worked example

Query \(q = [1, 0]\). Document 1 is \([0.9, 0.1]\) with cosine 0.994; document 2 is \([0.2, 0.9]\) with cosine 0.217, so document 1 is retrieved. The brute-force cost for \(N = 1{,}000{,}000\) vectors of length 768 is \(768{,}000{,}000\) multiplications per query. LSH (locality-sensitive hashing) hashes vectors with random dividing planes so similar vectors usually share a hash: two vectors with cosine 0.5 (\(\theta = 60^\circ\)) agree on a random plane with probability \(1 - 60/180 = 0.667\). HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World) builds layers of links, with sparse "highway" layers on top, and walks greedily downward, needing only about \(\log N\) steps.

RAG: retrieval-augmented generation

Instead of hoping the model memorized a fact, RAG retrieves relevant text chunks from a database and pastes them into the prompt before the model answers. The steps are: split documents into chunks, embed them, embed the question, fetch the closest chunks, then generate. When you combine several rankings (say keyword search and vector search), Reciprocal Rank Fusion rewards documents ranked high in any list.

\[ \text{RRF}(d) = \sum_{m} \frac{1}{k + r_m(d)} \qquad \text{Faithfulness} = \frac{\text{claims supported by the context}}{\text{total claims in the answer}} \]
  • \(r_m(d)\): the rank of document \(d\) in ranking list \(m\) (1 is best)
  • \(k\): a smoothing constant, commonly 60
Worked example

A document ranked 1st in one list and 3rd in another scores \(1/61 + 1/63 = 0.0323\). If 4 of the 5 claims in an answer are supported by the retrieved text, faithfulness is \(4/5 = 0.8\).

8.23 Multimodal models, agents and efficiency arithmetic

Images as tokens, and the agent loop

A Vision Transformer (ViT) cuts an image into square patches, flattens each patch into a vector, and treats the patches like words. For a 224 by 224 image with 16 by 16 patches there are \((224/16)^2 = 196\) patches, each with \(16 \times 16 \times 3 = 768\) colour numbers. Audio can be turned into a picture-like spectrogram and handled the same way. An agent is a loop: the model thinks, writes a structured request to call a tool (for example a small block of JSON), reads the tool's result, and repeats. Tool calling is just next-token prediction of a specific text format.

How fast and how costly is a model?

A forward pass costs about 2 FLOPs per parameter per token. MFU (model FLOPs utilization) is the fraction of the GPU's peak speed you actually achieve. Generating text is usually limited by memory bandwidth: for every token the GPU must read all the weights once.

\[ \text{FLOPs per token} \approx 2N \qquad \text{MFU} = \frac{\text{achieved FLOP/s}}{\text{peak FLOP/s}} \qquad \text{tokens/s} \lesssim \frac{\text{memory bandwidth}}{\text{model bytes}} \]
  • \(N\): number of parameters
Worked example

A 7-billion-parameter model needs \(2 \times 7\times10^9 = 14\) billion FLOPs per token. Achieving 300 of a possible 1000 TFLOP/s is an MFU of 30%. With 14 GB of 16-bit weights and a GPU that reads 1000 GB/s (illustrative numbers), each token takes at least \(14/1000 = 14\) ms, so at most about 71 tokens per second. Quantizing to 4-bit (3.5 GB) would raise that ceiling about 4 times.

In your vault

Reports/Deep_Dives/15, 23, 24, 25–27, 29, 31–33, 35, 36, 38, 39, 41 and 42 (and the matching Code/Deep_Dives and Sims files) cover Sections 8.11 to 8.23 in far more depth; Reports/09_Complete_ML_DL_AI_Libraries_Master_Guide.md covers the software side.

8.24 Decoding: how a model turns probabilities into text

Greedy, temperature, top-k, top-p, repetition penalty and beam search

At each step the model outputs a probability for every token (Section 8.6). Decoding is the rule for picking one. Greedy always takes the most likely token: predictable but dull, and prone to endless repetition. Temperature (Section 8.7) reshapes the probabilities. Top-k keeps only the \(k\) most likely tokens, renormalizes, and samples among them. Top-p (nucleus) keeps the smallest set of tokens whose probabilities add up to at least \(p\), so the shortlist is long when the model is unsure and short when it is confident. A repetition penalty divides the logit of an already-used token by a factor \(\theta > 1\) (and multiplies it if the logit is negative). Beam search keeps the \(B\) best partial sentences at every step instead of just one, which can find a higher-probability whole sentence than greedy choice.

\[ \text{top-}p: \ \ \text{keep the smallest set } S \text{ with } \sum_{i\in S}p_i \ge p \qquad \text{penalty: } z_i \leftarrow \begin{cases} z_i/\theta & z_i > 0\\ z_i\,\theta & z_i \le 0\end{cases} \]
  • \(p_i\): probability of token \(i\)
  • \(z_i\): its logit (raw score)
  • \(\theta\): penalty factor, e.g. 1.2
Worked example

Top-k: logits \([3, 2, 1, 0]\) with \(k = 2\) keep the first two, whose softmax shares become \([0.731,\ 0.269]\). Top-p: probabilities \([0.60, 0.25, 0.10, 0.05]\) with \(p = 0.9\): the running totals are \(0.60,\ 0.85,\ 0.95\), so the first three tokens are kept and renormalized to \([0.632,\ 0.263,\ 0.105]\). Penalty: with \(\theta = 1.2\), a reused token’s logit \(2.4\) becomes \(2.0\), and a negative logit \(-1\) becomes \(-1.2\). Beam search: the first token A has probability 0.5 (then best continuation 0.6) and B has 0.4 (then best continuation 0.95). Greedy picks A and reaches \(0.5\times0.6 = 0.30\), but keeping both beams finds \(0.4\times0.95 = 0.38\), a better complete sentence.

Why it matters

Typical chat settings are temperature about 0.7 with top-p about 0.9. Setting temperature to 0 for open-ended text often produces the "endless echo" repetition loops described in Section 18.7.

8.25 Long context: ALiBi, YaRN, sliding windows and grouped-query attention

Four tricks for handling very long inputs

ALiBi adds no position vectors at all: it subtracts a penalty proportional to the distance between two tokens from the attention score, with a different slope for each head, so far-away tokens are naturally attended to less and the model extrapolates to lengths it never trained on. NTK-aware scaling and YaRN stretch RoPE (Section 8.10) by dividing its rotation angles by a scale factor, letting a model trained on short contexts handle longer ones with little or no retraining. Sliding-window attention lets each token attend only to the previous \(w\) tokens, so cost grows as \(n\cdot w\) instead of \(n^2\). Multi-Query (MQA) and Grouped-Query attention (GQA) share Keys and Values across several query heads, shrinking the KV cache (Section 8.18).

\[ \text{score}_{ij} = \frac{q_i\cdot k_j}{\sqrt{d_k}} - m\,(i - j) \qquad \text{cost}_{\text{window}} = n\,w \qquad \text{GQA cache} = \frac{h_{kv}}{h_q}\times\text{MHA cache} \]
  • \(m\): the head-specific ALiBi slope (for 8 heads: \(1/2, 1/4, \ldots, 1/256\))
  • \(i - j\): how far back token \(j\) is from token \(i\)
  • \(w\): the window size
Worked example

ALiBi: a head with slope \(m = 0.5\) lowers the score of a token 3 positions back by \(0.5\times3 = 1.5\). Window: for \(n = 100{,}000\) tokens, full attention needs \(n^2 = 10^{10}\) score entries, a window of \(w = 4096\) only \(n\,w = 4.1\times10^{8}\), about 24 times fewer. (Information still travels farther than \(w\) because stacked layers pass it along: after \(L\) layers a token can be influenced by tokens about \(L\cdot w\) away.) MQA is GQA with a single shared Key/Value head, the smallest cache of all.

8.26 Training memory tricks: gradient accumulation, checkpointing and bf16

Fitting a big model on a small GPU

Gradient accumulation simulates a big batch with small ones: run several small "micro-batches", add up their gradients, and take one optimizer step. Gradient checkpointing does not store every layer’s activations for the backward pass; it stores only some and recomputes the rest when needed, trading extra compute for much less memory (your playbook reports about 20% more compute for about 60% less VRAM). Mixed precision uses 16-bit numbers for speed and memory. There are two 16-bit formats: fp16 has more fraction bits but a tiny range (its largest value is only 65,504), so numbers overflow easily; bf16 keeps the same range as 32-bit floats with fewer fraction bits, so it overflows far less and is the usual choice for training.

\[ \text{effective batch} = \text{micro-batch}\times\text{accumulation steps}\times\text{number of GPUs} \qquad \text{loss per micro-step} = \frac{\mathcal{L}}{\text{accumulation steps}} \]
Worked example

A micro-batch of 8 repeated over 8 accumulation steps gives an effective batch of \(64\); on 4 GPUs it is \(256\). Each micro-loss is divided by 8 so the summed gradient equals the average over the whole batch. fp16 overflow: a single \(e^{11} = 59{,}874\) still fits under 65,504, but \(e^{12} = 162{,}755\) does not, so an unscaled softmax over large logits can become infinity in fp16 and fine in bf16.

8.27 Data curation: deduplication with MinHash and LSH

Quality beats quantity

Your report “29” stresses that data quality matters more than raw size. A modern pipeline deduplicates (a sentence seen 50 times gets memorized), filters for quality (a small classifier scores each page and low scorers are dropped), and increasingly adds synthetic data written by strong models. Exact duplicates are easy to find with hashes. Near-duplicates (pages 90% identical) need a measure of overlap: the Jaccard similarity of the two documents’ word sets. MinHash estimates it cheaply: hash every word with a random hash function and keep the smallest value; the chance that two documents share the same minimum equals their Jaccard similarity. Repeat with \(k\) hash functions to get a signature, and the fraction of matching positions estimates the similarity. LSH banding then splits signatures into \(b\) bands of \(r\) values, and two documents become "candidate duplicates" if any whole band matches.

\[ J(A,B) = \frac{|A\cap B|}{|A\cup B|} \qquad \hat{J} = \frac{\#\text{matching signature entries}}{k} \qquad P(\text{candidate}) = 1 - \big(1 - s^{\,r}\big)^{b} \]
  • \(s\): the true Jaccard similarity
  • \(k\): signature length
  • \(b, r\): number of bands and rows per band, so \(k = b\,r\)
Worked example

Document A has the word set \(\{1,2,3,4\}\) and B has \(\{2,3,4,5\}\): intersection 3, union 5, so \(J = 3/5 = 0.6\). A signature of \(k = 128\) hashes estimates it with a standard error of \(\sqrt{0.6\cdot0.4/128} = 0.043\). Banding with \(r = 5\) rows and \(b = 20\) bands: a pair with \(s = 0.5\) becomes a candidate with probability \(1 - (1 - 0.5^5)^{20} = 0.47\); at \(s = 0.8\) it is \(0.9996\); at \(s = 0.9\) essentially 1. The curve is an S: dissimilar pairs are almost always skipped and similar pairs almost always caught (the threshold sits near \((1/b)^{1/r} = 0.55\)).

8.28 Evaluating models, and why "emergence" is partly a measurement effect

Benchmarks, confidence intervals and Elo

Standard benchmarks test different skills: MMLU (57-subject multiple choice), HumanEval (write code that passes hidden unit tests, scored with pass@k from Section 8.21), GSM8K and MATH (maths reasoning), and Chatbot Arena, where humans vote between two anonymous answers and models are ranked with an Elo rating. Two cautions apply to every benchmark. First, a score on \(n\) questions has statistical noise, so small differences are meaningless. Second, contamination: if test questions leaked into the training data, the score measures memory, not skill. Stress tests probe weaknesses: needle in a haystack (find one hidden fact in a long document), the reversal curse (a model that learned "A is B’s mother" may fail "who is A’s child?"), and jailbreaks.

\[ SE = \sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}},\ \ \text{95\% CI} = p \pm 1.96\,SE \qquad E_A = \frac{1}{1 + 10^{(R_B - R_A)/400}} \qquad R_A \leftarrow R_A + K\,(S_A - E_A) \]
  • \(p\): the accuracy
  • \(n\): number of test questions
  • \(E_A\): the expected score of A against B
  • \(S_A\): the actual result: 1 win, 0.5 draw, 0 loss
  • \(K\): how fast ratings move, e.g. 32
Worked example

Confidence: 80% accuracy on \(n = 1000\) questions has \(SE = 0.0126\), so about \(\pm2.5\) points; on only \(n = 100\) it is \(\pm7.8\) points, so a 4-point gap between two models on 100 questions means nothing. Elo: A rated 1000 versus B rated 1200 has \(E_A = 1/(1 + 10^{0.5}) = 0.24\). If A wins (\(S = 1\)) with \(K = 32\), A gains \(32(1 - 0.24) = 24.3\) points; a draw gains \(8.3\); a loss costs \(7.7\). Upsets move ratings the most.

Emergence: sudden jumps that may be a trick of the metric

Some abilities seem to appear suddenly as models grow ("emergence"). One explanation is the metric. If a task needs \(m\) steps all correct, and the per-step accuracy \(p\) improves smoothly, the all-or-nothing score is \(p^{m}\), which stays near zero for a long time and then shoots up. Your report also describes the compression hypothesis: to predict text well, a model benefits from learning the structure that generates it.

\[ \text{task score} = p^{m} \]
Worked example

A 10-step task: per-step accuracy \(0.80 \to 0.90 \to 0.95 \to 0.99\) gives task scores \(0.107,\ 0.349,\ 0.599,\ 0.904\). Per-step skill rose smoothly, yet the task seemed to "switch on". Using a partial-credit metric instead would show gradual progress.

8.29 Fine-tuning in depth: PEFT variants, preference methods and quantization formats

Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) beyond LoRA

Your fine-tuning report maps the options. Full fine-tuning updates all weights. LoRA (Section 8.9) trains tiny low-rank matrices. QLoRA does LoRA on a 4-bit-quantized frozen base model (Section 8.13). DoRA splits each weight into a learned magnitude \(m\) and a direction \(V/\|V\|\) and applies LoRA only to the direction. Adapters insert small bottleneck layers (shrink, nonlinearity, expand) after attention and feed-forward blocks. Prefix tuning prepends trainable key/value vectors at every layer and freezes everything else.

\[ \text{LoRA: } W = W_0 + \tfrac{\alpha}{r}BA \qquad \text{DoRA: } W = m\,\frac{W_0 + \Delta W}{\|W_0 + \Delta W\|} \qquad \text{adapter: } h \leftarrow h + W_{\text{up}}\,\sigma(W_{\text{down}}h) \]
  • \(\alpha/r\): the LoRA scaling factor
  • \(W_{\text{down}}, W_{\text{up}}\): the adapter’s shrinking and expanding matrices
Worked example

LoRA scale: rank \(r = 8\) with \(\alpha = 16\) multiplies the update by \(2\). Prefix tuning on a 32-layer model with \(d = 4096\) and a prefix of 20 tokens trains \(2\times32\times20\times4096 = 5{,}242{,}880\) numbers (keys and values), about 0.075% of a 7-billion-parameter model. An adapter with bottleneck 64 adds \(2\times4096\times64 = 524{,}288\) numbers per insertion point.

Why it matters

Debugging tip from your playbook: in LoRA, matrix \(A\) starts random and matrix \(B\) starts at zero, so at step 0 \(\Delta W = BA = 0\) and the model starts exactly as the base model. If both start random, training begins from a corrupted model.

Preference methods: PPO, DPO, KTO, ORPO and SimPO

Section 8.14 covered DPO. The family has more members. RLHF/PPO needs a policy, a reward model, a value model and a reference model. KTO needs only a thumbs-up or thumbs-down per answer rather than paired comparisons. ORPO adds an odds-ratio penalty to the ordinary SFT loss, removing the reference model. SimPO uses the average log-probability per token as the implicit reward, with a target margin \(\gamma\), also with no reference model.

\[ \text{odds}(p) = \frac{p}{1-p} \qquad \mathcal{L}_{\text{OR}} = -\ln\sigma\!\left(\ln\frac{\text{odds}(y_w)}{\text{odds}(y_l)}\right) \qquad \mathcal{L}_{\text{SimPO}} = -\ln\sigma\big(\beta\,\bar{\ell}_w - \beta\,\bar{\ell}_l - \gamma\big) \]
  • \(\bar{\ell}\): a response’s average per-token log-probability
  • \(\gamma\): the required margin between chosen and rejected
Worked example

ORPO: the model gives the chosen answer probability 0.6 (odds \(1.5\)) and the rejected one 0.3 (odds \(0.429\)); the odds ratio is \(3.5\), its log is \(1.253\), and the penalty is \(-\ln\sigma(1.253) = 0.251\). SimPO: with \(\beta = 2\), average log-probs \(-0.5\) (chosen) and \(-0.9\) (rejected), and \(\gamma = 0.5\): \(\sigma\)-input \(= (-1.0) - (-1.8) - 0.5 = 0.3\), loss \(= -\ln\sigma(0.3) = 0.554\).

Quantization formats you will see in practice

NF4 (4-bit NormalFloat) places its 16 levels where bell-curve-distributed weights are most common, which wastes fewer levels (used by QLoRA). GPTQ quantizes once after training, adjusting remaining weights to compensate for each rounding error using second-order information. AWQ protects the roughly 1% of weight channels that matter most, judged by activation size, and scales them before quantizing. GGUF is a file format for running quantized models on CPUs and consumer GPUs with llama.cpp. FP8 is used for training on newer GPUs.

8.30 GPUs, the roofline model and Triton kernels

Compute-bound versus memory-bound

A GPU has two limits: how many arithmetic operations it can do per second (FLOP/s) and how many bytes it can read from its main memory per second (bandwidth). The arithmetic intensity of an operation is the work done per byte moved. Below a GPU’s "ridge point" (peak FLOP/s divided by bandwidth) the operation is memory-bound: the cores sit idle waiting for data. Above it, it is compute-bound. FlashAttention (Section 8.17) wins by raising the intensity. Triton is a Python-like language for writing GPU kernels: you write what one block of threads does, loading tiles from slow memory into fast on-chip memory (tl.load), doing the maths (tl.dot), and writing back (tl.store).

\[ \text{intensity} = \frac{\text{FLOPs}}{\text{bytes moved}} \qquad \text{ridge point} = \frac{\text{peak FLOP/s}}{\text{bandwidth}} \]
Worked example

Using your report’s H100 figures (about \(2000\) TFLOP/s and \(3.35\) TB/s) the ridge point is \(2000/3.35 \approx 597\) FLOPs per byte. Adding two 16-bit vectors: 1 operation per element but 6 bytes moved (read 2+2, write 2), intensity \(1/6 = 0.17\), far below 597: memory-bound. Multiplying two \(N\times N\) 16-bit matrices: \(2N^3\) operations over \(6N^2\) bytes, intensity \(N/3\): for \(N = 1024\) that is 341 (still memory-bound), for \(N = 4096\) it is 1365 (compute-bound). The naive attention matrix at a 128,000-token context has \(128{,}000^2\) entries, about 65.5 GB in 32-bit floats, which is why it must never be stored.

8.31 Serving at scale: PagedAttention, continuous batching and honest benchmarking

Not wasting GPU memory or time

Preallocating a KV cache for the maximum length wastes most of it. PagedAttention (the idea behind vLLM) stores the cache in small fixed-size blocks that need not be contiguous, like an operating system’s virtual memory, allocating a new block only when a sequence grows. Static batching waits until the longest request in a batch finishes, leaving the GPU idle; continuous batching swaps in a new request the moment one finishes. To benchmark honestly, measure latency (time for one answer, including time-to-first-token), throughput (tokens per second overall), peak VRAM and FLOPs, and repeat the test at several sequence lengths: standard attention’s memory curves up as \(n^2\), a linear-time architecture draws a straight line.

\[ \text{blocks} = \left\lceil \frac{\text{tokens}}{\text{block size}}\right\rceil \qquad \text{throughput} = \frac{\text{batch size}}{\text{time per step}} \]
Worked example

Paging: a 1000-token sequence with 16-token blocks needs \(\lceil1000/16\rceil = 63\) blocks \(= 1008\) slots, wasting only 8 (0.8%). Preallocating for a 4096-token maximum wastes \((4096 - 1000)/4096 = 75.6\%\). Batching: three requests needing \([100, 40, 20]\) tokens do \(160\) tokens of useful work, but a static batch occupies \(3\times100 = 300\) slots, so utilization is \(53\%\). Scaling test: going from 512 to 4096 tokens multiplies the attention matrix by \((4096/512)^2 = 64\) (from \(262{,}144\) to \(16{,}777{,}216\) entries); to 16,384 tokens it is a further \(16\times\) (to \(268{,}435{,}456\)). Throughput: a batch of 32 completing a step in 0.04 s generates \(32/0.04 = 800\) tokens per second.

8.32 Multimodal models in more depth

Cross-attention, audio spectrograms and token budgets

An omnimodal model puts text, images, audio and video into one shared embedding space (Section 8.23). Two ways to combine them: feed everything in as one long token sequence, or use cross-attention, where the Queries come from one modality (text) and the Keys and Values from another (image patches), so each word can "look at" the picture. Continuous signals are first cut into tokens. Audio becomes a spectrogram (energy over time and frequency), often on the mel scale, which spaces frequencies the way human hearing does (finely at low pitch, coarsely at high pitch).

\[ \text{CrossAttn}(T, V) = \text{softmax}\!\left(\frac{T\,V^{T}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)V \qquad m = 2595\,\log_{10}\!\left(1 + \frac{f}{700}\right) \]
  • \(T\): text token vectors (the queries)
  • \(V\): image or audio token vectors (the keys and values)
  • \(f\): frequency in hertz, \(m\) the mel value
Worked example

Mel scale: 100 Hz maps to 150 mel and 1000 Hz to 1000 mel, but 4000 Hz maps to only 2146 mel: the top three octaves are squeezed into about twice the mel range of the bottom octaves. Token budgets: 30 seconds of audio at 100 frames per second is 3000 tokens; 8 video frames of 196 patches each (Section 8.23) is 1568 tokens. Long sequences are the main engineering cost of multimodality, and attention costs grow with their square.

8.33 Agents and reasoning in more depth

The tool loop, compounding errors and GRPO

An agent loops: think (write reasoning), act (emit a structured tool call), observe (read the result), and repeat, with a self-correction step when a result looks wrong. Tool-use ability is trained by fine-tuning on examples of correct calls (Section 8.14). The hazard is compounding error: if each step is right with probability \(p\), a chain of \(n\) steps succeeds with probability \(p^n\). To train reasoning with reinforcement learning, GRPO (which your SOTA review associates with DeepSeek-R1) avoids a separate value model: it samples a group of answers to one question and scores each answer relative to the group.

\[ P(\text{chain succeeds}) = p^{\,n} \qquad A_i = \frac{r_i - \text{mean}(r_{1..G})}{\text{std}(r_{1..G})} \]
  • \(r_i\): the reward of the \(i\)-th sampled answer
  • \(G\): the group size
  • \(A_i\): its advantage (Section 9.10)
Worked example

Compounding: 10 steps at 95% each succeed only \(0.95^{10} = 59.9\%\) of the time; at 99% each, \(90.4\%\); and 100 steps at 99% each, only \(36.6\%\). GRPO: 4 sampled answers with rewards \([1, 0, 0, 1]\) have mean 0.5 and standard deviation 0.5, so the advantages are \([+1, -1, -1, +1]\); if only one of four is wrong, rewards \([1, 1, 1, 0]\) give advantages \([0.58, 0.58, 0.58, -1.73]\), pushing hard against the rare failure.

8.34 Advanced RAG: chunking, BM25, HyDE and reranking

Getting the right text into the prompt

Chunking splits documents into pieces small enough to embed, with some overlap so a sentence cut at the boundary appears whole in one chunk. Semantic chunking cuts where the meaning shifts rather than at fixed lengths. Hybrid search combines keyword search with vector search (fused by RRF, Section 8.22). The classic keyword score is BM25, an improved TF-IDF that rewards rare words (IDF) and caps the benefit of repeating a word (saturation). HyDE first has the model write a hypothetical answer and embeds that instead of the short question, because an answer looks more like the documents. A cross-encoder reranker reads the question and each candidate together for a much more accurate score, but is slow, so it only re-sorts the top few from the fast first-stage search. In Modular/Advanced RAG these stages (rewrite query, retrieve, rerank, generate, check) are separate swappable components.

\[ \text{IDF}(t) = \ln\frac{N}{df_t} \qquad \text{BM25 term} = \text{IDF}(t)\cdot\frac{tf\,(k_1 + 1)}{tf + k_1} \qquad \#\text{chunks} = \left\lceil\frac{L - o}{c - o}\right\rceil \]
  • \(N, df_t\): number of documents, and how many contain term \(t\)
  • \(tf\): how often the term appears in this document
  • \(k_1\): saturation constant, about 1.5
  • \(L, c, o\): document length, chunk size, overlap (all in tokens)
Worked example

IDF: a word in 1000 of 1,000,000 documents has IDF \(\ln(1000) = 6.91\); a word in every document has IDF 0. Saturation with \(k_1 = 1.5\): the factor \(tf\cdot2.5/(tf + 1.5)\) gives \(1.0,\ 1.67,\ 2.17\) for \(tf = 1,\ 3,\ 10\): repeating a word 10 times is worth only about twice one mention. Chunks: a 10,000-token document with 500-token chunks and 50-token overlap needs \(\lceil(10000 - 50)/(500 - 50)\rceil = \lceil22.1\rceil = 23\) chunks.

8.35 What the 2026 literature review in your zip highlights

Your report 37_2026_SOTA_Literature_Review surveys frontier systems of its time: the OpenAI o-series and GPT-4o family, Anthropic’s Claude 3.5 line with its computer-use capability, xAI’s Grok models, Google’s Gemini 1.5/2.0 (very long context), DeepSeek-V3 and R1, and Google Research’s Titans memory. Model names and versions change every few months, so treat the list as a snapshot. The ideas it highlights are the ones you now have the maths for: MoE with many fine-grained routed experts (Section 8.8), Multi-head Latent Attention that compresses the KV cache (8.18), test-time compute and process rewards (8.21), reinforcement-learning-trained reasoning with GRPO (8.33), long-context architectures (8.15, 8.16, 8.25), and learned long-term memory (8.16). To learn about the current state of the art, read the primary papers in the reading list in Section 18.11.

In your vault

Reports/Deep_Dives/23, 28, 29, 30, 35, 37, 40 to 44 and LLM_DEBUGGING_AND_TROUBLESHOOTING.md cover Sections 8.24 to 8.35 in full depth.

Part 9. Reinforcement learning and RLHF

Reinforcement Learning (RL) is how a model learns by taking actions and receiving rewards, rather than being shown correct labels directly. It's the final piece behind how LLMs get tuned to human preferences (RLHF, used in ChatGPT-style alignment).

9.1 The basic setup: states, actions, rewards

An agent sits in some state \(s\), takes an action \(a\), and receives a reward \(r\), moving to a new state \(s'\). A policy \(\pi(a\mid s)\) is the agent's strategy — its probability of taking each action in each state (recall \(\pi\) from Part 0.2). The discounted return sums up all future rewards, valuing near-term rewards more:

\[ G_t = r_t + \gamma r_{t+1} + \gamma^2 r_{t+2} + \dots = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^k r_{t+k} \]

\(\gamma \in [0,1]\) ("gamma") is the discount factor: closer to 1 means the agent cares almost as much about far-future rewards; closer to 0 means it only cares about immediate reward.

Worked example

Rewards of 1 at each of the next 3 steps, with \(\gamma=0.9\): \(G = 1 + 0.9(1) + 0.9^2(1) = 1+0.9+0.81 = 2.71\).

9.2 Q-learning: learning the value of every action

\[ Q(s,a) \leftarrow Q(s,a) + \alpha\left[r + \gamma\max_{a'}Q(s',a') - Q(s,a)\right] \]

\(Q(s,a)\) estimates "how good is it to take action \(a\) in state \(s\), accounting for all future rewards?" This update rule nudges that estimate toward the reward actually received, plus the discounted value of the best action available in the next state — the term in brackets is called the TD error (temporal difference error), and it's exactly analogous to the loss/gradient signal from Section 5.4/5.5, just for sequential decisions instead of one-shot predictions.

Worked example

Current estimate \(Q(s,a)=0\), reward received \(r=1\), discount \(\gamma=0.9\), best next-state value \(\max_{a'}Q(s',a')=2\), learning rate \(\alpha=0.5\):

\[ Q_{\text{new}} = 0 + 0.5\left[1+0.9(2)-0\right] = 0.5\times2.8 = 1.4 \]

The estimate jumps from 0 toward 1.4, halfway (because \(\alpha=0.5\)) toward the newly-observed target of 2.8.

9.3 Policy gradients: directly learning the strategy

\[ \nabla_\theta J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}\left[\sum_t \nabla_\theta\ln\pi_\theta(a_t\mid s_t)\,G_t\right] \]

Instead of learning values and picking the best action, this directly adjusts the policy's parameters \(\theta\) to make good actions (ones followed by high return \(G_t\)) more likely and bad actions less likely — it's ordinary gradient ascent (Section 3.2, but climbing instead of descending, since here we want to maximize reward) on the log-probability of the actions actually taken, scaled by how good they turned out to be.

9.4 PPO: the algorithm behind RLHF

\[ r_t(\theta) = \frac{\pi_\theta(a_t\mid s_t)}{\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(a_t\mid s_t)} \qquad\qquad L^{\text{CLIP}} = \mathbb{E}_t\left[\min\big(r_t\hat A_t,\ \text{clip}(r_t, 1-\epsilon, 1+\epsilon)\hat A_t\big)\right] \]

Proximal Policy Optimization improves the policy-gradient idea by measuring how much the policy has changed (\(r_t\), a ratio of new vs. old probability for the same action) and clipping it so a single update can never move the policy too far from where it started — preventing training from collapsing after one bad, over-eager update. \(\hat A_t\) ("advantage") estimates how much better an action was than average.

9.5 RLHF's reward model: learning from human preferences (Bradley-Terry)

\[ P(\text{response A} \succ \text{response B}) = \frac{1}{1+e^{-(r_A - r_B)}} = \sigma(r_A-r_B) \]

To align an LLM with human preferences, humans compare pairs of model responses ("A is better than B"). A reward model is trained so that the sigmoid (Section 5.2.1) of its score difference matches how often humans actually preferred A — the bigger the gap the reward model predicts, the more confidently it should expect humans to have agreed.

Worked example

If the reward model scores response A as \(r_A=2\) and response B as \(r_B=0.5\): predicted probability humans preferred A is \(\sigma(2-0.5)=\sigma(1.5)=0.818\) — 81.8% confidence, matching intuition that a bigger reward gap should mean a more lopsided human preference. The training loss is then \(-\log(0.818)=0.201\) if humans indeed preferred A (small loss, as expected for a correct, confident prediction — the exact same cross-entropy shape as Section 5.4.2).

Once this reward model exists, the LLM itself becomes the "policy" from Section 9.1, and PPO (or simpler variants) nudges its word-choice probabilities to maximize the reward model's score — this is, in one sentence, what "RLHF" (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) means.

In your vault

Algorithm_Codex/Code/Deep_Dives/G01–G14 cover bandits, MDPs, Q-learning, policy gradients, actor-critic, and PPO in full runnable code; 24_RLHF_PPO_Training_Loop.py and Reports/Deep_Dives/24_Scaling_Laws_and_RLHF.md connect this directly to how real LLMs are aligned.

9.6 Multi-armed bandits: the simplest exploration problem

Explore or exploit?

Imagine several slot machines ("arms") with unknown payouts. Always pulling the best-looking arm (exploiting) might miss a better one; trying random arms (exploring) wastes pulls. Two classic recipes: epsilon-greedy (with small probability \(\epsilon\) pull a random arm, otherwise pull the best-looking one) and UCB (pull the arm with the highest optimistic estimate, which is its average plus a bonus that shrinks the more you have tried it).

\[ \text{UCB}_a = Q_a + c\sqrt{\frac{\ln t}{N_a}} \]
  • \(Q_a\): the average reward seen so far from arm \(a\)
  • \(t\): total number of pulls so far
  • \(N_a\): how many times arm \(a\) has been pulled
  • \(c\): how strongly to favour exploration
Worked example

An arm has \(Q = 0.5\), has been pulled \(N = 10\) times out of \(t = 100\), and \(c = 2\). Its UCB score is \(0.5 + 2\sqrt{\ln(100)/10} = 0.5 + 2(0.679) = 1.857\). An arm pulled only twice would get a much bigger bonus, so UCB naturally tries neglected arms.

9.7 Markov Decision Processes and the Bellman equation

Values that depend on future values

An MDP is the formal setup for RL: states, actions, transition probabilities \(P(s'\mid s,a)\), and rewards. The value \(V(s)\) of a state is the best total discounted reward you can expect from there. The Bellman equation says that value equals the immediate reward plus the discounted value of where you land, averaged over the possible outcomes. Dynamic programming (value iteration) simply applies this equation repeatedly until the values stop changing.

\[ V(s) = \max_a \sum_{s'} P(s'\mid s,a)\,\big[r + \gamma V(s')\big] \]
  • \(P(s'\mid s,a)\): probability of landing in state \(s'\) after taking action \(a\) in state \(s\)
  • \(r\): the immediate reward
  • \(\gamma\): the discount factor from Section 9.1
Worked example

One action leads to state A (value 10) with probability 0.8 and to state B (value 0) with probability 0.2, reward \(r = 1\) either way, \(\gamma = 0.9\). Then \(V = 0.8(1 + 0.9\cdot10) + 0.2(1 + 0.9\cdot0) = 8 + 0.2 = 8.2\).

9.8 Learning values from experience: Monte Carlo and TD learning

Two ways to estimate \(V(s)\) without knowing the rules

Monte Carlo waits until an episode ends, then averages the actual total returns seen from each state. Temporal-Difference (TD) learning updates after every single step, using its own next-state estimate as a stand-in for the future ("bootstrapping"). TD learns faster but leans on estimates that may be wrong.

\[ V(s) \leftarrow V(s) + \alpha\big[\underbrace{r + \gamma V(s')}_{\text{TD target}} - V(s)\big] \]
  • \(\alpha\): learning rate
  • \(r + \gamma V(s') - V(s)\): the TD error: target minus current guess
Worked example

Monte Carlo: if three visits to a state produced returns \([2, 4, 6]\), then \(V = 4\). TD: with \(V(s) = 1\), \(r = 0.5\), \(V(s') = 2\), \(\gamma = 0.9\), \(\alpha = 0.1\): the target is \(0.5 + 1.8 = 2.3\), the error is \(1.3\), and the new value is \(1 + 0.1\times1.3 = 1.13\).

9.9 Deep Q-Networks (DQN)

Q-learning with a neural network

When there are too many states for a table (for example every possible Atari screen), a neural network estimates \(Q(s,a)\). Two tricks keep training stable: a replay buffer (train on random old experiences instead of consecutive, highly similar ones) and a target network (a slowly updated copy used to compute the target, so the goalpost does not move every step).

\[ \mathcal{L}(\theta) = \Big(r + \gamma \max_{a'} Q_{\theta^{-}}(s',a') - Q_\theta(s,a)\Big)^2 \]
  • \(\theta\): the network being trained
  • \(\theta^{-}\): the frozen target-network copy
Worked example

With \(r = 1\), \(\gamma = 0.9\), \(\max Q_{\theta^-}(s',a') = 2\) and current \(Q_\theta(s,a) = 1.5\): target \(= 1 + 1.8 = 2.8\), error \(= 1.3\), loss \(= 1.3^2 = 1.69\). Gradient descent (Section 3.2) then nudges \(Q_\theta\) upward.

9.10 Actor-critic methods

An actor that acts and a critic that scores

Policy gradients (Section 9.3) are noisy because a whole episode's return judges every action. Actor-critic uses two networks: the actor is the policy, and the critic estimates \(V(s)\). The advantage \(A\) says how much better an action turned out than the critic expected, which gives a much steadier learning signal.

\[ A_t = r_t + \gamma V(s_{t+1}) - V(s_t) \qquad \nabla_\theta J \approx \mathbb{E}\big[\nabla_\theta \ln \pi_\theta(a_t\mid s_t)\,A_t\big] \]
  • \(A_t\): the advantage (a TD error, Section 9.8)
Worked example

If the critic says \(V(s_t) = 5\), the reward was \(r_t = 1\), and \(V(s_{t+1}) = 5\) with \(\gamma = 0.9\): \(A = 1 + 4.5 - 5 = 0.5\). Positive, so the action was better than expected and its probability is increased.

9.11 Continuous control (DDPG) and model-based RL with MCTS

DDPG: an actor for continuous actions

Steering angles and motor torques are numbers, not a menu of choices, so we cannot take a max over actions. DDPG trains a deterministic actor \(\mu(s)\) that outputs the action directly, plus a critic \(Q(s,a)\). Its target networks are updated slowly by a "soft update" \(\theta^{-} \leftarrow \tau\theta + (1-\tau)\theta^{-}\). With \(\tau = 0.01\), \(\theta^- = 1\) and \(\theta = 2\), the new target value is \(0.01\cdot2 + 0.99\cdot1 = 1.01\).

Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) and UCT

If you have a model of the world (like the rules of Go), you can plan by growing a tree of possible futures. MCTS repeats four steps: select a promising branch, expand it, simulate a random playout, and back up the result. The selection score UCT balances a branch's win rate against how little it has been explored.

\[ \text{UCT} = \frac{w}{n} + c\sqrt{\frac{\ln N}{n}} \]
  • \(w, n\): wins and visits of this branch
  • \(N\): visits of the parent node
  • \(c\): exploration constant, often about 1.41
Worked example

A branch has won \(w = 6\) of \(n = 10\) visits, its parent has \(N = 100\), and \(c = 1.41\): \(\text{UCT} = 0.6 + 1.41\sqrt{\ln(100)/10} = 0.6 + 0.957 = 1.557\).

9.12 Multi-agent RL

When many learners share a world

With several agents, each one's environment includes the others, and the others keep changing as they learn (the world looks "non-stationary"). VDN handles cooperative teams by adding individual values: \(Q_{\text{tot}} = \sum_i Q_i\). QMIX generalizes this to a learned mixing network that must be monotonic (\(\partial Q_{\text{tot}}/\partial Q_i \ge 0\)), so each agent maximizing its own \(Q_i\) also maximizes the team value. MADDPG uses a centralized critic that sees everyone's actions during training, but each actor uses only its own observations when deployed. Example: two agents with \(Q_1 = 2\) and \(Q_2 = 3\) give a VDN team value of \(5\).

9.13 Imitation, inverse RL, offline RL, hierarchical RL and partial observability

Five more ideas in plain English

Imitation learning (behaviour cloning) is ordinary supervised learning: predict the expert's action from the state, with loss such as \(\|\pi(s) - a_{\text{expert}}\|^2\). Inverse RL instead infers the reward function that would explain the expert's behaviour.
