Offline RL learns from a fixed dataset with no new interaction, so it must avoid trusting actions that never appear in the data (usually with a penalty on unfamiliar actions). Hierarchical RL learns reusable sub-skills ("options") and a higher-level policy that chooses between them. In a POMDP the agent cannot see the true state, so it keeps a belief (a probability distribution over states) and updates it with each observation \(o\):

\[ b'(s') \propto O(o\mid s')\sum_s T(s'\mid s,a)\,b(s) \]
  • \(b(s)\): the belief: probability that the true state is \(s\)
  • \(T\): transition probability
  • \(O(o\mid s')\): chance of seeing observation \(o\) in state \(s'\)
Worked example

Two possible states with belief \([0.5, 0.5]\), and the world does not change (\(T\) is the identity). An observation is 0.8 likely in state 1 but 0.2 likely in state 2. Unnormalized: \([0.4, 0.1]\); after dividing by the sum 0.5 the new belief is \([0.8, 0.2]\).

In your vault

Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives/G01 to G14 and Q01 to Q03 cover Sections 9.6 to 9.13 with runnable code (bandits, MDPs and dynamic programming, Monte Carlo, TD, DQN, REINFORCE, actor-critic, PPO, DDPG, MCTS, multi-agent RL, imitation and inverse RL, offline and hierarchical RL, POMDPs).

9.14 SARSA, n-step returns, eligibility traces and importance sampling

Four refinements of value learning

SARSA is Q-learning’s cautious sibling. Q-learning (Section 9.2) updates toward the value of the best next action (off-policy: it learns about the greedy policy while behaving differently). SARSA updates toward the value of the action it actually takes next (on-policy), so it accounts for its own exploration. n-step returns look \(n\) rewards ahead before bootstrapping, blending Monte Carlo (\(n = \infty\)) with one-step TD (\(n = 1\)). Eligibility traces (TD(\(\lambda\))) do this smoothly by giving recently visited states a decaying credit \((\gamma\lambda)^{k}\). Importance sampling corrects for learning from data produced by a different policy by reweighting with the probability ratio.

\[ \text{SARSA: } Q(s,a) \leftarrow Q + \alpha\big[r + \gamma Q(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}) - Q\big] \qquad G_t^{(n)} = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\gamma^k r_{t+k} + \gamma^n V(s_{t+n}) \qquad \rho = \frac{\pi(a\mid s)}{b(a\mid s)} \]
  • \(a^{\prime}\): the next action actually chosen
  • \(\rho\): the importance ratio of the target policy \(\pi\) over the behaviour policy \(b\)
Worked example

Take reward \(r = 1\), \(\gamma = 0.9\). If the best next action has \(Q = 5\) but the action actually taken has \(Q = 2\): the Q-learning target is \(1 + 0.9\cdot5 = 5.5\) while the SARSA target is \(1 + 0.9\cdot2 = 2.8\). 3-step return with rewards \(1, 1, 1\) and \(V(s_{t+3}) = 5\): \(1 + 0.9 + 0.81 + 0.729\cdot5 = 6.355\). Trace: with \(\gamma\lambda = 0.9\times0.9 = 0.81\), a state visited 2 steps ago still receives \(0.81^2 = 0.656\) of the current error. Importance sampling: an action the target policy takes with probability 0.9 but the behaviour policy only took with probability 0.3 is weighted \(0.9/0.3 = 3\).

In your vault

G03 to G05 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives and Sutton and Barto (Section 18.11) cover this in full.

Part 10. More classical ML: clustering, dimensionality reduction and supervised extras

Part 6 covered the main classical algorithms. This part adds the rest of the Algorithm Codex's classical toolbox, again from scratch with small numbers.

10.1 Hierarchical clustering

Building a tree of clusters

k-means needs you to choose the number of clusters up front. Agglomerative hierarchical clustering starts with every point as its own cluster and repeatedly merges the two closest clusters, producing a tree (a dendrogram) you can cut at any height. "Closest" needs a rule for measuring the distance between two groups, called the linkage.

\[ d_{\text{single}}(A,B) = \min_{a\in A,\,b\in B} d(a,b) \quad d_{\text{complete}}(A,B) = \max_{a,b} d(a,b) \quad d_{\text{average}}(A,B) = \frac{1}{|A||B|}\sum_{a,b} d(a,b) \]
  • \(A, B\): two clusters of points
  • \(d(a,b)\): the ordinary distance between two individual points (Section 6.4)
Worked example

Clusters \(A = \{1, 2\}\) and \(B = \{6, 7\}\) on a number line. The pairwise distances are \(5, 6, 4, 5\). Single linkage \(= \min = 4\); complete linkage \(= \max = 6\); average linkage \(= (5+6+4+5)/4 = 5\).

10.2 DBSCAN: clusters from density

Core, border and noise points

DBSCAN finds clusters of any shape by looking for crowded regions, and it labels lonely points as noise instead of forcing them into a cluster. You choose a radius \(\varepsilon\) and a minimum neighbour count minPts. A point is a core point if at least minPts points (counting itself) lie within \(\varepsilon\). A border point is not core but lies within \(\varepsilon\) of a core point. Everything else is noise.

Worked example

Points \(1, 2, 3, 10\) on a line with \(\varepsilon = 1.5\) and minPts \(= 3\). Point 2 has neighbours \(\{1, 2, 3\}\), which is 3 points, so it is core. Points 1 and 3 each have only 2 neighbours but sit within \(\varepsilon\) of point 2, so they are border. Point 10 has only itself, so it is noise. The cluster is \(\{1,2,3\}\) and 10 is an outlier.

10.3 Gaussian Mixture Models and the EM algorithm

Soft clustering with bell curves

k-means makes a hard yes/no assignment. A Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) says the data came from several bell curves (Section 4.3) mixed together, and each point belongs to every curve with some probability. The EM algorithm alternates two steps: the E-step computes each curve's "responsibility" for each point, and the M-step re-fits each curve using those responsibilities as weights.

\[ \gamma_{ik} = \frac{\pi_k\,\mathcal{N}(x_i\mid\mu_k,\sigma_k^2)}{\sum_j \pi_j\,\mathcal{N}(x_i\mid\mu_j,\sigma_j^2)} \qquad \mu_k = \frac{\sum_i \gamma_{ik}\,x_i}{\sum_i \gamma_{ik}} \qquad \pi_k = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i \gamma_{ik} \]
  • \(\gamma_{ik}\): responsibility: the probability that point \(i\) came from curve \(k\)
  • \(\pi_k\): the mixing weight: how common curve \(k\) is
  • \(\mu_k, \sigma_k^2\): the mean and variance of curve \(k\)
Worked example

Two curves \(\mathcal{N}(0,1)\) and \(\mathcal{N}(4,1)\) with equal weights \(\pi = [0.5, 0.5]\). For the point \(x = 1\): the first density is 0.2420 and the second is 0.00443, so \(\gamma_1 = 0.2420/(0.2420 + 0.00443) = 0.982\). The point is 98.2% attributed to the first curve.

10.4 Choosing the number of clusters, and k-means++

Inertia, silhouette and smarter starts

Inertia (also called SSE) is the total squared distance from points to their cluster centre. It always falls as you add clusters, so you look for an "elbow". The silhouette score compares how close a point is to its own cluster with how close it is to the next nearest one. k-means++ picks starting centres well apart from each other by choosing each new centre with probability proportional to \(D(x)^2\), where \(D(x)\) is the distance to the nearest centre already chosen.

\[ \text{SSE} = \sum_k\sum_{x\in C_k}\|x-\mu_k\|^2 \qquad s = \frac{b - a}{\max(a,b)} \]
  • \(a\): average distance from the point to the other points in its own cluster
  • \(b\): average distance to the points of the nearest other cluster
  • \(s\): silhouette between \(-1\) and \(1\); near 1 means well clustered
Worked example

Clusters \(\{1,2\}\) and \(\{6,7\}\) with centres 1.5 and 6.5: SSE \(= 0.25 + 0.25 + 0.25 + 0.25 = 1.0\). For the point 1: \(a = 1\) (distance to 2) and \(b = (5+6)/2 = 5.5\), so \(s = (5.5 - 1)/5.5 = 0.818\), a good fit.

10.5 PCA in numbers, and ICA

PCA: projecting and measuring variance kept

From Section 6.8, PCA finds the directions of greatest spread. To use it you (1) centre the data, (2) find the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of the covariance matrix, and (3) project each point onto the top few eigenvectors. Each eigenvalue is the variance captured along its direction.

\[ z = u^{T}x \qquad \text{explained variance ratio}_k = \frac{\lambda_k}{\sum_j \lambda_j} \]
  • \(u\): a unit-length eigenvector (a new axis)
  • \(z\): the new coordinate of the point along that axis
  • \(\lambda_k\): the eigenvalue for component \(k\)
Worked example

Eigenvalues \(4\) and \(1\) mean the first component keeps \(4/5 = 80\%\) of the variance. Projecting the point \(x = [3, 4]\) onto the unit vector \(u = [0.6, 0.8]\) gives \(z = 0.6\cdot3 + 0.8\cdot4 = 5.0\) (this is just a dot product, Section 2.1.2).

ICA: unmixing signals

The "cocktail party" problem: two microphones each record a mixture of two speakers. Independent Component Analysis finds an unmixing matrix \(W\) that recovers the original independent sources. It works by making the recovered signals as non-Gaussian (as un-bell-curved) as possible, because mixtures tend to look more Gaussian than their sources.

\[ x = A s \qquad \hat{s} = W x, \ W \approx A^{-1} \]
  • \(s\): the hidden original sources
  • \(A\): the unknown mixing matrix
  • \(x\): the recordings we can actually observe
Worked example

Take \(A = \begin{bmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{bmatrix}\) and sources \(s = [2, 1]\), so the recordings are \(x = As = [3, 1]\). The unmixing matrix is \(W = A^{-1} = \begin{bmatrix}0.5&0.5\\0.5&-0.5\end{bmatrix}\), and \(Wx = [0.5\cdot3 + 0.5\cdot1,\ 0.5\cdot3 - 0.5\cdot1] = [2, 1]\). The sources are recovered. (Real ICA must discover \(W\) without knowing \(A\).)

10.6 t-SNE, UMAP, Isomap and LLE: drawing high-dimensional data in 2D

t-SNE and UMAP

To look at data with hundreds of features, we squash it to 2D while keeping neighbours together. t-SNE turns distances into probabilities in both the original space (\(p_{ij}\), with bell curves) and the 2D map (\(q_{ij}\), with a heavier-tailed Student-t curve), then moves the 2D points until the two match, by minimizing the KL divergence from Section 4.6. UMAP builds a weighted neighbour graph and does a similar matching with a cross-entropy loss; it is usually faster and tends to keep more of the global layout.

\[ q_{ij} \propto \frac{1}{1 + \|y_i - y_j\|^2} \qquad \mathcal{L} = \text{KL}(P\,\|\,Q) = \sum_{i\ne j} p_{ij}\ln\frac{p_{ij}}{q_{ij}} \]
  • \(y_i\): the 2D position of point \(i\) in the map
  • \(p_{ij}\): similarity of points \(i, j\) in the original space
Worked example

The unnormalized 2D similarity is \(1/(1+d^2)\): for map distance 1 it is 0.5, for distance 2 it is 0.2. Nearby map points get high similarity, far ones low, but it falls off slowly (heavy tail), which lets separate clusters spread apart.

Why it matters

Distances between t-SNE clusters and their sizes on the map are not reliable, so use it for exploring, not for measuring.

Isomap and LLE: manifold learning

Data often lies on a curved sheet (a manifold) inside a high-dimensional space. Straight-line distance can cut through empty space. Isomap instead measures the geodesic distance, travelling along a neighbour graph, then unrolls the sheet. LLE rebuilds each point from its neighbours, \(x_i \approx \sum_j w_{ij}x_j\), and finds low-dimensional points that keep the same weights. Example: along a curved path A to B is 1 and B to C is 1, so the geodesic distance A to C is 2, even though the straight line through empty space might be only 1.4.

10.7 Association rules (market-basket analysis)

Support, confidence and lift

Which items are bought together? For a rule "bread \(\Rightarrow\) milk", support is how often both appear, confidence is how often milk appears among baskets with bread, and lift compares that to how common milk is on its own (above 1 means a genuine link).

\[ \text{support}(A\Rightarrow B) = P(A\cap B) \quad \text{confidence} = \frac{P(A\cap B)}{P(A)} \quad \text{lift} = \frac{\text{confidence}}{P(B)} \]
Worked example

Out of 5 baskets, bread appears in 4, milk in 4, and both in 3. Support \(= 3/5 = 0.6\). Confidence \(= 3/4 = 0.75\). Lift \(= 0.75/(4/5) = 0.9375\), slightly below 1, so bread does not really boost milk purchases.

10.8 Discriminant analysis (LDA and QDA)

Classify by which bell curve fits best

Linear Discriminant Analysis assumes each class is a bell curve with the same spread, so the boundary between classes is a straight line. It scores each class with a discriminant \(\delta_k\) and picks the largest. QDA lets each class have its own spread, giving curved boundaries. In one dimension:

\[ \delta_k(x) = \frac{x\,\mu_k}{\sigma^2} - \frac{\mu_k^2}{2\sigma^2} + \ln\pi_k \]
  • \(\mu_k\): the mean of class \(k\)
  • \(\sigma^2\): the shared variance
  • \(\pi_k\): the prior probability of class \(k\)
Worked example

Class 0 has \(\mu_0 = 0\), class 1 has \(\mu_1 = 2\), \(\sigma^2 = 1\), equal priors. The two scores tie when \(x = (\mu_0 + \mu_1)/2 = 1\), so the decision boundary sits exactly halfway at \(x = 1\).

10.9 Generalized linear models (GLMs)

One recipe for many kinds of target

Linear regression predicts any real number, but counts (0, 1, 2, ...) and probabilities need special care. A GLM predicts a linear score \(\eta = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x\) and passes it through a link function that keeps the output sensible. Logistic regression (Section 6.2) is a GLM with the logit link. Poisson regression models counts with a log link.

\[ \text{Poisson: } \ \ln\lambda = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x \ \Rightarrow\ \lambda = e^{\beta_0 + \beta_1 x} \]
  • \(\lambda\): the expected count (always positive thanks to \(e^{\cdot}\), Section 1.4)
Worked example

With \(\beta_0 = 0.5\), \(\beta_1 = 0.3\) and \(x = 2\): \(\lambda = e^{0.5 + 0.6} = e^{1.1} = 3.00\) expected events.

10.10 Probability calibration

Do "80% sure" predictions come true 80% of the time?

A model can rank examples well yet give badly scaled probabilities. A calibrated model's 80% predictions are right about 80% of the time. The Brier score measures probability quality (lower is better), and temperature scaling rescales the logits, \(p = \sigma(z/T)\), to fix over-confidence (\(T > 1\) softens predictions, as in Section 8.7).

\[ \text{Brier} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i (p_i - y_i)^2 \]
Worked example

Predictions \(p = [0.9, 0.2]\) for true outcomes \(y = [1, 0]\): Brier \(= (0.01 + 0.04)/2 = 0.025\), very good.

10.11 Boosting and stacking in more detail

AdaBoost: reweighting the mistakes

AdaBoost trains simple models one after another. After each round it increases the weight of the examples the model got wrong, so the next model focuses on them, and it gives each model a vote proportional to how accurate it was.

\[ \alpha_m = \frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{1 - \epsilon_m}{\epsilon_m} \qquad w_i \leftarrow w_i\,e^{\pm\alpha_m} \]
  • \(\epsilon_m\): the weighted error rate of model \(m\)
  • \(\alpha_m\): its voting weight
  • \(\pm\): plus for misclassified examples, minus for correct ones
Worked example

A model with error \(\epsilon = 0.2\) gets \(\alpha = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(0.8/0.2) = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln 4 = 0.693\). Misclassified examples have their weight multiplied by \(e^{0.693} = 2\); correct ones by \(e^{-0.693} = 0.5\).

Stacking trains several different models (say a tree, a kNN and a logistic regression) and then trains one more "meta-model" that takes their predictions as inputs and learns how much to trust each. Blending is the simpler version using a hold-out set.

In your vault

B07, B08, B09, C02 to C10 and D02, D04 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives cover this whole part in full depth.

Part 11. Probabilistic models and inference

These models treat everything as a probability distribution and use Bayes' theorem (Section 4.4) repeatedly. If Section 4.4 felt hard, re-read it first.

11.1 Bayesian networks

Factorizing a big probability into small pieces

A Bayesian network is a diagram where arrows show what directly influences what. It lets you write a huge joint probability as a product of small conditional ones, and each variable only needs to know its parents. To get the probability of one variable alone, you add up over the others ("marginalize").

\[ P(x_1,\dots,x_n) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} P\big(x_i \mid \text{parents}(x_i)\big) \]
Worked example

Rain (R) may cause the sprinkler (S) to be off. Let \(P(R) = 0.2\), \(P(S\mid R) = 0.1\), \(P(S\mid\neg R) = 0.5\). Then \(P(S) = P(R)P(S\mid R) + P(\neg R)P(S\mid\neg R) = 0.2\cdot0.1 + 0.8\cdot0.5 = 0.02 + 0.40 = 0.42\).

11.2 Hidden Markov Models (HMMs)

A hidden state you can only guess from clues

An HMM has hidden states that change step by step (for example "rainy" or "sunny") and visible observations produced by each state (for example "umbrella" or "no umbrella"). It is defined by starting probabilities \(\pi\), transition probabilities \(A_{ij}\) between states, and emission probabilities \(B_j(o)\). The forward algorithm computes the probability of an observation sequence by summing over all hidden paths efficiently; Viterbi is the same with a max instead of a sum, giving the single most likely hidden path.

\[ \alpha_1(j) = \pi_j\,B_j(o_1) \qquad \alpha_t(j) = \Big[\sum_i \alpha_{t-1}(i)\,A_{ij}\Big]\,B_j(o_t) \]
  • \(\alpha_t(j)\): probability of the observations so far and being in state \(j\) at time \(t\)
Worked example

Two states. Start \(\pi = [0.6, 0.4]\); transitions \(A = \begin{bmatrix}0.7&0.3\\0.4&0.6\end{bmatrix}\); emissions \(B = \begin{bmatrix}0.9&0.1\\0.2&0.8\end{bmatrix}\) (row = state, column = observation). Observations \([0, 1]\). Step 1: \(\alpha_1 = [0.6\cdot0.9,\ 0.4\cdot0.2] = [0.54,\ 0.08]\). Step 2, state 0: \((0.54\cdot0.7 + 0.08\cdot0.4)\cdot0.1 = 0.41\cdot0.1 = 0.041\). State 1: \((0.54\cdot0.3 + 0.08\cdot0.6)\cdot0.8 = 0.21\cdot0.8 = 0.168\). The probability of the observations is \(0.041 + 0.168 = 0.209\).

11.3 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)

Sampling from a distribution you cannot write down

Sometimes you know a probability only up to a scaling constant (like the unknown \(Z\) in Section 15.13). Metropolis-Hastings still lets you draw samples from it: propose a small random move, and accept it with a probability that always accepts improvements and sometimes accepts worse moves. After many steps, the visited points follow the target distribution.

\[ P(\text{accept}) = \min\!\left(1,\ \frac{p(x')}{p(x)}\right) \]
  • \(x\): the current point
  • \(x'\): the proposed new point
  • \(p\): the (unnormalized) target probability
Worked example

If the proposed point is half as likely as the current one, \(p(x')/p(x) = 0.5\), you accept the move with probability 0.5 (flip a fair coin). If it were twice as likely, the ratio is 2 and \(\min(1, 2) = 1\), so you always accept.

11.4 Variational inference and the ELBO

Approximating a hard posterior with an easy one

Exact Bayesian inference is often impossible, so variational inference picks a simple family of distributions \(q(z)\) and tunes it to be as close as possible to the true posterior \(p(z\mid x)\). Directly measuring closeness is impossible, but there is an identity that turns it into something computable, the ELBO (Evidence Lower BOund). Maximizing the ELBO shrinks the KL gap. This is exactly the objective of the VAE in Section 7.4.

\[ \ln p(x) = \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_q[\ln p(x,z)] - \mathbb{E}_q[\ln q(z)]}_{\text{ELBO}} + \text{KL}\big(q(z)\,\|\,p(z\mid x)\big) \]
Worked example

If \(\ln p(x) = -3.0\) and the KL gap is 0.4, then ELBO \(= -3.4\). Because KL is never negative, the ELBO can never exceed \(\ln p(x)\); improving the ELBO squeezes the gap toward 0.

11.5 Gaussian processes

A distribution over whole functions

A Gaussian process (GP) says: nearby inputs should have similar outputs, and it uses a kernel to define "nearby". Given a few observed points, it predicts both a mean and an uncertainty at every new input. The common RBF kernel is:

\[ k(x,x') = \exp\!\left(-\frac{(x - x')^2}{2\ell^2}\right) \qquad \mu_* = k_*^{T}K^{-1}y \qquad \sigma_*^2 = k(x_*,x_*) - k_*^{T}K^{-1}k_* \]
  • \(\ell\): the length scale: how far apart two inputs can be and still be similar
  • \(K\): the matrix of kernel values between all training inputs
  • \(k_*\): kernel values between the new input and each training input
Worked example

One noise-free training point at \(x = 0\) with value \(y = 2\), \(\ell = 1\). Predict at \(x_* = 1\): \(k_* = e^{-1/2} = 0.6065\) and \(K = 1\). Mean \(= 0.6065 \times 2 = 1.213\). Variance \(= 1 - 0.6065^2 = 0.632\). Far from data the variance grows back toward 1, so the GP says "I am not sure here".

11.6 Kalman filters and particle filters

Tracking something from noisy measurements

A Kalman filter blends a prediction of where a thing is with a noisy measurement, trusting each in proportion to how certain it is. The Kalman gain \(K\) is that trust weight. (Scalar version below.)

\[ \text{predict: } \ P^- = P + Q \qquad K = \frac{P^-}{P^- + R} \qquad \hat{x} = \hat{x}^- + K(z - \hat{x}^-) \qquad P = (1-K)P^- \]
  • \(\hat{x}\): the estimate
  • \(P\): its uncertainty (variance)
  • \(Q\): how much the world itself drifts each step
  • \(R\): how noisy the sensor is
  • \(z\): the new measurement
Worked example

Prior estimate \(\hat{x}^- = 0\) with variance \(P^- = 1\), sensor noise \(R = 1\), and measurement \(z = 1\). Then \(K = 1/(1+1) = 0.5\), the new estimate is \(0 + 0.5(1 - 0) = 0.5\), and the new variance is \((1 - 0.5)\cdot1 = 0.5\). Equal trust, so the answer is halfway, and we are now more certain.

When the world is too messy for a bell curve, a particle filter tracks many random guesses ("particles"), gives each a weight by how well it explains the measurement, then resamples. The effective sample size \(N_{\text{eff}} = 1/\sum_i w_i^2\) tells you when the weights have collapsed onto just a few particles: with weights \([0.1, 0.6, 0.3]\), \(N_{\text{eff}} = 1/(0.01 + 0.36 + 0.09) = 2.17\) out of 3. The Extended and Unscented Kalman filters (Section 17.5) handle curved, non-linear motion.

11.7 Conditional random fields and topic models

CRFs: scoring whole label sequences

An HMM models how data is generated. A Conditional Random Field (CRF) instead models the probability of a whole label sequence given the input directly (a discriminative model), which is why CRFs are good at tagging words as names, places, and so on. Each candidate sequence gets a score, and the scores are turned into probabilities with a softmax-style normalizer \(Z(x)\):

\[ p(y\mid x) = \frac{\exp(\text{score}(x, y))}{Z(x)} \]
Worked example

Two candidate label sequences with scores 2 and 1: \(p = e^{2}/(e^{2} + e^{1}) = 0.731\) for the first and \(0.269\) for the second, exactly the sigmoid shape of Section 5.2.1.

LDA: topic models

Latent Dirichlet Allocation imagines each document as a mix of hidden topics, and each topic as a mix of words. The probability of a word in a document adds up over topics. (Not to be confused with the classifier LDA in Section 10.8.)

\[ p(w\mid d) = \sum_k \theta_{dk}\,\phi_{kw} \]
  • \(\theta_{dk}\): how much of document \(d\) is about topic \(k\)
  • \(\phi_{kw}\): how likely word \(w\) is within topic \(k\)
Worked example

A document is 70% topic 1 and 30% topic 2. The word "goal" has probability 0.1 in topic 1 (say, cooking) and 0.5 in topic 2 (say, sport). Then \(p(\text{goal}\mid d) = 0.7\cdot0.1 + 0.3\cdot0.5 = 0.07 + 0.15 = 0.22\).

11.8 Bayesian optimization

Tuning expensive experiments with a smart guess

If each experiment is very costly (training a big model), you cannot try thousands of settings. Bayesian optimization fits a Gaussian process (Section 11.5) to the results so far, then chooses the next setting using an acquisition function that balances a high predicted value against high uncertainty. A simple one is the upper confidence bound:

\[ \text{UCB}(x) = \mu(x) + \kappa\,\sigma(x) \]
  • \(\mu(x)\): the GP's predicted mean at setting \(x\)
  • \(\sigma(x)\): the GP's uncertainty there
  • \(\kappa\): how much you value exploring
Worked example

A setting with \(\mu = 1\), \(\sigma = 0.5\) and \(\kappa = 2\) scores \(1 + 2(0.5) = 2.0\). A setting with a lower mean but much higher uncertainty could beat it, which is how the method explores.

In your vault

E01 to E09 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives cover Sections 11.1 to 11.8 in full.

11.9 Gibbs sampling and belief propagation

Two more ways to reason in graphical models

Gibbs sampling is MCMC (Section 11.3) for several variables at once: update one variable at a time, drawing its new value from its distribution given the current values of all the others. Cycling through the variables many times produces samples from the joint distribution. Belief propagation computes the exact marginal distribution of each variable in a tree-shaped graph by passing "messages" between neighbours: each message summarizes everything one side of the graph knows. A variable’s belief is the product of its own evidence and all incoming messages, then normalized.

\[ x_j \sim p\big(x_j \mid x_{\setminus j}\big) \qquad \text{belief}(x_i) \propto \phi_i(x_i)\prod_{k\in\text{nbrs}(i)} m_{k\to i}(x_i) \]
  • \(x_{\setminus j}\): all variables except \(x_j\)
  • \(\phi_i\): the local evidence for variable \(i\)
  • \(m_{k\to i}\): the message from neighbour \(k\)
Worked example

Gibbs: two binary variables are positively linked, with \(p(x_2 = 1\mid x_1 = 1) = 0.8\). Given \(x_1 = 1\), draw \(x_2\) equal to 1 with probability 0.8, then redraw \(x_1\) from its conditional given the new \(x_2\), and repeat. Belief propagation: a variable has local evidence \([0.6, 0.4]\) and receives one incoming message \([0.95, 0.55]\) from its neighbour. The unnormalized belief is \([0.6\cdot0.95,\ 0.4\cdot0.55] = [0.57,\ 0.22]\), which normalizes to \([0.72,\ 0.28]\).

Part 12. Time series and survival analysis

Data that arrives in time order (prices, temperatures, sales) cannot be shuffled like ordinary data, because order carries information. This part covers the classical tools for forecasting, plus the tools for "how long until something happens?".

12.1 Decomposition and smoothing

Trend, seasonality and noise; exponential smoothing

A time series is often split into a slow trend \(T_t\), a repeating seasonal pattern \(S_t\) (weekly, yearly) and leftover random noise \(\varepsilon_t\): \(y_t = T_t + S_t + \varepsilon_t\). To smooth out noise, exponential smoothing keeps a running average that weights recent values more than old ones.

\[ s_t = \alpha\,y_t + (1-\alpha)\,s_{t-1} \]
  • \(y_t\): the observed value at time \(t\)
  • \(s_t\): the smoothed value
  • \(\alpha\): between 0 and 1: near 1 follows the data closely, near 0 smooths heavily
Worked example

Data \(y = [10, 12, 14]\), \(\alpha = 0.5\), starting with \(s_1 = 10\). Then \(s_2 = 0.5\cdot12 + 0.5\cdot10 = 11\) and \(s_3 = 0.5\cdot14 + 0.5\cdot11 = 12.5\). The smoothed line lags behind the rising data but is less jumpy.

12.2 AR, MA and ARIMA

Predicting from your own past

An autoregressive AR(1) model predicts the next value from the previous one; it is stable ("stationary") when \(|\phi| \lt 1\). A moving-average MA model predicts from recent random shocks. ARIMA(p, d, q) combines \(p\) AR terms, \(q\) MA terms, and \(d\) rounds of differencing (replacing the series by its step-to-step changes, which removes a trend). SARIMA adds the same idea at the seasonal lag.

\[ \text{AR(1): } y_t = c + \phi\,y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t \qquad \text{differencing: } y'_t = y_t - y_{t-1} \]
  • \(\phi\): how strongly the past value carries into the present
  • \(c\): a constant
  • \(\varepsilon_t\): a random shock
Worked example

Differencing the trending series \([10, 12, 15, 19]\) gives \([2, 3, 4]\), which is steadier. For an AR(1) with \(c = 0\), \(\phi = 0.8\) and last value \(10\), the forecast is \(0.8 \times 10 = 8\); the next after that is \(6.4\), decaying toward 0.

12.3 Volatility: ARCH and GARCH

When the size of the swings changes over time

In finance, calm periods and turbulent periods cluster together. GARCH models the variance of tomorrow's shock as a base level plus a reaction to yesterday's surprise plus a memory of yesterday's variance.

\[ \sigma_t^2 = \omega + \alpha\,\varepsilon_{t-1}^2 + \beta\,\sigma_{t-1}^2 \]
  • \(\sigma_t^2\): today's predicted variance
  • \(\varepsilon_{t-1}\): yesterday's surprise (shock)
  • \(\omega, \alpha, \beta\): model constants, with \(\alpha + \beta \lt 1\) for stability
Worked example

With \(\omega = 0.1\), \(\alpha = 0.1\), \(\beta = 0.8\), yesterday's shock \(\varepsilon = 2\) and yesterday's variance \(1\): \(\sigma^2 = 0.1 + 0.1\cdot4 + 0.8\cdot1 = 1.3\). A big surprise raised the predicted variance from 1 to 1.3.

12.4 Survival analysis

Time until an event, even when some people have not had it yet

Survival analysis studies how long until an event (a customer leaves, a machine breaks). Its key difficulty is censoring: some subjects are still "alive" when the study ends, so you know only that their time is at least so long. The survival function \(S(t) = P(T > t)\) is the chance of lasting past time \(t\). The Kaplan-Meier estimator builds it step by step, and the Cox model lets features scale the risk.

\[ \hat{S}(t) = \prod_{t_i \le t}\left(1 - \frac{d_i}{n_i}\right) \qquad h(t\mid x) = h_0(t)\,e^{\beta x} \]
  • \(d_i\): number of events at time \(t_i\)
  • \(n_i\): number of subjects still at risk just before \(t_i\)
  • \(h(t)\): the hazard: the instantaneous risk at time \(t\)
  • \(e^{\beta x}\): a multiplier on the baseline risk
Worked example

Ten subjects. At time \(t_1\), one has the event: \(S = 1 - 1/10 = 0.9\). At \(t_2\), nine remain and one more has the event: \(S = 0.9 \times (1 - 1/9) = 0.9 \times 8/9 = 0.8\). In a Cox model with \(\beta = 0.7\), a feature value \(x = 1\) multiplies the risk by \(e^{0.7} = 2.01\), i.e. about double.
