F01 to F04 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives cover this part in full.

Part 13. Optimization and numerical methods

Training a model is an optimization problem, and everything on a computer is really numerical approximation. This part covers the mathematics of finding minima faster than plain gradient descent, and of computing things reliably.

13.1 Newton's method and BFGS

Using curvature, not just slope

Gradient descent uses only the slope, so it takes cautious steps. Newton's method also uses the curvature (the second derivative, the "Hessian" in many dimensions) to jump straight toward the minimum. The same idea finds where a function equals zero (root finding). BFGS avoids computing the expensive Hessian by learning a curvature estimate from how the gradient changed between steps.

\[ x_{k+1} = x_k - \frac{f'(x_k)}{f''(x_k)} \ \ (\text{minimize}) \qquad x_{k+1} = x_k - \frac{f(x_k)}{f'(x_k)} \ \ (\text{root}) \qquad B\,s = y \]
  • \(f''\): second derivative (how quickly the slope itself changes)
  • \(s\): the last step \(x_{k+1} - x_k\)
  • \(y\): the change in gradient over that step
  • \(B\): BFGS's running estimate of curvature
Worked example

Minimize \(f(x) = (x-3)^2\) starting at \(x_0 = 0\): \(f'= 2(x-3) = -6\), \(f''= 2\), so \(x_1 = 0 - (-6)/2 = 3\), the exact minimum in one step. Root finding for \(f(x) = x^2 - 2\) from \(x_0 = 1\): \(x_1 = 1 - (-1)/2 = 1.5\), \(x_2 = 1.5 - 0.25/3 = 1.4167\), already close to \(\sqrt{2} = 1.4142\). For BFGS in one dimension, a step \(s = 1\) that changed the gradient by \(y = 2\) gives curvature estimate \(B = 2\).

13.2 Convexity, Lagrange multipliers, KKT and duality

Optimizing with rules

A function is convex if the straight line between any two points on its graph never dips below the graph (like a bowl). For a convex problem, any local minimum is the global minimum, which is why gradient descent is reliable there. To minimize while obeying constraints, a Lagrange multiplier \(\lambda\) prices each constraint and folds it into one function called the Lagrangian. The KKT conditions are the rules that the best answer must satisfy; they are the maths behind the SVM in Section 6.6.

\[ f(\lambda x + (1-\lambda)y) \le \lambda f(x) + (1-\lambda)f(y) \qquad \mathcal{L}(x,\lambda) = f(x) + \lambda\,g(x) \]
  • \(g(x)\): the constraint, written so that \(g(x) = 0\) (or \(\le 0\)) is required
  • \(\lambda\): the multiplier: how costly the constraint is
Worked example

Convex check: \(f(x) = x^2\) with \(x = 0\), \(y = 2\), weight 0.5: \(f(1) = 1 \le 0.5\cdot0 + 0.5\cdot4 = 2\), true. Constrained minimum: minimize \(x^2 + y^2\) subject to \(x + y = 1\). Setting the derivatives of \(x^2 + y^2 + \lambda(x + y - 1)\) to zero gives \(2x + \lambda = 0\) and \(2y + \lambda = 0\), so \(x = y = 0.5\), \(\lambda = -1\), and the minimum value is \(0.25 + 0.25 = 0.5\).

Why it matters

The four KKT conditions for inequality constraints are: stationarity (the gradient of the Lagrangian is zero), primal feasibility (the constraints hold), dual feasibility (\(\lambda \ge 0\)) and complementary slackness (\(\lambda\,g(x) = 0\): either the constraint is tight or its price is zero).

Duality

Every constrained problem (the "primal") has a mirror problem (the "dual") that gives a lower bound on the answer. For convex problems the two often meet exactly ("strong duality"). Example: minimize \(x^2\) subject to \(x \ge 1\). The primal answer is 1 (at \(x = 1\)). The Lagrangian is \(x^2 + \lambda(1 - x)\); minimizing over \(x\) gives \(x = \lambda/2\) and the dual function \(g(\lambda) = \lambda - \lambda^2/4\). Its maximum is at \(\lambda = 2\), where \(g = 2 - 1 = 1\), the same value.

13.3 Linear programming and the simplex method

Best answer when everything is a straight line

A linear program maximizes or minimizes a linear objective subject to linear constraints. The feasible region is a many-sided shape, and the best answer always sits at one of its corners. The simplex method walks from corner to adjacent corner, always improving, until no neighbour is better.

Worked example

Maximize \(3x + 2y\) subject to \(x + y \le 4\), \(x \le 2\), \(x, y \ge 0\). The corners are \((0,0)\), \((2,0)\), \((2,2)\) and \((0,4)\). The objective values are \(0, 6, 10, 8\), so the best is 10 at \((2, 2)\).

13.4 Numerical linear algebra: LU and QR

Solving \(Ax = b\) reliably

Rather than computing an inverse (slow and numerically fragile), computers factor the matrix. LU decomposition writes \(A = LU\) with \(L\) lower-triangular and \(U\) upper-triangular, so solving becomes two easy passes: first \(Ly = b\), then \(Ux = y\). QR decomposition writes \(A = QR\) with \(Q\) orthogonal (\(Q^{T}Q = I\)) and \(R\) upper-triangular. The QR algorithm for eigenvalues repeatedly factors and recombines, \(A_{k+1} = R_kQ_k\), until the matrix becomes triangular with the eigenvalues (Section 2.2.4) on its diagonal.

\[ A = LU \qquad Ly = b \qquad Ux = y \]
Worked example

\(A = \begin{bmatrix}2&1\\4&3\end{bmatrix}\) factors as \(L = \begin{bmatrix}1&0\\2&1\end{bmatrix}\), \(U = \begin{bmatrix}2&1\\0&1\end{bmatrix}\) (multiply to check). Solve \(Ax = b\) for \(b = [3, 7]\): from \(Ly = b\), \(y_1 = 3\) and \(y_2 = 7 - 2\cdot3 = 1\). From \(Ux = y\), \(x_2 = 1\) and \(x_1 = (3 - 1)/2 = 1\). So \(x = [1, 1]\).

13.5 Root finding and numerical integration

Bisection, trapezoid and Simpson

Bisection finds a zero by repeatedly halving an interval where the function changes sign: slow but guaranteed. To compute an integral (area under a curve) numerically, the trapezoid rule replaces the curve with straight segments and Simpson's rule with parabolas.

\[ \int_a^b f \approx \frac{h}{2}\big[f_0 + 2f_1 + \dots + f_n\big] \ \ (\text{trapezoid}) \qquad \int_a^b f \approx \frac{h}{3}\big[f_0 + 4f_1 + f_2\big] \ \ (\text{Simpson, 2 intervals}) \]
  • \(h\): the width of each interval
  • \(f_i\): the function value at the \(i\)-th point
Worked example

Bisection for \(x^2 - 2 = 0\) on \([1, 2]\): the midpoint 1.5 gives \(0.25 > 0\), so the root lies in \([1, 1.5]\); halve again. Integration of \(x^2\) from 0 to 2 (true value \(8/3 = 2.667\)) with \(h = 1\): trapezoid \(= \tfrac{1}{2}(0 + 2\cdot1 + 4) = 3.0\); Simpson \(= \tfrac{1}{3}(0 + 4\cdot1 + 4) = 2.667\), exact here.

13.6 Natural gradient and information geometry

Steps that respect how sensitive each direction is

Ordinary gradient descent treats every parameter direction equally, but changing some parameters alters the model's predictions far more than others. The natural gradient rescales the step by the inverse of the Fisher information matrix \(F\) (a measure of how sensitive the predictions are to each parameter), so steps are equal in prediction space rather than parameter space.

\[ \theta \leftarrow \theta - \alpha\,F^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{L} \]
  • \(F\): the Fisher information matrix
  • \(F^{-1}\): its inverse (Section 2.2.3)
Worked example

With \(F = \text{diag}(2, 0.5)\) and gradient \(\nabla\mathcal{L} = [1, 1]\): \(F^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{L} = [1/2,\ 1/0.5] = [0.5,\ 2]\). The insensitive direction (small \(F\)) takes a four-times larger step. Adam (Section 5.7) can be seen as a cheap approximation of this idea.

13.7 Automatic differentiation: dual numbers and expression trees

How computers take exact derivatives

Forward-mode autodiff uses dual numbers: numbers of the form \(a + b\varepsilon\) where \(\varepsilon^2 = 0\). Arithmetic on them carries the derivative along automatically. Reverse-mode autodiff (what PyTorch does for backpropagation) records every operation on a "tape" during the forward pass, then walks it backwards applying the chain rule (Section 3.5). Symbolic systems instead represent a formula as a tree and rewrite it using calculus rules and simplifications.

\[ (a + b\varepsilon)(c + d\varepsilon) = ac + (ad + bc)\,\varepsilon \]
  • \(\varepsilon\): a tiny "infinitesimal" with \(\varepsilon^2 = 0\); the coefficient of \(\varepsilon\) is the derivative
Worked example

To differentiate \(f(x) = x^2\) at \(x = 3\), compute with \(x = 3 + 1\varepsilon\): \((3 + \varepsilon)^2 = 9 + 6\varepsilon\). The number multiplying \(\varepsilon\) is \(6 = f'(3) = 2\cdot3\), an exact derivative with no formula lookup. Forward mode is cheap when there are few inputs; reverse mode is cheap when there are many inputs and one output, which is the situation in neural network training.

In your vault

I02 to I05, N01 to N03, O03 and V01 to V05 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives cover this whole part with runnable code.

Part 14. Modern learning paradigms

Ordinary supervised learning assumes plenty of labeled data in one place, for one task, that never changes. Real life breaks each of those assumptions, and each break has produced a family of methods.

14.1 Self-supervised and contrastive learning

Learning without labels by comparing

In self-supervised learning the data creates its own labels (for example, hide a word and predict it). Contrastive learning teaches a model that two views of the same thing (two crops of one photo, or an image and its caption, as in CLIP) should have similar embeddings, while different things should not. The InfoNCE loss is a softmax that must pick the correct partner out of many wrong ones:

\[ \mathcal{L} = -\ln\frac{\exp(s^{+}/\tau)}{\exp(s^{+}/\tau) + \sum_{j}\exp(s^{-}_j/\tau)} \]
  • \(s^{+}\): similarity (e.g. cosine, Section 2.1.3) between the matching pair
  • \(s^{-}_j\): similarities to non-matching examples
  • \(\tau\): a temperature (Section 8.7): small makes the softmax sharper
Worked example

Positive similarity 0.9, two negatives with similarities 0.1 and 0.2, \(\tau = 0.1\). The exponentials are \(e^{9} = 8103.1\), \(e^{1} = 2.718\), \(e^{2} = 7.389\). Probability of the right partner \(= 8103.1/(8103.1 + 2.718 + 7.389) = 0.99875\), so the loss is \(-\ln 0.99875 = 0.00125\), tiny because the model is already very sure.

14.2 Semi-supervised learning

A few labels and a lot of unlabeled data

Labels are expensive but raw data is cheap. Pseudo-labeling lets the model label unlabeled examples itself and train on the ones it is very confident about (say probability above 0.95). Consistency regularization punishes the model if a slightly altered copy of an input (cropped, noised) gets a different prediction: \(\|f(x) - f(\text{aug}(x))\|^2\).

Worked example

The prediction for an image is \([0.9, 0.1]\) but for its augmented copy is \([0.7, 0.3]\). The consistency penalty is \((0.9-0.7)^2 + (0.1-0.3)^2 = 0.04 + 0.04 = 0.08\). Training reduces it, so the model becomes robust to such changes without needing any label.

14.3 Meta-learning (MAML): learning to learn

A starting point that adapts in a few steps

MAML searches for initial weights \(\theta\) such that after just one or two gradient steps on a brand-new task, the model already does well. Each task takes an inner step, then the outer loop improves \(\theta\) based on how well the adapted weights did.

\[ \theta' = \theta - \alpha\,\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}_{\text{task}}(\theta) \qquad \min_\theta \sum_{\text{tasks}} \mathcal{L}_{\text{task}}(\theta') \]
  • \(\alpha\): the inner-loop learning rate
  • \(\theta'\): the weights after adapting to one task
Worked example

A task has loss \(\mathcal{L} = (\theta - a)^2\) with \(a = 2\). Starting at \(\theta = 0\), the loss is 4. The gradient is \(2(\theta - a) = -4\); with \(\alpha = 0.25\), \(\theta' = 0 + 0.25\cdot4 = 1.0\), and the loss is now \((1 - 2)^2 = 1\). One step cut the loss from 4 to 1.

14.4 Federated learning

Training without collecting anyone's data

In federated learning the data stays on each phone or hospital. Every client trains locally and sends back only the updated weights, which a server combines with FedAvg, a weighted average that gives clients with more data more say.

\[ w = \sum_{k} \frac{n_k}{n}\,w_k \]
  • \(w_k\): client \(k\)'s weights
  • \(n_k\): how many examples client \(k\) has
  • \(n\): total examples across clients
Worked example

Client 1 has \(n_1 = 100\) examples and weight \(w_1 = 1\); client 2 has \(n_2 = 300\) and \(w_2 = 2\). Then \(w = \tfrac{100}{400}(1) + \tfrac{300}{400}(2) = 0.25 + 1.5 = 1.75\).

14.5 Online learning and concept drift

Learning as data arrives, while the world changes

In online learning the model must predict one example at a time and is scored immediately. Its quality is measured by regret: how much worse it did than the best fixed expert in hindsight. The Hedge algorithm keeps a weight for each expert and shrinks the weight of experts that make mistakes. Concept drift means the data's pattern itself shifts over time (customer tastes change), so old knowledge must be down-weighted or refreshed.

\[ \text{Regret}_T = \sum_{t=1}^{T}\ell_t - \min_i\sum_{t=1}^{T}\ell_{i,t} \qquad w_i \leftarrow w_i\,e^{-\eta\,\ell_i} \]
  • \(\ell_i\): the loss of expert \(i\) this round
  • \(\eta\): learning rate
Worked example

Two experts start with weights \([1, 1]\), \(\eta = 1\). Expert 1 has loss 0, expert 2 has loss 1. New weights: \([1,\ e^{-1}] = [1, 0.368]\), which normalize to \([0.731, 0.269]\). Trust shifted toward the expert that was right.

14.6 Continual (lifelong) learning and catastrophic forgetting

Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC)

A network trained on task B often forgets task A ("catastrophic forgetting"). EWC adds a penalty that protects the weights that mattered for the old task, measured by the Fisher information \(F_i\) (how sensitive the old task was to weight \(i\)).

\[ \mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_B + \frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_i F_i\,(\theta_i - \theta_i^{*})^2 \]
  • \(\theta_i^{*}\): the weight value after learning the old task
  • \(F_i\): how important weight \(i\) was to the old task
  • \(\lambda\): how strongly to protect the old task
Worked example

Two weights with importance \(F = [2, 0]\) have moved by \([0.5, 3]\) since the old task, \(\lambda = 1\). The penalty is \(\tfrac{1}{2}(2\cdot0.25 + 0\cdot9) = 0.25\). The unimportant weight (\(F = 0\)) may move freely even by 3; the important one is held close.

14.7 Causal inference and causal discovery

Correlation is not causation

Ice-cream sales and drownings rise together, but heat causes both (heat is a confounder). Causal inference asks what would happen if we intervened. The average treatment effect compares outcomes with and without treatment. In a randomized experiment a simple difference of averages works; otherwise we adjust for confounders \(Z\) with the backdoor formula. The notation \(do(X = x)\) means "force X to x", which is different from merely observing it.

\[ \text{ATE} = \mathbb{E}[Y(1)] - \mathbb{E}[Y(0)] \qquad P(y\mid do(x)) = \sum_z P(y\mid x, z)\,P(z) \]
  • \(Y(1), Y(0)\): the outcome if treated and if not treated
  • \(Z\): a confounder that affects both treatment and outcome
Worked example

ATE: a randomized trial gives an average outcome of 7 for the treated and 5 for the control, so ATE \(= 2\). Adjustment: a confounder \(Z\) is 50/50. Given treatment, the success rate is 0.3 when \(Z = 0\) and 0.8 when \(Z = 1\). Then \(P(y\mid do(x=1)) = 0.5\cdot0.3 + 0.5\cdot0.8 = 0.55\).

Causal discovery: the PC algorithm

Can we learn the cause-and-effect diagram from data alone? The PC algorithm starts with every pair of variables connected and removes an edge when two variables become independent after conditioning on others. For bell-curve data, independence is tested with the partial correlation:

\[ \rho_{XZ\cdot Y} = \frac{\rho_{XZ} - \rho_{XY}\,\rho_{YZ}}{\sqrt{(1 - \rho_{XY}^2)(1 - \rho_{YZ}^2)}} \]
Worked example

Suppose \(\rho_{XY} = 0.8\), \(\rho_{YZ} = 0.8\) and \(\rho_{XZ} = 0.64\). Then \(\rho_{XZ\cdot Y} = (0.64 - 0.8\cdot0.8)/\ldots = 0\). Once Y is known, X tells us nothing more about Z, so the direct X-Z edge is removed, leaving the chain X, Y, Z.

14.8 Anomaly detection

Finding the odd one out

The simplest detector flags a value whose z-score \(z = (x - \mu)/\sigma\) exceeds about 3. Isolation Forest notices that anomalies are easy to isolate: random splits separate them from the crowd in very few steps. Its score uses the average path length \(E[h]\) needed to isolate a point:

\[ s = 2^{-E[h]/c(n)} \]
  • \(E[h]\): average number of random splits needed to isolate the point
  • \(c(n)\): the typical path length for a dataset of size \(n\) (a normalizing constant)
Worked example

If a point needs a typical number of splits, \(E[h] = c(n)\), then \(s = 2^{-1} = 0.5\), so it is ordinary. If it needs only half that, \(E[h] = 0.5\,c(n)\) and \(s = 2^{-0.5} = 0.707\), clearly closer to 1, so more anomalous.

14.9 Recommender systems

Matrix factorization

Store ratings in a big table of users by items, mostly empty. Matrix factorization gives each user a hidden vector \(p_u\) and each item a hidden vector \(q_i\), so that the predicted rating is their dot product (Section 2.1.2). The vectors are learned by minimizing the error on known ratings plus an L2 penalty (Section 5.12).

\[ \hat{r}_{ui} = p_u \cdot q_i \qquad \min\sum_{(u,i)}\big(r_{ui} - p_u\cdot q_i\big)^2 + \lambda\big(\|p_u\|^2 + \|q_i\|^2\big) \]
Worked example

A user vector \(p = [1, 2]\) and an item vector \(q = [3, 1]\) predict a rating of \(1\cdot3 + 2\cdot1 = 5\).

14.10 Explainable AI

Shapley values and gradient-times-input

Shapley values (from game theory) share credit for a prediction fairly among the input features: a feature's credit is its average marginal contribution over all the orders in which features could be added. A quick alternative for neural networks is gradient times input. LIME fits a small, simple model around one prediction to see which features matter locally.

\[ \phi_i = \sum_{S \subseteq N\setminus\{i\}}\frac{|S|!\,(n - |S| - 1)!}{n!}\Big[v(S \cup \{i\}) - v(S)\Big] \qquad \text{attribution}_i = x_i\cdot\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i} \]
  • \(v(S)\): the model's payoff when only the features in set \(S\) are known
  • \(n\): the number of features
Worked example

Shapley: two features with \(v(\varnothing) = 0\), \(v(\{1\}) = 10\), \(v(\{2\}) = 20\), \(v(\{1,2\}) = 40\). Feature 1: \(\tfrac{1}{2}(10 - 0) + \tfrac{1}{2}(40 - 20) = 15\). Feature 2: \(\tfrac{1}{2}(20 - 0) + \tfrac{1}{2}(40 - 10) = 25\). The credits sum to \(40 = v(\{1,2\})\). Gradient times input: \(f = 3x_1 + 2x_2\) at \(x = [2, 1]\) gives attributions \([2\cdot3,\ 1\cdot2] = [6, 2]\).

14.11 Active learning

Asking for the most useful labels

If labeling costs money, ask for labels on the examples the model is most unsure about. Two common measures of uncertainty are the entropy of the predicted probabilities (Section 4.5) and least confidence \(1 - \max p\).

Worked example

Prediction A is \([0.5, 0.5]\): entropy \(= 0.693\) (natural log) and least confidence \(= 0.5\). Prediction B is \([0.9, 0.1]\): entropy \(= 0.325\) and least confidence \(= 0.1\). A is far more uncertain, so ask a human to label A first.

14.12 Multi-task and transfer learning

Sharing what was learned

Multi-task learning trains one model on several related tasks with a combined loss, so tasks help each other. Transfer learning starts from a model already trained on a large task and either freezes its early layers (general features like edges) while training new later layers, or fine-tunes everything gently (Section 8.14).

\[ \mathcal{L} = \sum_{t} w_t\,\mathcal{L}_t \]
  • \(w_t\): how much task \(t\) counts
  • \(\mathcal{L}_t\): the loss on task \(t\)
Worked example

Two tasks with losses \(0.4\) and \(1.0\) and weights \([0.7, 0.3]\): total \(= 0.7\cdot0.4 + 0.3\cdot1.0 = 0.28 + 0.30 = 0.58\).

In your vault

J01 to J12 and Z05 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives cover this whole part with runnable code.

Part 15. More neural network models and generative maths

The vault covers many architectures beyond the mainstream ones in Part 7. Each is a different answer to the question "what should a neuron or a layer be?".

15.1 Hopfield networks: memory as an energy landscape

Recalling a stored pattern from a damaged copy

A Hopfield network stores patterns of \(+1\) and \(-1\) values in its weights so that each stored pattern is a low point (a valley) of an energy function. Starting from a noisy pattern and updating neurons one at a time slides downhill into the nearest valley, recalling the memory.

\[ E(x) = -\tfrac{1}{2}\,x^{T}Wx \qquad W = \sum_p x^{(p)}x^{(p)T} - I \qquad x_i \leftarrow \text{sign}\Big(\sum_j W_{ij}x_j\Big) \]
  • \(x\): the state: a vector of \(+1\) and \(-1\)
  • \(W\): symmetric weights built from the stored patterns (Hebbian rule: "neurons that fire together wire together")
  • \(I\): identity matrix: removes each neuron's connection to itself
Worked example

Store \(x = [1, -1, 1]\). Then \(W = \begin{bmatrix}0&-1&1\\-1&0&-1\\1&-1&0\end{bmatrix}\) and the stored pattern has energy \(E = -\tfrac{1}{2}(9 - 3) = -3\). Start from a damaged copy \(x = [1, 1, 1]\): \(Wx = [0, -2, 0]\). Neuron 2 sees \(-2\) and flips to \(-1\) (the zeros leave neurons 1 and 3 unchanged), giving \([1, -1, 1]\), the original.

15.2 Word2Vec: the classic word embeddings

Predict the neighbours, get the meaning for free

Skip-gram trains a tiny network to predict the words around a given word. It uses negative sampling to stay cheap: push up the score of true neighbour pairs and push down the score of a few random pairs. The learned word vectors end up capturing meaning, so that vector arithmetic works.

\[ \mathcal{L} = -\ln\sigma(u_o\cdot v_c) - \sum_{k=1}^{K}\ln\sigma(-u_k\cdot v_c) \]
  • \(v_c\): the vector of the centre word
  • \(u_o\): the vector of a true neighbour word
  • \(u_k\): vectors of random "negative" words
Worked example

If the true pair has dot product 2, its term is \(-\ln\sigma(2) = 0.127\), small and good. Analogy toy example: with king \(= [0.9, 0.8]\), man \(= [0.5, 0.1]\), woman \(= [0.5, 0.9]\): king \(-\) man \(+\) woman \(= [0.9, 1.6]\), which real embeddings place near "queen".

15.3 Normalizing flows

Exact probabilities by transforming a simple distribution

A normalizing flow builds a complicated distribution by pushing a simple one (a bell curve) through a chain of invertible functions. Because you can run it backwards, you can compute the exact probability of any data point using the change-of-variables rule, something GANs cannot do and VAEs only bound.

\[ p_x(x) = p_z\big(f(x)\big)\,\left|\det\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\right| \]
  • \(f\): the invertible map from data \(x\) to the simple variable \(z\)
  • \(\det \partial f/\partial x\): how much \(f\) stretches or squeezes space (the determinant of its slope matrix)
Worked example

Let \(f(x) = 2x\) and \(z\) be a standard bell curve. At \(x = 1\): \(z = 2\), \(p_z(2) = 0.05399\), and the stretch factor is \(|f'| = 2\), so \(p_x(1) = 0.05399 \times 2 = 0.108\). Stretching space by 2 spreads probability out, and the factor of 2 keeps the total at 1.

15.4 Neural ODEs and liquid neural networks

Layers as continuous time

A residual layer computes \(h \leftarrow h + f(h)\). Shrink the step size toward zero and you get a differential equation, \(dh/dt = f(h, t)\): a Neural ODE, whose "depth" is continuous time, solved numerically (for instance by Euler's method). Liquid neural networks use neurons whose time constant itself depends on the input, letting them adapt to changing conditions.

\[ \frac{dh}{dt} = f(h, t;\theta) \qquad h_{t+\Delta t} = h_t + \Delta t\,f(h_t, t) \qquad \frac{dx}{dt} = -\frac{x}{\tau} + f(x, I, \theta) \]
  • \(\Delta t\): the Euler step size
  • \(\tau\): the time constant: how quickly the neuron forgets
  • \(I\): the input
Worked example

Take \(dx/dt = -x/\tau + I\) with \(\tau = 1\), \(I = 1\), \(x_0 = 0\), \(\Delta t = 0.1\). Then \(x_1 = 0 + 0.1(0 + 1) = 0.1\) and \(x_2 = 0.1 + 0.1(-0.1 + 1) = 0.19\). The state climbs toward the balance point \(x = \tau I = 1\).

15.5 Spiking neural networks

Neurons that fire discrete spikes

Brains communicate with brief pulses, not smooth numbers. In a leaky integrate-and-fire (LIF) neuron the voltage accumulates input, leaks a little each step, and when it reaches a threshold the neuron spikes and resets. Because spikes are rare, such networks can be extremely energy-efficient on special hardware. (The vault writes this as \(\tau_m\,dV/dt = -(V - V_{\text{rest}}) + RI\); the discrete version is below.)

\[ V_t = \beta\,V_{t-1} + I_t \qquad \text{spike if } V_t \ge V_{\text{th}}, \text{ then } V_t \leftarrow 0 \]
  • \(\beta\): the leak: fraction of voltage kept each step (e.g. 0.9)
  • \(V_{\text{th}}\): the firing threshold
Worked example

Leak \(\beta = 0.9\), threshold 1, constant input \(0.5\). Voltages: \(0.5\), then \(0.9\cdot0.5 + 0.5 = 0.95\), then \(0.9\cdot0.95 + 0.5 = 1.355\), which is at least 1, so it spikes and resets to 0, then \(0.5\) again. Spike train: \([0, 0, 1, 0]\).

15.6 Restricted Boltzmann Machines and Deep Belief Networks (DeepBelief nets)

An energy-based model with hidden switches

An RBM has visible units \(v\) and hidden units \(h\), connected in one bipartite layer. Each joint configuration has an energy, and lower energy means higher probability. Stacking RBMs gives a Deep Belief Network, an early way to pretrain deep networks.

\[ E(v,h) = -\sum_i a_i v_i - \sum_j b_j h_j - \sum_{i,j} v_i W_{ij} h_j \qquad P(v,h) = \frac{e^{-E(v,h)}}{Z} \qquad P(h_j = 1\mid v) = \sigma\Big(b_j + \sum_i v_i W_{ij}\Big) \]
  • \(a, b\): biases of visible and hidden units
  • \(W\): connection weights
  • \(Z\): the sum of \(e^{-E}\) over every configuration (the normalizer)
Worked example

One visible and one hidden unit (each 0 or 1), \(W = 1\), \(a = b = 0\). Energies: \(E(1,1) = -1\) and 0 for the other three states, so \(Z = e^{1} + 1 + 1 + 1 = 5.718\) and \(P(1,1) = 2.718/5.718 = 0.475\). Also \(P(h = 1\mid v = 1) = \sigma(1) = 0.731\). Training uses contrastive divergence: \(\Delta W = \eta(\langle vh\rangle_{\text{data}} - \langle vh\rangle_{\text{reconstruction}})\).

15.7 Self-Organizing Maps (Kohonen maps)

A grid of neurons that unfolds over the data

A SOM arranges neurons on a grid, each holding a weight vector. For each input, find the best-matching unit (the closest weight vector), then pull it and its grid neighbours toward the input, with less pull for neighbours further away. Over time the grid stretches to cover the data while preserving neighbourhoods.

\[ c = \arg\min_v\|x - W_v\| \qquad W_v \leftarrow W_v + \theta(c,v)\,\alpha\,(x - W_v) \qquad \theta(c,v) = \exp\!\Big(-\frac{\|r_c - r_v\|^2}{2\sigma^2}\Big) \]
  • \(c\): the winning neuron
  • \(r_c, r_v\): grid positions of the winner and of neuron \(v\)
  • \(\theta\): the neighbourhood factor: 1 at the winner, smaller further away
Worked example

Winner weight \(W = [0, 0]\), input \(x = [1, 1]\), \(\alpha = 0.5\), \(\theta = 1\) at the winner: \(W \leftarrow [0, 0] + 0.5([1,1] - [0,0]) = [0.5, 0.5]\), halfway to the input.

15.8 Capsule networks

Neurons that output little vectors

A capsule outputs a vector whose length means "how likely is this thing present" and whose direction encodes its pose (position, angle). A squash function keeps every length between 0 and 1, and dynamic routing sends a capsule's output to the higher capsule whose prediction it agrees with.

\[ v = \frac{\|s\|^2}{1 + \|s\|^2}\,\frac{s}{\|s\|} \]
  • \(s\): the capsule's raw total input vector
  • \(v\): its squashed output; its length is \(\|s\|^2/(1 + \|s\|^2)\)
Worked example

If \(\|s\| = 1\) the output length is \(1/2 = 0.5\). If \(\|s\| = 3\) it is \(9/10 = 0.9\). Long inputs give near-1 lengths (confident presence) and short inputs shrink toward 0.

15.9 Reservoir computing and Echo State Networks

Random recurrent network, train only the last layer

An Echo State Network uses a big recurrent network with fixed random weights (the "reservoir") that echoes inputs through rich dynamics. Only a simple linear readout is trained, using ridge regression (Section 6.1), so training is fast and needs no backpropagation through time.

\[ x_t = \tanh(W_{\text{res}}\,x_{t-1} + W_{\text{in}}\,u_t) \qquad W_{\text{out}} = (X^{T}X + \lambda I)^{-1}X^{T}Y \]
  • \(u_t\): the input at time \(t\)
  • \(x_t\): the reservoir state
  • \(W_{\text{out}}\): the only trained weights

15.10 Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs)

Learnable curves on the edges instead of weights

In an MLP each edge holds a fixed-shape multiplier and the neuron applies a fixed activation. In a KAN every edge holds a learnable function (a spline, meaning a smooth curve stitched from polynomial pieces) and the neurons just add. Gradient descent bends the curve's control points. KANs can be very parameter-efficient and their learned functions can be read off directly, but they are slow on GPUs built for matrix multiplication.

\[ \text{MLP: } y = f\big(\textstyle\sum_i w_i x_i\big) \qquad \text{KAN: } y = \sum_i \varphi_i(x_i) \]
  • \(\varphi_i\): a learnable one-input function on edge \(i\)
Worked example

Suppose the learned curves are \(\varphi_1(x) = x^2\) and \(\varphi_2(x) = 2x\). For inputs \(x = [1, 2]\): \(y = 1^2 + 2\cdot2 = 5\).

15.11 JEPA, active inference and neurosymbolic AI

JEPA: predict in meaning space, not pixel space

A Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture predicts the embedding of a hidden part from the embedding of the visible part, instead of predicting raw pixels or words. It ignores unpredictable detail and learns abstract structure. The target encoder is usually a slowly moving average of the main encoder (an "EMA").

\[ \hat{s}_y = P(s_x, z) \qquad \mathcal{L} = \|\hat{s}_y - s_y\|_2^2 \qquad \theta_y \leftarrow \tau\,\theta_y + (1 - \tau)\,\theta_x \]
  • \(s_x, s_y\): embeddings of the visible and hidden parts
  • \(P\): the predictor network
  • \(\tau\): EMA rate, close to 1
Worked example

Target embedding \(s_y = [1, 0]\), prediction \(\hat{s}_y = [0.8, 0.2]\): loss \(= 0.04 + 0.04 = 0.08\). EMA update with \(\tau = 0.99\), \(\theta_y = 1.0\), \(\theta_x = 2.0\): \(\theta_y = 0.99\cdot1 + 0.01\cdot2 = 1.01\).

Active inference and neurosymbolic AI

Active inference says an agent acts to minimize its surprise. Its free energy \(F\) is (up to sign) the same quantity as the ELBO in Section 11.4: a measure of how well its internal beliefs \(q(s)\) explain its observations \(o\). \(F = \text{KL}[q(s)\,\|\,p(s)] - \mathbb{E}_q[\ln p(o\mid s)]\). Neurosymbolic AI combines a neural network (good at perception and fuzzy patterns) with symbolic logic and rules (good at exact reasoning), for example a network that recognizes objects feeding a rule engine that reasons about them.

15.12 Optimal transport: Wasserstein distance and Sinkhorn

The cost of moving one pile of dirt into another

KL divergence (Section 4.6) breaks down when two distributions do not overlap. The Wasserstein (earth mover's) distance instead asks: what is the least total work to move the probability mass of one distribution to match the other? In one dimension it equals the summed gap between the two cumulative distributions. Sinkhorn makes the calculation fast by adding a small "entropy" smoothing and then alternately rescaling rows and columns of a matrix until both distributions' totals are matched. WGANs use this distance for stable GAN training.

\[ W_1(P,Q) = \sum_x\big|F_P(x) - F_Q(x)\big|\ \Delta x \ \ (\text{1-D}) \]
  • \(F_P, F_Q\): the cumulative distributions (running totals of probability)
  • \(\Delta x\): the spacing between positions
Worked example

Positions 0, 1, 2 with spacing 1. \(P = [0.5, 0.5, 0]\) and \(Q = [0, 0.5, 0.5]\). Cumulative sums: \(F_P = [0.5, 1, 1]\), \(F_Q = [0, 0.5, 1]\). Gaps \([0.5, 0.5, 0]\) sum to \(W_1 = 1.0\): for instance move 0.5 of mass one step and another 0.5 of mass one more step.

15.13 Energy-based models, score matching, Langevin dynamics and HMC

Probability from an energy, without the normalizer

An energy-based model defines \(p(x) = e^{-E(x)}/Z\), where low energy means high probability. The normalizer \(Z\) is usually impossible to compute, but the score \(s(x) = \nabla_x \ln p(x) = -\nabla_x E(x)\) does not contain \(Z\). Score matching trains a network to predict the score, and Langevin dynamics generates samples by following the score uphill with a little random noise. This is the mathematical core behind diffusion models (Section 7.6).

\[ x' = x + \tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\,s(x) + \sqrt{\varepsilon}\,z,\ \ z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1) \qquad \text{Gaussian score: } s(x) = -\frac{x - \mu}{\sigma^2} \]
  • \(\varepsilon\): the step size
  • \(z\): fresh random noise each step
Worked example

For a standard bell curve, \(s(x) = -x\); at \(x = 2\), \(s = -2\). With \(\varepsilon = 0.1\) and (for illustration) \(z = 0\): \(x' = 2 + 0.05(-2) = 1.9\), a step toward the high-probability centre.

Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) and the leapfrog integrator

HMC adds a fictional momentum \(p\) and simulates a ball rolling on the surface of the potential energy \(U(x) = -\ln p(x)\), then applies the Metropolis rule (Section 11.3). It travels much farther per proposal than a random walk. The leapfrog steps alternate half-steps of momentum with full steps of position:

\[ p \leftarrow p - \tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\nabla U(x) \quad x \leftarrow x + \varepsilon\,p \quad p \leftarrow p - \tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\nabla U(x) \]
Worked example

Take \(U(x) = x^2/2\) (so \(\nabla U = x\)), start at \(x = 1\), \(p = 0\), \(\varepsilon = 0.1\). First half-step: \(p = 0 - 0.05\cdot1 = -0.05\). Position: \(x = 1 + 0.1(-0.05) = 0.995\). Second half-step: \(p = -0.05 - 0.05\cdot0.995 = -0.09975\). The ball rolls toward the bottom at \(x = 0\).

In your vault

Reports/Deep_Dives/09 to 12, 18 to 21, and in Algorithm_Codex the K01 to K07, O01 to O03 and P01 to P03 deep dives cover this part in full.

Part 16. Search, logic, games, evolutionary algorithms and fast-lookup structures

Not all of AI is learning from data. A large part is search (trying possibilities cleverly), logic (following rules), game theory (reasoning about other decision-makers), and evolution-inspired optimization. These need very little calculus and a lot of careful counting.

16.1 Uninformed search: BFS, DFS and uniform-cost

Exploring a maze of possibilities with no hints

Model a problem as a graph: nodes are situations and edges are moves. Breadth-first search (BFS) explores all nodes one step away, then two steps away, and so on, so it finds the path with the fewest steps but must remember a lot. Depth-first search (DFS) dives down one path as far as possible before backing up, using little memory but possibly finding a long path first. Uniform-cost search expands whichever node has the lowest total path cost so far.

\[ \text{nodes at depth } d = b^d \qquad \text{total up to depth } d = \sum_{i=0}^{d} b^i \]
  • \(b\): the branching factor: how many moves are possible from each node
  • \(d\): the depth (number of moves)
Worked example

With \(b = 10\) choices per step and \(d = 5\) steps, there are \(10^5 = 100{,}000\) nodes at depth 5 and \(1 + 10 + 100 + 1000 + 10{,}000 + 100{,}000 = 111{,}111\) nodes in total. The count explodes exponentially, which is why smarter search matters.

16.2 Informed search: A*

Search with a sense of direction

A* expands the node with the lowest estimated total cost \(f\): the cost already paid, \(g\), plus a heuristic guess \(h\) of the cost remaining. If \(h\) never overestimates the true remaining cost (it is "admissible"), A* is guaranteed to find the cheapest path.

\[ f(n) = g(n) + h(n) \]
  • \(g(n)\): actual cost from the start to node \(n\)
  • \(h(n)\): estimated cost from \(n\) to the goal (for example straight-line distance)
Worked example

A node has already cost \(g = 3\) to reach and the goal looks about \(h = 4\) away, so \(f = 7\). A rival node with \(g = 2\) and \(h = 6\) has \(f = 8\), so A* explores the first node first.

16.3 Adversarial search: minimax and alpha-beta pruning

Planning against an opponent

In a two-player game, minimax assumes you pick the move that maximizes your score while your opponent picks the reply that minimizes it. Values are passed up a game tree: max levels take the largest child, min levels the smallest. Alpha-beta pruning skips branches that cannot change the final decision, giving the same answer faster.

\[ V(\text{node}) = \begin{cases}\max_{\text{children}} V & \text{your turn}\\ \min_{\text{children}} V & \text{opponent's turn}\end{cases} \]
Worked example

You choose between two moves. After move A the opponent can leave you 3 or 5, so they choose 3. After move B they can leave you 2 or 9, so they choose 2. You pick the larger of \(\{3, 2\}\), which is move A with value 3. Pruning: once you know A is worth 3, as soon as the opponent shows a 2 under move B you can ignore the 9, because B can only be at most 2, which is already worse than 3.

16.4 Constraint satisfaction problems (CSP)

Variables, domains, constraints

A CSP has variables, each with a domain of allowed values, and constraints between them (Sudoku, scheduling, map colouring). Solvers use backtracking (try a value, undo it on conflict) improved by forward checking (after assigning a value, immediately remove impossible values from neighbours) and arc consistency (keep pruning until every remaining value has a compatible partner).

Worked example

Colour three mutually adjacent regions with three colours so that neighbours differ. The first region has 3 choices, the second 2 (not the first's colour), the third 1, so there are \(3 \times 2 \times 1 = 6\) valid colourings. With only 2 colours there would be 0 solutions, and forward checking would discover this immediately.

16.5 Fuzzy logic

Degrees of truth between 0 and 1

Ordinary logic says a statement is true or false. Fuzzy logic lets it be partly true: a person can be "tall" to degree 0.5. A membership function assigns that degree. Operators generalize AND, OR and NOT, and defuzzification turns the fuzzy result back into one number, often by the centroid (weighted average).

\[ \mu_A\wedge\mu_B = \min(\mu_A,\mu_B) \quad \mu_A\vee\mu_B = \max(\mu_A,\mu_B) \quad \neg\mu_A = 1 - \mu_A \quad x^* = \frac{\sum \mu_i x_i}{\sum \mu_i} \]
Worked example

A triangular membership for "tall" rises from 0 at 160 cm to 1 at 190 cm, so a 175 cm person has \(\mu = (175-160)/30 = 0.5\). If "heavy" has degree 0.8: tall AND heavy \(= \min(0.5, 0.8) = 0.5\); tall OR heavy \(= 0.8\); NOT tall \(= 0.5\). Centroid defuzzification of outputs \(x = [10, 20, 30]\) with degrees \([0.2, 0.6, 0.2]\) gives \((2 + 12 + 6)/1.0 = 20\).

16.6 Rule-based expert systems

If-then knowledge, chained together

An expert system stores knowledge as if-then rules and a working memory of known facts. Forward chaining applies any rule whose conditions are satisfied to derive new facts until nothing changes. Backward chaining starts from a goal and asks what would need to be true. Classic systems attach certainty factors (CF) to rules. When two rules support the same conclusion, their evidence combines:

\[ CF = CF_1 + CF_2\,(1 - CF_1) \]
Worked example

Two independent rules support a diagnosis with confidences 0.6 and 0.5. Combined: \(0.6 + 0.5(1 - 0.6) = 0.6 + 0.2 = 0.8\). More evidence raises confidence but never past 1.

16.7 Classical graph algorithms

Shortest paths and PageRank

Dijkstra's algorithm finds the cheapest path from one node to all others by always finalizing the closest unfinished node. PageRank scores importance: a page is important if important pages link to it. A random surfer follows links with probability \(d\) (the damping factor, usually 0.85) and jumps to a random page otherwise. Graph structure also feeds machine learning: the graph Laplacian \(L = D - A\) (degree matrix minus adjacency matrix) drives spectral clustering, and a minimum spanning tree (for example by Kruskal's algorithm) connects all nodes with least total edge weight.

\[ r = d\,M\,r + \frac{1 - d}{N} \]
  • \(r\): the vector of page importances
  • \(M\): the link matrix (each column splits a page's vote among the pages it links to)
  • \(N\): number of pages
Worked example

Dijkstra: edges A to B cost 2, B to C cost 3, A to C cost 7. The cheapest route A to C goes through B and costs 5, not 7. PageRank: two pages that link only to each other are perfectly symmetric, so each ends with importance \(0.5\) (the iteration \(r = 0.85\,Mr + 0.075\) stays at \([0.5, 0.5]\)).

16.8 Game theory and mechanism design

Nash equilibrium

In a game, each player picks a strategy and gets a payoff that depends on everyone's choices. A Nash equilibrium is a set of strategies where no player can do better by changing only their own choice. In the Prisoner's Dilemma, both players are better off cooperating, yet each is individually tempted to defect, so the equilibrium is mutual defection. Some games (like matching pennies) have no pure equilibrium and are solved by mixed strategies, randomizing so the opponent cannot exploit you.

Worked example

Payoffs (you, them): both cooperate \((3, 3)\); you defect, they cooperate \((5, 0)\); you cooperate, they defect \((0, 5)\); both defect \((1, 1)\). Whatever they do, defecting pays you more (5 vs 3, or 1 vs 0), so both defect and get \((1, 1)\), the Nash equilibrium. In matching pennies the best mixed strategy is 50/50, with expected payoff 0.

Auctions and VCG

Mechanism design asks the reverse question: design the rules so that people's selfish behaviour produces a good outcome. In a second-price (Vickrey) auction the highest bidder wins but pays the second-highest bid, which makes honest bidding the best strategy. VCG generalizes this: each winner pays the harm their presence causes to everyone else.

\[ \text{utility} = \text{true value} - \text{price paid} \]
Worked example

Bids are \([10, 7, 4]\). The bidder with value 10 wins and pays 7, getting utility \(10 - 7 = 3\). Bidding lower could not lower the price they pay and would only risk losing, and bidding higher could only force them to pay more than their value, so bidding the truth is best.

16.9 Genetic algorithms and genetic programming

Evolution as an optimizer

A genetic algorithm keeps a population of candidate solutions, each encoded as a string (a "chromosome"). Each generation: select parents with probability proportional to their fitness, crossover (swap tails between two parents), and mutate (randomly flip a few bits). Fitter solutions spread. Genetic programming evolves whole programs stored as expression trees instead of bit strings.

\[ P(\text{select } i) = \frac{f_i}{\sum_j f_j} \]
  • \(f_i\): the fitness of individual \(i\) (higher is better)
Worked example

Fitness values \([1, 2, 7]\) give selection probabilities \([0.1, 0.2, 0.7]\). Crossover after position 4 of parents 110010 and 001101 gives children 1100+01 \(=\) 110001 and 0011+10 \(=\) 001110.

16.10 Evolution strategies, differential evolution, swarms, annealing and multi-objective search

Six more nature-inspired optimizers

Evolution Strategies (ES) estimate a gradient without backpropagation by trying random perturbations of the parameters and moving toward the ones that scored well. Differential Evolution (DE) builds a new candidate by adding a scaled difference of two population members to a third. Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) moves candidates ("particles") with a velocity pulled toward their own best position and the swarm's best. Ant Colony Optimization (ACO) lets artificial ants lay pheromone on good routes, which evaporates over time. Simulated Annealing (SA) accepts worse moves with a probability that shrinks as a "temperature" cools, escaping local minima. NEAT (NeuroEvolution of Augmenting Topologies, an example of neuroevolution) evolves both the weights and the wiring of a neural network, protecting new structures by grouping similar networks into species.

\[ \text{ES: } \nabla \approx \frac{1}{n\sigma}\sum_i F(\theta + \sigma\epsilon_i)\,\epsilon_i \qquad \text{DE: } v = a + F(b - c) \qquad \text{PSO: } v \leftarrow w v + c_1 r_1 (p_{\text{best}} - x) + c_2 r_2 (g_{\text{best}} - x) \]
  • \(F\): a scaling factor (DE), or the fitness score (ES)
  • \(w, c_1, c_2\): PSO inertia and attraction strengths
  • \(r_1, r_2\): random numbers between 0 and 1
  • \(p_{\text{best}}, g_{\text{best}}\): the particle's own best position and the swarm's best
Worked example

DE: \(a = 2\), \(b = 4\), \(c = 1\), \(F = 0.5\): \(v = 2 + 0.5(4 - 1) = 3.5\). PSO: \(w = 0.7\), \(v = 1\), \(x = 1\), \(p_{\text{best}} = 2\), \(g_{\text{best}} = 3\), \(c_1 = c_2 = 1.5\), \(r_1 = r_2 = 0.5\): \(v = 0.7 + 1.5\cdot0.5\cdot1 + 1.5\cdot0.5\cdot2 = 0.7 + 0.75 + 1.5 = 2.95\). ACO: pheromone \(\tau \leftarrow (1 - \rho)\tau + \Delta\tau\); with \(\rho = 0.5\), \(\tau = 1\), \(\Delta\tau = 0.4\) it becomes \(0.9\). If two routes hold pheromone \([2, 1]\), ants pick them with probability \([0.667, 0.333]\). SA: accept a worse move with probability \(e^{-\Delta E/T}\); for \(\Delta E = 2\) and \(T = 1\) that is \(e^{-2} = 0.135\), and as \(T\) shrinks (say \(T \leftarrow 0.95T\)) it falls further.

Multi-objective optimization (NSGA-II)

When goals conflict (cheap versus accurate), there is no single best answer. Solution A dominates B if A is at least as good on every objective and strictly better on at least one. The set of solutions that nothing dominates is the Pareto front, and NSGA-II evolves a population toward it while keeping it spread out.

Worked example

Each option is (cost, error), lower is better. \(A = (2, 3)\) dominates \(B = (3, 4)\) since it is better on both. But \(C = (1, 5)\) versus \(A = (2, 3)\): C is cheaper but less accurate, so neither dominates and both stay on the Pareto front.

16.11 Fast lookup: k-d trees, sketches and Bloom filters

Answering questions about huge data with tiny memory

A k-d tree splits space by the median along alternating axes, so a nearest-neighbour query can skip whole regions. (HNSW and LSH for high dimensions were in Section 8.22.) Probabilistic sketches trade a small error for a tiny memory footprint. A Bloom filter answers "have I seen this item?" with no false negatives but occasional false positives. A Count-Min sketch estimates how often each item appeared; it never underestimates. HyperLogLog estimates the number of distinct items from the longest run of leading zeros in their hashes.

\[ \text{Bloom false-positive rate} \approx \left(1 - e^{-kn/m}\right)^{k} \qquad \text{Count-Min estimate} = \min_j \text{count}_j(x) \]
  • \(m\): number of bits in the filter
  • \(n\): items inserted
  • \(k\): number of hash functions
Worked example

Bloom: \(m = 1000\) bits, \(n = 100\) items, \(k = 3\): \((1 - e^{-0.3})^3 = (0.2592)^3 = 0.0174\), about a 1.7% false-positive rate. Count-Min: if an item's counters in three rows read \([5, 3, 7]\), the estimate is \(\min = 3\); collisions can only inflate counters, so the smallest is the best guess.

In your vault

H01 to H09, L01 to L08, M01 to M04 and Q01 to Q03 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives cover this part in full with runnable code.

Part 17. Science and engineering algorithms: quantum, physics, biology, signals, robotics, chips, geometry and security

The Algorithm Codex goes far beyond machine learning into the numerical methods that science and engineering run on. Each topic below gets one beginner-friendly idea, its key formula, and one hand-checkable example.

17.1 Quantum computing

Qubits, gates and measurement

A qubit is described by two complex numbers (amplitudes) \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\). When measured it gives 0 with probability \(|\alpha|^2\) and 1 with probability \(|\beta|^2\), so those two probabilities must add to 1. A quantum gate is a matrix that transforms the amplitudes. The Hadamard gate turns a definite 0 into an equal mixture ("superposition").

\[ |\psi\rangle = \alpha|0\rangle + \beta|1\rangle \quad |\alpha|^2 + |\beta|^2 = 1 \qquad H|0\rangle = \frac{|0\rangle + |1\rangle}{\sqrt{2}} \]
Worked example

After a Hadamard gate, \(\alpha = \beta = 1/\sqrt{2} = 0.7071\), so each outcome has probability \(0.7071^2 = 0.5\), a fair coin. Why simulating is hard: \(n\) qubits need \(2^n\) amplitudes. For \(n = 30\) that is \(1{,}073{,}741{,}824\) numbers, about 17.2 GB at 16 bytes each. A circuit simulator (Codex R02) stores exactly this state vector.

Grover, Shor, VQE, QAOA, error correction and tensor networks

Grover's search finds one marked item among \(N\) in about \(\tfrac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N}\) steps instead of about \(N/2\). Shor's algorithm factors numbers by finding the period \(r\) of \(a^x \bmod N\), then using \(\gcd(a^{r/2}\pm1, N)\). VQE (the Variational Quantum Eigensolver) finds the lowest energy of a system with a tunable circuit, using the variational principle \(E(\theta) = \langle\psi(\theta)|H|\psi(\theta)\rangle \ge E_0\), so any guess is an upper bound and training pushes it down (a classical optimizer tunes \(\theta\)). QAOA applies alternating "cost" and "mixer" layers to attack optimization problems. The bit-flip error-correcting code stores one bit in three and takes a majority vote. Tensor networks / Matrix Product States compress a quantum state into a chain of small tensors of "bond dimension" \(\chi\), using about \(n\,d\,\chi^2\) numbers instead of \(d^n\).

\[ \text{Grover steps} \approx \tfrac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N} \qquad p_{\text{logical}} = 3p^2 - 2p^3 \qquad \text{MPS size} \approx n\,d\,\chi^2 \]
  • \(p\): the chance a single physical bit flips
  • \(d\): states per site (2 for a qubit)
Worked example

Grover: \(N = 10^6\) gives about \(\tfrac{\pi}{4}\times1000 = 785\) steps. Shor: factor 15 with \(a = 7\). The powers \(7^1, 7^2, 7^3, 7^4 \bmod 15\) are \(7, 4, 13, 1\), so the period is \(r = 4\). Then \(\gcd(7^2 - 1, 15) = \gcd(48, 15) = 3\) and \(\gcd(7^2 + 1, 15) = \gcd(50, 15) = 5\), and indeed \(15 = 3\times5\). Error correction: with \(p = 0.1\), the logical error is \(3(0.01) - 2(0.001) = 0.028\), much better than 0.1. MPS: 50 qubits with \(\chi = 10\) need \(50\cdot2\cdot100 = 10{,}000\) numbers instead of \(2^{50} \approx 1.1\times10^{15}\).

17.2 Physics simulation

N-body gravity, molecular dynamics and the Verlet integrator

To simulate motion, compute the force on every body, convert to acceleration (\(a = F/m\)), and step forward in small time increments \(\Delta t\). Velocity Verlet is the favourite method because it keeps energy nearly constant over long runs. The same integrator drives molecular dynamics (atoms with forces from a model such as Lennard-Jones) and is the basis of the leapfrog step in Section 15.13.

\[ F = \frac{G\,m_1 m_2}{r^2} \qquad x' = x + v\,\Delta t + \tfrac{1}{2}a\,\Delta t^2 \qquad v' = v + \tfrac{1}{2}(a + a')\,\Delta t \qquad V_{LJ}(r) = 4\varepsilon\Big[\big(\tfrac{\sigma}{r}\big)^{12} - \big(\tfrac{\sigma}{r}\big)^{6}\Big] \]
  • \(G\): gravitational constant, \(6.674\times10^{-11}\)
  • \(a, a'\): acceleration before and after the step
  • \(\varepsilon, \sigma\): Lennard-Jones depth and size
Worked example

Gravity: two 1000 kg masses 1 m apart attract with \(6.674\times10^{-11}\cdot10^6/1 = 6.674\times10^{-5}\) N. Verlet: suppose \(x = 0\), \(v = 1\), the current acceleration is \(a = -1\) (a force pulling back) and \(\Delta t = 0.1\). Then \(x' = 0 + 0.1 + 0.5(-1)(0.01) = 0.095\). If the spring law \(a = -x\) now gives \(a' = -0.095\), then \(v' = 1 + 0.5(-1 - 0.095)(0.1) = 0.945\). Lennard-Jones: the minimum energy is \(-\varepsilon\) at \(r = 2^{1/6}\sigma\), where attraction and repulsion balance.

Ising model, finite differences, Kepler and Lomb-Scargle

The 2D Ising model (magnets that point up or down) has energy \(E = -J\sum s_i s_j\); Metropolis sampling (Section 11.3) flips a spin and accepts with probability \(e^{-\Delta E/kT}\). Finite differences replace derivatives by differences between neighbouring grid points, which turns a PDE (such as the heat equation) into simple arithmetic. Kepler's equation links where a planet is on its orbit to time; it has no closed-form solution, so one solves it numerically (for instance with Newton's method, Section 13.1). The Lomb-Scargle periodogram finds repeating cycles in data sampled at uneven times, where the plain FFT cannot be used.

\[ u''(x_i) \approx \frac{u_{i+1} - 2u_i + u_{i-1}}{h^2} \qquad u_i^{\text{new}} = u_i + r\,(u_{i+1} - 2u_i + u_{i-1}),\ r = \frac{D\,\Delta t}{h^2} \qquad M = E - e\sin E \]
  • \(h\): grid spacing
  • \(D\): diffusion coefficient
  • \(e\): orbit eccentricity
  • \(M, E\): mean and eccentric anomaly (angles describing orbit position)
Worked example

Ising: flipping a spin that raises the energy by \(\Delta E = 2\) at \(kT = 1\) is accepted with probability \(e^{-2} = 0.135\). Finite difference: for \(u = [0, 1, 4]\), \(h = 1\): \((4 - 2 + 0)/1 = 2\), exactly \(u'' = 2\) for \(u = x^2\). Heat step: \(r = 0.25\), \(u = [0, 1, 0]\): the middle becomes \(1 + 0.25(0 - 2 + 0) = 0.5\) as heat spreads out (stable only for \(r \le 0.5\)). Kepler: \(e = 0.5\), \(E = 1\) radian gives \(M = 1 - 0.5\sin 1 = 0.579\).

17.3 Biology and chemistry algorithms

Sequence alignment (Needleman-Wunsch and Smith-Waterman)

Comparing two DNA or protein strings means finding the best way to line them up, allowing mismatches and gaps. Needleman-Wunsch fills a table where each cell is the best score for aligning the first \(i\) letters of one string with the first \(j\) of the other. Smith-Waterman adds a 0 option inside the max so it finds the best local match instead of a whole-sequence match.

\[ F(i,j) = \max\begin{cases}F(i-1,j-1) + s(a_i,b_j) & \text{match/mismatch}\\ F(i-1,j) + g & \text{gap}\\ F(i,j-1) + g & \text{gap}\end{cases} \]
  • \(s\): +1 for a match, \(-1\) for a mismatch (an example scoring)
  • \(g\): the gap penalty, here \(-1\)
Worked example

Align AG with AC using match +1, mismatch -1, gap -1. The first row and column start as \(0, -1, -2\). Cell (A,A) \(= 0 + 1 = 1\). Cell (G,C) \(= \max(1 + (-1),\ \ldots) = 0\). The best alignment score is \(0\): A matches A (+1) and G mismatches C (-1).

Trees, fingerprints, force fields, genetics and patterns

UPGMA builds an evolutionary (phylogenetic) tree by repeatedly merging the closest pair and averaging distances (the average-linkage idea from Section 10.1); neighbor-joining is a refinement that does not assume a constant evolution rate. A molecular fingerprint is a bit string flagging which substructures a molecule contains, and the Tanimoto score compares two of them. A molecular-mechanics force field adds simple energy terms (springs for bonds, \(E = \tfrac{1}{2}k(r - r_0)^2\), plus Lennard-Jones). In population genetics (Wright-Fisher), random drift steadily removes genetic variety, and the coalescent runs the same story backwards in time: two gene copies trace back to a common ancestor after about \(2N\) generations on average (about 100 generations for \(N = 50\) diploid individuals). Reaction-diffusion (Turing) patterns arise when two chemicals react and spread at different speeds, producing spots and stripes.

\[ \text{Tanimoto} = \frac{|A\cap B|}{|A\cup B|} \qquad H_t = H_0\left(1 - \frac{1}{2N}\right)^{t} \qquad \frac{\partial u}{\partial t} = D_u\nabla^2 u + f(u,v) \]
  • \(H_t\): the genetic diversity (heterozygosity) after \(t\) generations
  • \(N\): population size
Worked example

UPGMA: distances \(d(A,B) = 2\), \(d(A,C) = 6\), \(d(B,C) = 8\): merge A and B first, then \(d(AB, C) = (6 + 8)/2 = 7\). Tanimoto: bits on in A \(= \{1,2,3\}\), in B \(= \{2,3,4,5\}\): \(2/5 = 0.4\). Bond energy: \(k = 100\), stretch \(0.1\): \(\tfrac{1}{2}(100)(0.01) = 0.5\). Drift: \(N = 50\), \(t = 10\): diversity is multiplied by \((0.99)^{10} = 0.904\).

17.4 Signals, control, circuits and error-correcting codes

The Fourier transform and digital filters

Any signal is a sum of waves of different frequencies. The Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT) measures how much of each frequency is present. The FFT computes it in \(N\log_2 N\) steps instead of \(N^2\). A digital filter is a weighted sum of recent inputs; a moving average is the simplest low-pass filter. (Fourier Neural Operators in Section 17.8 use exactly this transform.)

\[ X_k = \sum_{n=0}^{N-1} x_n\,e^{-2\pi i\,kn/N} \qquad y_n = \sum_k b_k\,x_{n-k} \]
  • \(x_n\): the signal samples
  • \(X_k\): the strength of frequency number \(k\)
  • \(b_k\): the filter coefficients
Worked example

DFT of \(x = [1, 0, -1, 0]\) gives \(X = [0, 2, 0, 2]\): the signal is a single wave, so only frequency 1 (and its mirror, 3) is non-zero. FFT cost for \(N = 1024\): \(1024\times10 = 10{,}240\) versus \(1024^2 = 1{,}048{,}576\), about 100 times faster. Filter: \(y_n = (x_n + x_{n-1})/2\) on \([2, 4, 6]\) gives \(3\) and \(5\), a smoother signal.

PID control and LQR

A PID controller (like a thermostat or cruise control) reacts to the error \(e\) (target minus actual) with three parts: Proportional (how big the error is now), Integral (how long it has persisted), and Derivative (how fast it is changing). LQR is the optimal-control version: for a system \(\dot{x} = Ax + Bu\) it chooses \(u = -Kx\) to minimize the total cost \(\int (x^TQx + u^TRu)\,dt\), balancing how far you stray against how much effort you spend.

\[ u = K_p\,e + K_i\!\int e\,dt + K_d\,\frac{de}{dt} \]
  • \(K_p, K_i, K_d\): tuning gains for the three parts
  • \(Q, R\): LQR weights on state error and on control effort
Worked example

With \(e = 2\), accumulated error \(\int e = 3\), and \(de/dt = -1\) (error shrinking), gains \(K_p = 1\), \(K_i = 0.5\), \(K_d = 0.2\): \(u = 2 + 1.5 - 0.2 = 3.3\).

Circuits and error-correcting codes

Nodal analysis writes one equation per circuit node from "currents in equal currents out" and solves the system \(Gv = i\) (Section 13.4). Error-correcting codes add redundancy so that a few flipped bits can be detected and repaired. Hamming(7,4) adds 3 parity bits to 4 data bits and can locate and fix any single-bit error. Reed-Solomon works on whole symbols and corrects up to \(t = (n - k)/2\) wrong symbols.

Worked example

Circuit: 10 V source, two 1 kilo-ohm resistors in series, node voltage \(V\) between them: \((V - 10)/1000 + V/1000 = 0\), so \(V = 5\) V. Hamming: the code rate is \(4/7 = 0.571\). Reed-Solomon RS(255, 223): \(t = (255 - 223)/2 = 16\) symbol errors correctable.

17.5 Robotics and computer vision

Motion planning and kinematics

RRT (Rapidly-exploring Random Tree) grows a tree by sampling a random point, finding the nearest tree node, and stepping toward the sample if the way is clear. RRT* additionally rewires nearby nodes to shorten paths over time. PRM scatters random points, connects nearby free ones into a roadmap graph, then runs a graph search (Section 16.2). Forward kinematics computes where a robot's hand ends up from its joint angles; inverse kinematics goes the other way, usually by solving \(\Delta\theta = J^{+}\Delta x\) with the Jacobian \(J\) (the matrix of how each joint moves the hand).

\[ x = l_1\cos\theta_1 + l_2\cos(\theta_1 + \theta_2) \qquad y = l_1\sin\theta_1 + l_2\sin(\theta_1 + \theta_2) \]
  • \(l_1, l_2\): arm segment lengths
  • \(\theta_1, \theta_2\): joint angles
Worked example

A two-link arm with \(l_1 = l_2 = 1\), \(\theta_1 = 90^\circ\), \(\theta_2 = 0\) points straight up: \(x = 0 + 0 = 0\), \(y = 1 + 1 = 2\), so the hand is at \((0, 2)\).

Kalman filters for robots, SLAM and particle filters

The Extended Kalman Filter (EKF) handles curved motion by linearizing it at each step with a Jacobian \(F\): predict \(\hat{x}^- = f(\hat{x})\), \(P^- = FPF^{T} + Q\) (compare the scalar filter in Section 11.6). The Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF) avoids Jacobians by pushing a handful of carefully chosen "sigma points" (\(2n + 1\) of them) through the true non-linear function. SLAM (Simultaneous Localization and Mapping) estimates the robot's position and the map at once, with EKF-SLAM or a particle filter. A particle filter reweights and resamples many guesses (including its \(N_{\text{eff}}\) check from Section 11.6).

RANSAC, edge detection and optical flow

RANSAC fits a model despite outliers: repeatedly pick the minimum number of points, fit, and count how many other points agree, keeping the best. The number of rounds needed to succeed with probability \(p\) is \(N = \ln(1 - p)/\ln(1 - w^{s})\). Canny edge detection measures the image gradient with Sobel filters, \(G = \sqrt{G_x^2 + G_y^2}\), then thins and thresholds it. Lucas-Kanade optical flow estimates how pixels move between video frames by solving \(I_x u + I_y v + I_t = 0\) in a least-squares sense over a small window.

\[ N = \frac{\ln(1 - p)}{\ln(1 - w^{s})} \qquad G = \sqrt{G_x^2 + G_y^2} \]
  • \(p\): desired chance of finding a good model
  • \(w\): fraction of points that are genuine (inliers)
  • \(s\): points needed to fit the model
  • \(G_x, G_y\): horizontal and vertical brightness change
Worked example

RANSAC: fitting a line (\(s = 2\)) with half the points good (\(w = 0.5\)) and \(p = 0.99\): \(N = \ln(0.01)/\ln(0.75) = 16.0\), so about 17 rounds. Canny: \(G_x = 3\), \(G_y = 4\) gives edge strength 5.

17.6 Chip design and engineering algorithms

Electronic design automation (EDA)

Designing a chip means placing millions of cells and wiring them. Maze routing (Lee algorithm) floods outward from the start wire one step at a time (a BFS, Section 16.1) and traces back from the target. A Rectilinear Steiner tree connects pins with the least total wire, allowed to add extra junction points. Placement measures wire length by the half-perimeter wirelength (HPWL) of each net's bounding box and minimizes it with force-directed methods or simulated annealing (Section 16.10). Floorplanning arranges blocks using a slicing tree (often written as a Polish expression). Min-cut partitioning splits a circuit to minimize connections crossing the cut. Quine-McCluskey performs Boolean logic minimization (merging terms that differ in one variable) and BDDs represent Boolean functions compactly. Static timing analysis adds gate delays along paths to check the clock can be met. SAT solving (DPLL) decides whether a logic formula can be satisfied, using unit propagation.

\[ \text{HPWL} = (x_{\max} - x_{\min}) + (y_{\max} - y_{\min}) \qquad \text{arrival} = \max_{\text{inputs}}(\text{arrival}) + \text{gate delay} \]
Worked example

HPWL of pins \((0,0), (3,4), (1,2)\): \((3 - 0) + (4 - 0) = 7\). Steiner: pins \((0,0), (2,0), (1,1)\): a spanning tree needs \(2 + 2 = 4\) units, but adding a junction at \((1, 0)\) gives \(1 + 1 + 1 = 3\). Boolean: \(\overline{A}B + AB = B\). Timing: inputs arriving at times 2 and 3 into a gate with delay 1 give arrival \(3 + 1 = 4\). DPLL: for \((A) \wedge (\neg A \vee B) \wedge (\neg B \vee C)\), the single-literal clause forces \(A = \text{true}\), which forces \(B\), which forces \(C\).

Mechanical and thermal engineering

The Finite Element Method (FEM) splits a structure into small elements, each acting like a spring, and solves \(Ku = f\) for the displacements \(u\). Topology optimization (SIMP) decides where to keep material by giving each cell a density \(\rho \in [0, 1]\) and a stiffness \(E = \rho^p E_0\) with penalty \(p\) (typically 3), which discourages grey, in-between cells. A slider-crank (an engine's piston mechanism) turns rotation into back-and-forth motion. The ideal Otto engine cycle (a thermodynamic cycle simulation) has efficiency \(\eta = 1 - r^{1-\gamma}\). Surrogate-based optimization fits a cheap model to a few expensive simulations and optimizes that instead (Section 11.8).

\[ K u = f \qquad E = \rho^{p}E_0 \qquad x = r\cos\theta + \sqrt{l^2 - r^2\sin^2\theta} \qquad \eta = 1 - r^{1-\gamma} \]
  • \(r\): compression ratio (Otto) or crank radius (slider-crank)
  • \(\gamma\): heat-capacity ratio of the gas, about 1.4 for air
  • \(l\): connecting-rod length
Worked example

FEM: one spring with \(k = 100\) N/m under \(f = 50\) N: \(u = 50/100 = 0.5\) m. SIMP: \(\rho = 0.5\), \(p = 3\): stiffness \(0.125\,E_0\), only 12.5% for half the material, so half-density cells are "expensive" and the optimizer pushes toward 0 or 1. Slider-crank with \(r = 1\), \(l = 3\): piston at \(x = 4\) when \(\theta = 0\) and \(x = \sqrt{8} = 2.83\) when \(\theta = 90^\circ\). Otto with \(r = 8\), \(\gamma = 1.4\): \(\eta = 1 - 8^{-0.4} = 0.565\).

17.7 Geometry, topology, symmetry, security and information

Computational geometry

The cross product of two edge vectors tells you whether three points turn left or right, which is the building block of the convex hull (the rubber band around a point set). A Delaunay triangulation connects points into triangles so that no point lies inside any triangle's circumscribed circle, giving well-shaped triangles.

\[ \text{cross}(a,b,c) = (b_x - a_x)(c_y - a_y) - (b_y - a_y)(c_x - a_x) \]
Worked example

Points \(a = (0,0)\), \(b = (1,0)\), \(c = (1,1)\): \((1)(1) - (0)(1) = 1 > 0\), a left turn (counter-clockwise). A negative value would be a right turn, and 0 means the points lie on one line.

Lie groups, equivariance and topological data analysis

Rotation matrices in 3D form the group SO(3): a matrix \(R\) is a rotation when \(R^TR = I\) and \(\det R = 1\). A network is equivariant if rotating the input rotates the output the same way, \(f(Rx) = R\,f(x)\), which is essential for molecules and robots. Topological data analysis (persistent homology) tracks shapes (connected pieces, loops) as you grow a scale; each feature is "born" and later "dies", and features that live a long time are real, while short-lived ones are noise. Betti numbers count them: a circle has \(b_0 = 1\) piece and \(b_1 = 1\) loop. Gromov-Wasserstein and sliced optimal transport compare shapes using only their internal distances (no shared coordinates) or by averaging cheap 1-D comparisons (Section 15.12) along many random directions.

\[ R^TR = I,\ \det R = 1 \qquad f(Rx) = R\,f(x) \]
Worked example

A 90-degree turn about the z-axis is \(R = \begin{bmatrix}0&-1&0\\1&0&0\\0&0&1\end{bmatrix}\). Applied to \((1, 0, 0)\) it gives \((0, 1, 0)\), a quarter turn.

Cryptography: zero-knowledge proofs, elliptic curves and hash trees

A zero-knowledge proof convinces someone you know a secret without revealing it. In the Schnorr protocol with public \(g\), \(p\) and \(y = g^x \bmod p\): the prover sends a commitment \(t = g^r\), the verifier replies with a random challenge \(c\), the prover answers \(s = r + cx\), and the verifier checks \(g^s = t\,y^c\). An elliptic curve \(y^2 = x^3 + ax + b\) gives a group of points used for compact, strong encryption. A Merkle tree hashes pairs of items upward to a single root, so one item can be proven to belong using only about \(\log_2 n\) hashes.

\[ g^{s} \equiv t\cdot y^{c} \pmod p \qquad y^2 = x^3 + ax + b \]
  • \(x\): the prover's secret
  • \(r\): a one-time random number
  • \(c\): the verifier's challenge
Worked example

Schnorr toy: \(p = 23\), \(g = 5\), secret \(x = 6\), so \(y = 5^6 \bmod 23 = 8\). Prover picks \(r = 3\), sends \(t = 5^3 \bmod 23 = 10\). Challenge \(c = 2\). Response \(s = 3 + 2\cdot6 = 15\). Check: \(5^{15} \bmod 23 = 19\) and \(t\,y^c = 10\cdot64 \bmod 23 = 640 \bmod 23 = 19\). They match, and the verifier never saw 6. Curve: \((3, 6)\) lies on \(y^2 = x^3 + 2x + 3\) because \(36 = 27 + 6 + 3\). Merkle: 8 items need a path of \(\log_2 8 = 3\) hashes.

Algorithmic information theory and compression

The Kolmogorov complexity \(K(x)\) of a string is the length of the shortest program that prints it. It is a measure of randomness: a string like "ab" repeated 50 times is highly compressible (a tiny program prints it), while truly random digits are not. It cannot be computed exactly, but real compressors give upper bounds. Shannon's entropy (Section 4.5) sets the limit for a known random source: no lossless code can use fewer than \(H\) bits per symbol on average. A biased coin with \(H = 0.469\) bits per flip needs at least \(0.469\times1000 = 469\) bits for 1000 flips, instead of the naive 1000.

17.8 Scientific machine learning

Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs)

A PINN trains a network to fit data and to obey a known physical law. The law (a differential equation) is turned into a residual, the amount by which the network's output fails the equation, and that residual is added to the loss. Derivatives come free from automatic differentiation (Section 13.7).

\[ \mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_{\text{data}} + \lambda\,\mathbb{E}\big[\,r(t)^2\,\big],\ \ r(t) = \frac{d\hat{u}}{dt} + \hat{u} \]
  • \(\hat{u}(t)\): the network's prediction
  • \(r(t)\): the residual of the law \(du/dt = -u\) (zero when satisfied)
Worked example

For the law \(du/dt = -u\), suppose at \(t = 1\) the network gives \(\hat{u} = 0.5\) with derivative \(-0.3\). The residual is \(-0.3 + 0.5 = 0.2\), contributing \(0.2^2 = 0.04\) to the loss. Training pushes it to 0, making the network obey the physics even where there is no data.

Fourier Neural Operators and equivariant GNNs

An operator maps a whole function to another function (for instance, today's temperature field to tomorrow's). A Fourier Neural Operator (FNO) layer takes the FFT of the input field (Section 17.4), multiplies the few lowest frequencies by learned weights \(R\), transforms back, and adds a local linear term. Because it learns on frequencies it works at any resolution. Equivariant GNNs (Section 17.7) process molecules while respecting rotation and translation, so predicted forces rotate along with the molecule, which is what makes them effective for molecular dynamics.

\[ v_{\text{out}} = \sigma\Big(W v + \mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(R\cdot\mathcal{F}(v)\big)\Big) \]
  • \(\mathcal{F}, \mathcal{F}^{-1}\): the Fourier transform and its inverse
  • \(R\): learned weights on the low-frequency modes
  • \(W\): a learned local linear map

AlphaFold-style Evoformer, chip-design AI, neural quantum states and theorem proving

The Evoformer in AlphaFold-type models keeps a table of alignments of related protein sequences plus a table of how every pair of residues relates, and updates them with attention along both rows and columns and with triangle updates that enforce geometry such as the triangle inequality \(d_{ik} \le d_{ij} + d_{jk}\) (distances 3, 4 and 5 fit; 3, 4 and 9 would not). AI for chip design treats placement as a game: place one block at a time and reward the agent with the negative of wirelength (HPWL, Section 17.6) plus a congestion penalty, as in reinforcement learning (Part 9). Neural Network Quantum States use a network as the wavefunction \(\psi_\theta\) and train it by Variational Monte Carlo: sample configurations, compute the local energy \(E_{\text{loc}} = H\psi/\psi\), and descend on the average (the variational principle of Section 17.1 guarantees it never goes below the true ground energy). Automated theorem proving with systems like Lean treats a proof as a sequence of steps checked by a verifier; a language model proposes steps, the checker gives an exact reward (1 if the proof is accepted), and search (Section 16) explores alternatives.

\[ E(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{x\sim|\psi_\theta|^2}\big[E_{\text{loc}}(x)\big] \ge E_0 \qquad P(\text{proof}) = \prod_t p(\text{step}_t) \]
Worked example

VMC: three sampled local energies \([-1.1, -0.9, -1.0]\) average to \(E \approx -1.0\). Proof search: three steps with model probabilities \(0.9, 0.8, 0.9\) give a joint probability \(0.9\times0.8\times0.9 = 0.648\) for that proof path, so longer proofs are harder to find by chance, which is why guided search matters.

In your vault

R01 to Z05 in Algorithm_Codex/Reports/Deep_Dives (quantum, physics simulation, bioinformatics, signals and control, numerical linear algebra, chip design and engineering, robotics, geometry, cryptography) and Transformer/Reports/Deep_Dives/45 to 51 (PINNs, FNO, equivariant GNNs, Evoformer and AlphaFold, chip design AI, neural quantum states, Lean 4) cover this part in full.

Part 18. The bigger picture: history, architecture trade-offs, limits, the future, the tools, a debugging playbook, a study plan, a reading list and a map of the vault’s code

Your vault's overview reports (02 to 05, 08, 09 and the CUDA/PyTorch glossary) are mostly descriptive rather than mathematical. This part turns them into plain-English explanations with the few numbers that matter, so you can see how all the earlier maths fits into the real field.

18.1 A short history of neural networks

From one neuron to Transformers

The story is a chain of fixes. The perceptron (late 1950s) was one neuron that learned a straight dividing line. It could not solve problems that no straight line can separate, so people stacked neurons into multi-layer perceptrons and found how to train them with backpropagation (popularized in the 1980s). Fully connected layers were wasteful for pictures, so CNNs shared small filters across an image. Fully connected layers could not handle order, so RNNs added memory, and LSTMs/GRUs added gates to remember over longer spans. Then generative models (VAEs, GANs, diffusion) learned to create data, and in 2017 the Transformer replaced recurrence with attention (Part 8), which could be trained in parallel on enormous text collections. Since then, work has focused on making attention cheaper (Sections 8.15 to 8.18) and on training and using such models better (8.12 to 8.14, 8.21).

\[ w \leftarrow w + \eta\,(y - \hat{y})\,x \qquad b \leftarrow b + \eta\,(y - \hat{y}) \qquad \hat{y} = \begin{cases}1 & w\cdot x + b > 0\\0 & \text{otherwise}\end{cases} \]
  • \(y\): the true label (0 or 1)
  • \(\hat{y}\): the perceptron's output using a hard on/off "step" activation
  • \(\eta\): learning rate
  • \(w, b\): the weights and bias (Section 5.1)
Worked example

Start with \(w = [0, 0]\), \(b = 0\), input \(x = [1, 2]\), true label \(y = 1\), \(\eta = 1\). The score is \(0\cdot1 + 0\cdot2 + 0 = 0\), not above 0, so \(\hat{y} = 0\): wrong. The error is \(y - \hat{y} = 1\), so \(w \leftarrow [0,0] + 1\cdot[1,2] = [1, 2]\) and \(b \leftarrow 1\). Check: \(1\cdot1 + 2\cdot2 + 1 = 6 > 0\), so the perceptron now answers 1 correctly. The rule only changes the weights when it makes a mistake.

Why it matters

Modern training replaces this all-or-nothing step with smooth activations and a smooth loss so that calculus (Sections 3 and 5.5) tells every weight how to improve, not just when a mistake happened.

18.2 Architecture trade-offs at a glance

This table condenses the ideas of 07_All_Neural_Network_Types and 08_Architecture_Pros_and_Cons. "Cost" is how computation grows with input length \(n\).

ArchitectureBest atMain weaknessSection
MLPTabular data, small problems, the "building block"Ignores structure (order, neighbourhood); many parameters5
CNNImages and grids (shared filters)Sees only local patches unless deep; fixed-size input7.1
RNNShort sequences, streamingSlow (sequential), vanishing/exploding gradients7.2
LSTM / GRULonger sequences than RNNStill sequential; limited memory span7.3
VAESmooth latent space, compressionBlurry samples7.4
GANSharp images, fast samplingUnstable training, mode collapse7.5
DiffusionHigh-quality, diverse imagesMany sampling steps (slow)7.6
GNNGraphs: molecules, social networksOver-smoothing with many layers7.7
Dense TransformerLanguage and almost everything; parallel trainingCost \(\mathcal{O}(n^2)\); huge KV cache8
Mixture of ExpertsHuge capacity at lower per-token computeAll parameters must still fit in memory; routing complexity8.8
SSM / MambaVery long sequences, cost \(\mathcal{O}(n)\)Weaker exact recall of far-back details8.15
