18.3 How today's large models are trained, end to end

Your report 03_Transformers_and_SOTA_Models describes the main frontier and open-source model families of its time (it names several commercial and open-weight families). Model names change every few months, so use them only as examples. What stays constant is the recipe, and you now know every maths piece in it:

  1. Tokenize text (Section 8.11) and look up embeddings plus positions (8.1).
  2. Pretrain on huge text by predicting the next token with cross-entropy (8.6), using Adam (5.7) with warmup and cosine decay (5.15), normalization (5.8, 5.14), distributed across many GPUs (8.19). Size is guided by scaling laws (8.12).
  3. Supervised fine-tuning on example conversations (8.14), often with LoRA (8.9) and quantization (8.13).
  4. Preference tuning with a reward model and PPO (Part 9) or with DPO (8.14).
  5. Serve it efficiently with a KV cache, FlashAttention, speculative decoding and quantization (8.17 to 8.18), optionally with retrieval (8.22) and test-time compute (8.21).

18.4 The limits of Transformers, and the fixes

The quadratic bottleneck, the memory wall and reasoning limits

Your report 04_Limitations_and_Improvements names three core problems. (1) Quadratic attention: every token attends to every other, so the score matrix has \(n^2\) entries per head per layer. (2) The KV-cache memory wall: the cache (Section 8.18) grows linearly with context and with every simultaneous user, so GPUs run out of memory before they run out of compute. (3) Reasoning versus memorization: a model that predicts the next token spends the same effort on every token, so it cannot "think harder" without writing more tokens, and because it predicts statistically likely text it can state falsehoods confidently (hallucination).

\[ \text{score entries} = n^2 \qquad \text{GQA cache} = \frac{h_{kv}}{h_q}\times\text{MHA cache} \qquad \text{Ring attention per-GPU tokens} = \frac{n}{G} \]
  • \(h_q, h_{kv}\): number of query heads and of shared key/value heads
  • \(G\): number of GPUs in the ring
Worked example

Quadratic: a 100,000-token context gives \(10^{10}\) (ten billion) entries per head per layer. GQA (Grouped-Query Attention, one key/value pair shared by several queries): 32 query heads sharing 8 key/value heads gives a cache \(8/32 = \tfrac{1}{4}\) the size, so the 2 GiB example in Section 8.18 becomes 0.5 GiB. Ring attention splits the context around a ring of GPUs: 1,000,000 tokens on \(G = 8\) GPUs leaves 125,000 tokens per GPU. MoE (Section 8.8): a 100-billion-parameter model with only 10 billion active per token uses just 10% of its parameters in compute for each token.

Why it matters

The leading remedies, all covered earlier: sub-quadratic models (Mamba and linear attention, 8.15 and 8.16), cheaper memory (GQA, MLA, 8.18), and more thinking at answer time (chain-of-thought and Tree of Thoughts, which generates several possible next steps, scores them and searches like a game tree, plus best-of-N, 8.21). Hybrids such as Jamba interleave Mamba layers (cheap long context) with a few attention layers (precise recall).

18.5 The future: systems around the model

RAG, knowledge graphs and GraphRAG

A model's knowledge is frozen at the end of training, and it may hallucinate. RAG (Section 8.22) fetches relevant text at question time. But vector search finds similar text, not multi-step relationships. A knowledge graph stores facts as triples (subject, relation, object), and a question becomes a walk along edges. GraphRAG combines both. A language model can extract triples from text and also write the graph query, giving a neuro-symbolic system: flexible language from the network, exact verifiable facts from the graph.

Worked example

Triples: (John, founded, StartupX), (CompanyA, acquired, StartupX), (CompanyA, CEO, Mary). Question: "Who is the CEO of the company that acquired the startup John founded?" Walk: John to StartupX (founded), StartupX to CompanyA (acquired by), CompanyA to Mary (CEO). Answer: Mary, in 3 exact hops. Plain vector search would likely retrieve three separate passages and leave the model to guess the chain.

Biological and quantum computing, and liquid networks

Organoid intelligence is the experimental idea of using small lab-grown clusters of brain cells, wired to electrodes, as computing hardware. The motivation is energy: the human brain runs on about 20 watts, while a large GPU cluster draws hundreds of kilowatts. This is highly experimental. Quantum machine learning (Section 17.1) hopes quantum computers can speed up parts of training, but today's noisy hardware limits it. Liquid neural networks (Section 15.4) are tiny networks whose dynamics keep adapting to the input, aimed at robots and vehicles.

Worked example

Energy illustration: 1000 GPUs at 700 W each draw \(700{,}000\) W, which is \(700{,}000/20 = 35{,}000\) times the brain's 20 W (illustrative numbers, not a claim about any specific system).

Everything in this guide is implemented by a handful of libraries. You do not need to memorize their commands, but you should know which tool does which job (this follows 09_Complete_ML_DL_AI_Libraries_Master_Guide and the PyTorch/CUDA glossary 40).

ToolWhat it is forMaths it hides

The four-step training loop in real code

A tensor is just an array of numbers (a scalar, vector, matrix, or higher-dimensional grid) that can live on a GPU and remember the operations applied to it so gradients can be computed. The training loop you did by hand in Section 5.5 is the same four lines in PyTorch:

import torch, torch.nn as nn

model = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(1, 1), nn.ReLU(), nn.Linear(1, 1))
opt = torch.optim.SGD(model.parameters(), lr=0.1)
x = torch.tensor([[2.0]])
y = torch.tensor([[1.0]])

for step in range(100):
    y_hat = model(x)                    # 1. forward pass      (5.3)
    loss = 0.5 * ((y_hat - y) ** 2).mean()   # 2. loss          (5.4)
    opt.zero_grad()                     # clear old gradients
    loss.backward()                     # 3. backpropagation   (5.5, 5.9)
    opt.step()                          # 4. gradient descent  (3.2)

zero_grad() is needed because PyTorch adds new gradients onto old ones. Mixed precision does most arithmetic in 16-bit numbers (half the memory of 32-bit: a 7-billion-parameter model is 28 GB in 32-bit but 14 GB in 16-bit) while keeping a 32-bit master copy for accuracy (Section 8.19). In scikit-learn the same ideas look like model.fit(X_train, y_train), model.predict(X_test), cross_val_score(...) for the train/validation idea from the glossary, and a Pipeline that chains preprocessing and a model so there is no data leakage from the test set.

Which tool should a beginner pick? Start with pandas plus scikit-learn for tabular data and classical ML. Use PyTorch when you need neural networks. Use Hugging Face when you want to start from a pretrained model rather than training from scratch.

In your vault

Reports/02 to 05, 07, 08, 09 and Deep_Dives/40, 43, 44 (frameworks encyclopedia and libraries guide) give the full descriptive coverage of this part.

18.7 When training goes wrong: a debugging playbook

Your LLM_DEBUGGING_AND_TROUBLESHOOTING.md is a "symptom, cause, fix" guide. Here it is in beginner form, with the maths that tells you why each fix works.

Three sanity checks to run before you trust any training run

1) Is the starting loss right? A model that knows nothing should assign every token roughly equal probability, so its starting cross-entropy loss is about \(\ln V\) for a vocabulary of \(V\) tokens. If your loss starts far from that, the labels, the shapes or the loss function are probably wrong. 2) Can it overfit one tiny batch? A healthy model and optimizer can drive the loss on 8 or so examples to nearly 0. If it cannot, there is a bug (not a data problem). 3) Is the learning rate sensible? Try a few values spaced by factors of 3 or 10; too high gives noisy or exploding loss (Section 3.2), too low gives a flat line.

\[ \mathcal{L}_{\text{init}} \approx \ln V \]
Worked example

For \(V = 50{,}000\) the expected starting loss is \(\ln 50{,}000 = 10.82\). A starting loss of 25 means something is broken; a starting loss of 0.5 on the first step means the labels are leaking into the input.

SymptomLikely causeFix (and why it works)
CUDA out of memory at step 0Model or batch too big for the GPUSmaller batch with gradient accumulation (Section 8.26); gradient checkpointing (about 60% less activation memory for about 20% more compute, per your playbook); mixed precision (bf16 halves memory per number); quantize or LoRA the model (8.13, 8.9). Remember training with Adam needs about 16 bytes per parameter (Section 8.19).
Memory grows every step, then OOMA memory leak: storing the loss tensor keeps its whole computation graph aliveStore plain numbers: losses.append(loss.item()) or loss.detach(), never losses.append(loss).
Loss becomes nan or infExploding gradients; log(0); fp16 overflow (max 65,504); learning rate too highClip the gradient norm (Section 5.15); use log(x + 1e-8) (since \(\ln 0 = -\infty\)); switch to bf16; add learning-rate warmup (5.15).
Loss does not go down at allLearning rate far too small or large; wrong labels; frozen parameters; forgot optimizer.zero_grad() or loss.backward()Run the three sanity checks above and the four-step loop of Section 18.6 line by line.
Training loss falls but validation loss risesOverfitting (Section 6.9)More data, dropout (5.13), weight decay (5.12), early stopping, a smaller model.
mat1 and mat2 shapes cannot be multipliedThe inner dimensions do not matchFor \((A\times B)\cdot(B\times C)\) the two \(B\)s must be equal (Section 2.2.1). After permute or transpose call .contiguous() before .view() (Section 18.8).
LoRA run ends identical to the base model, or outputs gibberishWrong initialization or targetsMatrix \(A\) random and \(B\) zero (so \(\Delta W = BA = 0\) at step 0); apply LoRA to the attention projections (q, k, v, o); check the scale \(\alpha/r\) (e.g. \(16/8 = 2\)).
The model repeats the same sentence foreverGreedy decoding; missing end-of-sequence tokenRepetition penalty about 1.2; temperature about 0.7 with top-p about 0.9 (Section 8.24); make sure the EOS token id is passed to the generation loop.

Gradient accumulation in code

optimizer.zero_grad()
for micro_step in range(accumulation_steps):      # e.g. 8 micro-batches of 8 = effective batch 64
    outputs = model(inputs_micro)
    loss = loss_fn(outputs, targets) / accumulation_steps   # divide so the sum is the average
    loss.backward()                                          # gradients add up in .grad
optimizer.step()                                             # one update for the whole group

18.8 PyTorch shapes, einsum and the keyword glossary

Tensor shapes: the cause of most bugs

Almost every deep-learning bug is a shape mismatch. Learn the standard shapes of a Transformer (Section 8) and check them first. einsum ("Einstein summation") writes a tensor operation by naming each axis with a letter: axes that appear on both inputs but not in the output are summed over, exactly like the inner dimension in matrix multiplication.

\[ (B,N,D)\times(D,K)\to(B,N,K) \qquad \text{head\_dim} = \frac{\text{embed\_dim}}{\text{num\_heads}} \]
  • \(B\): batch size
  • \(N\): sequence length (number of tokens)
  • \(D\): embedding size
  • \(K\): output size
Worked example

Query projection: a batch \(X\) of shape \((2, 4, 8)\) (2 sentences, 4 tokens, embedding 8) times a weight \(W_Q\) of shape \((8, 16)\) gives \(Q\) of shape \((2, 4, 16)\). In code: Q = torch.einsum("b n d, d k -> b n k", X, W_Q); the axis d appears in both inputs but not the output, so it is summed. Heads: embedding 512 split over 8 heads gives head_dim \(64\). Scores for 2 sentences, 8 heads and 4 tokens have shape \((2, 8, 4, 4)\) (batch, heads, query position, key position). Why divide by \(\sqrt{d_k}\): if the query and key entries each have variance 1, the dot product has variance \(d_k\), i.e. standard deviation \(\sqrt{128} = 11.3\) for \(d_k = 128\), which pushes the softmax into its flat region (Section 8.3).

TensorShape
Input token ids[batch, seq_len]
Embeddings[batch, seq_len, embed_size]
Q, K, V per head[batch, seq_len, heads, head_dim] (or [batch, heads, seq_len, head_dim])
Attention scores \(QK^T\)[batch, heads, seq_len, seq_len]
Attention output[batch, seq_len, embed_size]
Final logits[batch, seq_len, vocab_size]

The keywords you will meet in code

A short translation table (condensed from your Python/PyTorch/CUDA glossary). nn.Module is the base class of every layer; call model(x), never model.forward(x), so hooks run. nn.Parameter marks a tensor as trainable. requires_grad=False freezes a tensor (this is how LoRA keeps the base model fixed). with torch.no_grad() turns off gradient tracking for inference to save memory. .view() needs contiguous memory while .reshape() copies if needed; after permute/transpose, call .contiguous() first. torch.gather/scatter_/topk pick values by index (used in MoE routing, Section 8.8). masked_fill applies the causal mask (so a token cannot see the future). torch.clamp caps values (PPO clipping, Section 9.4). torch.compile speeds code up through three stages: TorchDynamo captures the graph, AOTAutograd captures forward and backward, and TorchInductor fuses operations into fast GPU kernels. In Triton, tl.load and tl.store move tiles between slow and fast memory and tl.dot multiplies them (Section 8.30). Python dunder methods: __init__ builds a layer, __call__ lets model(x) work, and __getitem__/__len__ make a dataset.

18.9 A study roadmap: where to start, and in what order

The vault’s build-priority guide, in plain terms

Your Transformer cheat sheet separates what you must know to build a working Transformer from what is optional. Tier 1 (required): token and position embeddings; Q, K, V projections; scaled dot-product attention (Section 8.3); multi-head reshaping (8.4); the causal mask; residual connection plus LayerNorm (5.8, 8.5); the feed-forward block; a stack of blocks with an output head and cross-entropy loss (8.6); backprop with an optimizer step (5.5, 5.7); and the shape table above. Tier 2 (modern practice): RoPE (8.10), SwiGLU (5.11), AdamW with gradient clipping and mixed precision (5.15, 5.16, 8.26), torch.compile, and the KV cache (8.18). Tier 3 (only with a reason): MoE (8.8), FlashAttention and Triton (8.17, 8.30), LoRA and quantization (8.9, 8.13), and the other architecture families (Parts 7 and 15). About two thirds of the cheat sheet is this survey layer.

18.10 Research directions: where the field is heading

Efficient Transformers now, better ones next, and "beyond silicon"

Your FUTURE_RESEARCH_MAP splits the future into three layers. Efficient Transformers today: FlashAttention (Section 8.17), Ring Attention (split a million-token sequence over GPUs that pass keys and values around a ring, Section 18.4), quantization to FP8 and Int4 (8.13), and GQA (8.25). A better Transformer: continual learning so models are not frozen after training (14.6, 8.16), hybrid architectures mixing cheap linear-time layers with a few attention layers (8.15), and adaptive compute that spends more effort on hard questions (8.21). Beyond silicon: organoid intelligence, neuromorphic chips that run spiking networks (15.5) and quantum machine learning (17.1, 18.5). The report notes the human brain uses about 20 watts, which motivates the energy-efficiency push.

Your non-agentic-ai-research-directions report asks where else these architectures are used besides chat. Its quick-reference table, in plain words: protein structure and design (Evoformer attention plus diffusion, Section 17.8); genomics (state-space/convolution hybrids such as StripedHyena, 8.15); materials discovery and weather/climate (graph networks plus diffusion, 7.7, 7.6); chemical reactions (sequence Transformers over molecule strings); physics simulation (Fourier Neural Operators, 17.8); particle physics (set Transformers and GNNs on point clouds); astronomy (multimodal masked Transformers); brain decoding (contrastive alignment plus a diffusion decoder, 14.1); world models (JEPA, 15.11); time-series forecasting (decoder Transformers, Part 12); remote sensing (Vision Transformers with masked autoencoding, 8.23); and symbolic regression (KANs, 15.10).

ArchitectureTraining costInference memoryExact recall of far-back details

Mamba-2 (state-space)\(\mathcal{O}(N d)\)\(\mathcal{O}(1)\) stateModerate

18.12 Using the vault’s code and simulations, and the bridge between classical and modern

How to run what is in the zip

The zip contains two kinds of runnable material. Code: the Algorithm Codex has 175 Python files that need only NumPy; running python Code/verify_all.py from its folder tests all 174 algorithms end to end (the README reports about a minute). The Transformer folder has about 48 files that need PyTorch; run one with, for example, python Code/Deep_Dives/13_Classic_Transformer_Dense.py, watch its __main__ block run on dummy data, then do the vault’s own challenge: change batch_size, embed_dim or seq_length. If it still runs without a shape error, you understood the matrix dimensions (Section 18.8). Simulations: open Sims/index.html in a browser. The Algorithm Codex has 34 interactive pages and the Transformer folder 58, one per deep dive. The table maps them to this guide.

Classical algorithm, modern analogue

The vault’s CROSS_REFERENCE_WITH_TRANSFORMER_REPO links each classical algorithm to the modern idea it prefigures. The most useful links for a beginner: MLE under bell-curve noise gives the squared-error loss, and under Bernoulli or categorical noise gives cross-entropy (Section 4.8). Logistic regression with many classes is the softmax of attention and of every classifier head. k-NN and KD-trees become HNSW and vector databases. Naive Bayes and frequency statistics underlie BPE tokenization. Decision trees partition space with axis-aligned steps, while KANs learn curves on the edges. The SVM kernel trick relates to attention as a similarity function. PCA via SVD is the ancestor of LoRA’s low-rank update (Section 2.3). ICA corresponds to sparse autoencoders (8.20). k-means powers product quantization inside vector databases. GMM with EM parallels the VAE’s encoder-decoder loop. Bagging corresponds to self-consistency and best-of-\(N\) sampling (8.21). Gradient boosting fits residuals just as residual connections add to a stream. HMMs are discrete ancestors of state-space models such as Mamba. MCMC and Gibbs sampling are the ancestors of score-based diffusion (15.13). Bayesian optimization tunes learning rates and batch sizes (11.8). Exponential smoothing is the recency-weighted memory in RWKV. TD learning is the critic update inside PPO. MCTS underlies search over reasoning steps with process reward models. Calibration and temperature scaling are the same \(z/T\) trick as sampling temperature (10.10, 8.7).

Four standalone HTML files in the zip complement this guide: math_mastery_transformer.html is a nine-part maths course from "the language of numbers" to alignment, hardware and interpretability; report (1).html, titled "Inside the Machine", walks from one neuron to a full Transformer block and ends with a "model atlas" of real open-weight models; AI_Architecture_Research_Breakdown.html is a gap analysis of the vault itself, plus the complexity table above and a tiered reading syllabus; and neural_codex (1).html is a large interactive page titled "NEURAL CODEX" (I did not go through that one in detail).

In your vault

Everything in Sections 18.7 to 18.12 is based on LLM_DEBUGGING_AND_TROUBLESHOOTING.md, FUTURE_RESEARCH_MAP.md, non-agentic-ai-research-directions.md, both STUDY_ROADMAP.md files, both paper/textbook catalogs, CROSS_REFERENCE_WITH_TRANSFORMER_REPO.md, both READMEs and the two cheat sheets.

Part 20. Check yourself

Try answering before revealing each answer. If you can work through all of these without peeking, you've genuinely absorbed the core ideas.

1. Why does a neural network need an activation function at all?

Because stacking purely linear layers (matrix multiplications with no bending function) collapses into one single linear transformation — no matter how many layers you stack, the network could never represent a curve, only a straight line/plane. See Section 5.2.

2. A neuron has inputs [1, 4], weights [2, -0.5], bias 0.5. What is z? What is a with ReLU?

\(z = (2)(1)+(-0.5)(4)+0.5 = 2-2+0.5=0.5\). ReLU: \(a=\max(0,0.5)=0.5\). See Section 5.1.

3. Why is cross-entropy loss almost always paired with softmax, rather than MSE?

Cross-entropy is built directly from the probability/information-theory idea of "extra bits wasted using the wrong distribution" (KL divergence, Section 4.6), and its combination with softmax/sigmoid gives a clean gradient (\(\hat y-y\)) that doesn't vanish the way MSE's gradient can when paired with a saturating activation. See Sections 4.6, 5.4.2, 6.2.

4. In gradient descent, what does the learning rate α actually control, and what happens if it's too large?

It controls the size of each step taken opposite the gradient. Too large, and the steps overshoot the minimum and can bounce around or diverge instead of settling down; too small, and training crawls unnecessarily slowly. See Section 3.2.

5. What does the "K^T" (Key transpose) in the attention formula accomplish, shape-wise?

Q has shape [seq_len, d_k] and K also has shape [seq_len, d_k]; transposing K to [d_k, seq_len] makes the inner dimensions match for matrix multiplication (Section 2.2.1), producing a [seq_len, seq_len] matrix of every word's relevance to every other word. See Section 8.3.

6. Why is Q·K scaled by 1/√d_k before the softmax?

Because raw dot products grow large as the embedding dimension grows, which pushes softmax into a nearly-flat region where gradients vanish (Section 5.2.1's sigmoid problem, again). Dividing by √d_k keeps the variance of the scores roughly constant regardless of dimension. See Section 8.3.

7. What's the difference between bias and variance in the bias-variance tradeoff?

Bias is error from a model too simple to capture the real pattern (underfitting). Variance is error from a model so flexible it also fits the noise in one particular training sample (overfitting) — it would give very different answers on a different training sample. See Section 6.9.

8. Why does LoRA use two small matrices (B and A) instead of one matrix the same size as W?

A full weight update matrix ΔW the same shape as W (e.g. 4096×4096) has millions of trainable numbers. Factoring it as B (4096×8) times A (8×4096) — a low "rank" — needs only a tiny fraction as many trainable numbers while still producing a full-shaped correction when multiplied out, since W itself stays frozen. See Section 8.9.

9. In Q-learning, what does the term "r + γ·max Q(s',a') − Q(s,a)" represent, in plain English?

It's the gap ("TD error") between what you currently believe an action is worth (Q(s,a)) and a better, updated estimate — the reward you actually got, plus the discounted value of the best thing you could do next. The update nudges your belief toward that better estimate. See Section 9.2.

10. Why is entropy for a fair coin exactly 1 bit, but entropy for a 90%-heads coin is lower?

Entropy measures average surprise/unpredictability. A fair coin's outcome is maximally hard to guess (1 bit of genuine uncertainty). A 90%-heads coin is mostly predictable — you can usually correctly guess "heads" — so there's less surprise on average, giving lower entropy. See Section 4.5.

More questions (the extension parts)

11. Why do we initialize ReLU layers with variance 2/n_in instead of 1/n_in?

ReLU sets about half of its inputs to zero, which halves the variance of the signal passed on. Doubling the weights' variance to \(2/n_{\text{in}}\) compensates, so activations keep a steady size from layer to layer. See Section 5.10.

12. What is the difference between BatchNorm and LayerNorm?

Both subtract a mean and divide by a spread. BatchNorm computes them for each feature across the examples in the batch; LayerNorm computes them for each example across its own features, so it does not depend on other examples, which is why Transformers use it. See Section 5.14.

13. What does byte-pair encoding do, and why is it better than whole words or single letters?

It starts from single characters and repeatedly merges the most frequent neighbouring pair into a new token. This gives a compact vocabulary that still handles any new word by splitting it into known pieces, with sequences much shorter than letter-by-letter. See Section 8.11.

14. Is FlashAttention an approximation of attention?

No. It computes exactly the same result as ordinary attention, but processes scores in tiles and keeps a running maximum and sum (online softmax) so the huge \(n\times n\) matrix is never stored in slow memory. See Section 8.17.

15. In DPO, what is the reference model for?

It is a frozen copy of the starting model. DPO measures how much more likely the trained model finds the chosen answer than the reference does (and likewise for the rejected one), which keeps the model from drifting arbitrarily far while it learns preferences. See Section 8.14.

16. How does a Gaussian Mixture Model differ from k-means?

k-means assigns each point to exactly one cluster. A GMM gives every point a probability (responsibility) of belonging to each bell-curve cluster, and fits means, spreads and weights with the EM algorithm. See Sections 6.7 and 10.3.

17. Why is mutual defection the Nash equilibrium of the Prisoner's Dilemma?

Whatever the other player does, defecting gives you a higher payoff (5 vs 3 if they cooperate, 1 vs 0 if they defect), so neither player can gain by changing only their own choice from (defect, defect), even though both would prefer (cooperate, cooperate). See Section 16.8.

18. Why do state-space models like Mamba scale linearly with sequence length?

They carry a fixed-size memory state updated once per token, instead of comparing every token with every earlier token. Cost per token is constant, so the total grows as \(\mathcal{O}(n)\) rather than \(\mathcal{O}(n^2)\). See Section 8.15.

19. A test gives p = 0.03. Does that mean the null hypothesis is 3% likely to be true?

No. It means that if the null hypothesis were true, data at least this extreme would occur about 3% of the time. It says nothing directly about the probability that the null is true. See Section 4.7.

20. Two features have Shapley values 15 and 25. What must the model's full output (above the empty baseline) be?

40, because Shapley values always add up to the difference between the full prediction and the baseline prediction (here \(v(\{1,2\}) - v(\varnothing) = 40\)). See Section 14.10.

Even more questions (gap-fill parts)

21. A model with vocabulary 50,000 starts training with loss 25. Is that normal?

No. A model that knows nothing should start near \(\ln 50{,}000 = 10.82\). A much higher value signals a bug such as wrong labels or a wrongly scaled loss. See Section 18.7.

22. Why is MLE of a coin's bias with 7 heads in 10 flips equal to 0.7, and how does a Beta(2,2) prior change it?

Maximizing \(7\ln p+3\ln(1-p)\) gives \(p=7/10\). A Beta(2,2) prior adds two imaginary heads and two imaginary tails, giving a posterior mean \(9/14=0.643\) and MAP \(8/12=0.667\), pulled toward 0.5. See Sections 4.8 and 4.9.

23. Why does bagging reduce variance only down to \(\rho\sigma^2\)?

Averaging cancels the independent part of the models' errors, but any error they share (correlation \(\rho\)) survives the average. Random forests lower \(\rho\) by letting each split see only a random subset of features. See Section 6.15.

24. What does an AUC of 0.75 mean in plain words?

If you pick one random positive and one random negative example, the model gives the positive a higher score 75% of the time. See Section 6.13.

25. What is the difference between SARSA and Q-learning?

Q-learning updates toward the best next action's value (off-policy); SARSA updates toward the value of the action it actually takes next (on-policy), so it accounts for its own exploration. See Section 9.14.

26. Why do skip connections help very deep networks?

They make each block's derivative \(1+F'(x)\) instead of \(F'(x)\), so the gradient never has to pass through a long product of numbers below 1 and does not vanish. See Section 5.17.

27. Why is a "sudden" emergent ability sometimes just a measurement effect?

If a task needs \(m\) steps all correct, the all-or-nothing score is \(p^m\). A smoothly rising per-step accuracy \(p\) makes \(p^m\) stay near zero and then jump. See Section 8.28.

28. When is an operation memory-bound, and why does FlashAttention help?

When its arithmetic intensity (FLOPs per byte moved) is below the GPU's ridge point. Standard attention writes and re-reads a huge matrix; FlashAttention keeps tiles in fast memory so it moves far fewer bytes per FLOP. See Sections 8.17 and 8.30.

29. A 10-step agent is 95% reliable per step. How often does the whole chain succeed?

\(0.95^{10}=59.9\%\). Errors compound, which is why verification and self-correction steps matter. See Section 8.33.

30. You get mat1 and mat2 shapes cannot be multiplied (64x128 and 256x512). What is wrong?

For \((A\times B)\cdot(B\times C)\) the inner dimensions must be equal, but 128 does not equal 256. Fix the layer's input size or reshape the tensor. See Sections 2.2.1 and 18.8.

Part 21. Beginner glossary: the everyday words

These words appear everywhere in AI writing, and books often assume you already know them.

WordPlain meaning
AI, ML, DLArtificial Intelligence is the big goal (machines doing smart things). Machine Learning is the part of AI where the machine learns rules from data instead of being programmed with them. Deep Learning is the part of ML that uses neural networks with many layers.
ModelThe function (Section 1.1) with adjustable numbers inside that we train.
ParametersThe adjustable numbers inside the model: all the weights and biases (together called \(\theta\)). GPT-style models have billions.
HyperparametersSettings you choose before training that are not learned: learning rate \(\alpha\), number of layers, batch size.
FeaturesThe input numbers describing one example (house size, number of rooms...).
Label / targetThe correct answer \(y\) for an example.
Supervised learningLearning from examples that come with correct labels (regression, classification).
Unsupervised learningFinding structure in data with no labels (k-means, PCA).
Reinforcement learningLearning by trial and error from rewards (Part 9).
Regression vs classificationRegression predicts a number (a price). Classification predicts a category (cat or dog).
Training / validation / test setData split in three: the model learns from the training set, you tune settings on the validation set, and you finally grade it once on the test set it has never seen.
EpochOne full pass through the whole training set.
Batch / mini-batchA small group of examples processed together in one update step (Section 5.6).
Iteration / stepOne weight update (one round of forward, loss, backward, update).
LogitsThe raw, un-normalized scores a network outputs before softmax turns them into probabilities.
OverfittingThe model memorizes the training data (including its noise) and does badly on new data. High variance (Section 6.9).
UnderfittingThe model is too simple to learn even the training data. High bias.
RegularizationAny trick that discourages overfitting (weight penalties, dropout, early stopping).
DropoutRandomly switching off some neurons during training. With drop probability 0.2, the kept values are scaled by \(1/(1-0.2)=1.25\) so the average stays the same.
Vanishing / exploding gradientWhen the chain rule multiplies many numbers below 1 (they shrink to nothing) or above 1 (they blow up) across deep layers.
TokenA chunk of text (a word or piece of a word) that a language model reads as one unit.
EmbeddingA learned vector of numbers standing for a word, image patch, etc. (Section 8.1).
InferenceUsing a trained model to make predictions (as opposed to training it).
Fine-tuningContinuing to train an already-trained model on a smaller, specific dataset (LoRA, Section 8.9, is a cheap way).

More words (the extension parts)

WordPlain meaning
PerceptronThe original single artificial neuron with a hard on/off output (Section 18.1).
Prior, likelihood, posteriorPrior: what you believed before seeing data. Likelihood: how well the data fits a hypothesis. Posterior: your updated belief (Bayes' theorem, 4.4).
Latent variableA hidden quantity that is never observed directly but explains the data (a VAE's code, a topic, an HMM state).
ManifoldA curved surface (like a rolled-up sheet) that data lies on inside a higher-dimensional space (10.6).
StationaryA time series whose average and spread do not drift over time (12.2).
CensoringKnowing only that an event had not happened yet when observation stopped (12.4).
ConfounderA hidden factor that affects both a cause and an effect, creating a false link (14.7).
HeuristicA rule-of-thumb estimate used to guide search (A*, 16.2).
Pareto frontThe set of solutions where improving one goal means worsening another (16.10).
Surrogate modelA cheap model that imitates an expensive simulation or experiment (11.8, 17.6).
SparseMostly zeros. Sparse penalties and features keep only a few things active (5.12, 8.20).
QuantizationStoring numbers with fewer bits to save memory and time (8.13).
Context windowThe maximum number of tokens a model can read at once.
KV cacheStored keys and values of earlier tokens, so generation does not recompute them (8.18).
PromptThe text you give a language model to continue.
HallucinationA fluent but false statement produced because the model predicts likely text, not verified fact (18.4).
RAGRetrieval-Augmented Generation: fetch relevant documents and paste them into the prompt (8.22).
AgentA model in a loop that can call tools and observe results (8.23).
Mixture of Experts (MoE)A model with many sub-networks where only a few run for each token (8.8).
PretrainingThe first, very large training stage on raw data (18.3).
FLOP vs FLOPs vs FLOP/sOne floating-point operation; many of them (total work); and operations per second (speed).
Latency vs throughputLatency is the time for one answer; throughput is how many answers per second overall.
BenchmarkA fixed test used to compare models (pass@k, accuracy, perplexity).
AblationRemoving one component to see how much it mattered.
TensorAn array of numbers of any dimension; the basic data type of deep learning frameworks (18.6).
Mixed precisionDoing most arithmetic in 16-bit numbers while keeping some 32-bit copies for accuracy (18.6).
CheckpointA saved snapshot of a model's weights during or after training.

More words (gap-fill parts)

WordPlain meaning
MLE / MAPPick the parameters that make the data most likely / most probable after also using a prior (4.8, 4.9).
Conjugate priorA prior whose posterior has the same shape, so updating is simple arithmetic (4.9).
Stationary distributionThe long-run fraction of time a Markov chain spends in each state (4.10).
SVDWriting any matrix as rotate, stretch, rotate; the base of PCA and LoRA (2.3).
Nesterov, AdaGrad, RMSProp, AdamWVariants of gradient descent with look-ahead, per-parameter step sizes, or decoupled weight decay (5.16).
One-hot encodingRepresenting a category as a vector with a single 1 (6.12).
Data leakageTest-set information sneaking into training, making scores look too good (6.12).
ROC / AUCCurve of true-positive against false-positive rate over all thresholds, and the area under it (6.13).
Cross-validation / bootstrap / OOBWays to estimate performance by re-splitting or resampling the data (6.14, 6.15).
Kernel trickGetting the dot product in a huge feature space without building that space (6.16).
Top-k / top-p / beam searchDecoding rules for choosing the next token (8.24).
ALiBi, YaRN, GQA, MQA, sliding windowTricks for long context and a smaller KV cache (8.25).
Gradient accumulation / checkpointingWays to train big models in little memory (8.26).
MinHash / LSHCheap ways to find near-duplicate documents (8.27).
Elo / contamination / emergenceA rating system; test data leaking into training; abilities that seem to appear suddenly (8.28).
PEFT, DoRA, adapters, prefix tuningFine-tuning methods that train only a small number of parameters (8.29).
KTO, ORPO, SimPOPreference-tuning methods that avoid RLHF's extra models (8.29).
NF4, GPTQ, AWQ, GGUFQuantization formats (8.29).
Roofline / arithmetic intensityThe model that says whether a computation is limited by math or by memory (8.30).
TritonA Python-like language for writing GPU kernels (8.30).
PagedAttention / continuous batching / TTFTServing techniques; and time-to-first-token (8.31).
BM25 / HyDE / rerankerKeyword scoring; searching with a hypothetical answer; a slow, accurate second-stage ranker (8.34).
GRPOReinforcement learning that scores each sampled answer relative to its group (8.33).
SARSA / eligibility traceOn-policy value learning; decaying credit to recently visited states (9.14).
einsum / contiguous / viewAxis-named tensor math; memory layout; reshaping without copying (18.8).
